### [76] [Quantifying Event Impacts on Time Series via Multiscale Contrastive Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19447)
*Yiming Sun,Shengyu Chen,Zhengzhang Chen,Haoyu Wang,Xiaowei Jia,Haifeng Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EventTime is a multi-resolution, event-conditioned forecasting framework for short-term post-disclosure abnormal losses, integrating long-horizon market context, short-horizon pre-event dynamics, and event metadata with a dynamic contrastive objective; evaluated on the SECURE dataset with superior performance and interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: External shocks like cybersecurity breach disclosures induce abrupt, heterogeneous market movements that endogenous time-series models struggle to predict, especially with sparse, high-impact events and limited metadata.

Method: A multi-resolution architecture that fuses temporal representations with event attributes via an event fusion module; a dynamic contrastive objective that builds event- and time-series-aware positive/negative pairs to mitigate sparse supervision; construction of the SECURE real-world dataset aligning cybersecurity incidents with stock-time series and semantic features (structured and LLM-derived).

Result: Experimentally, EventTime outperforms state-of-the-art time-series and event-aware baselines in predicting post-event abnormal losses; the learned representations are more event-sensitive, robust to incomplete metadata, and yield more interpretable short-term impact estimates.

Conclusion: EventTime offers accurate, robust, and interpretable predictions for short-term market impact following cybersecurity disclosures, and the SECURE dataset supports broader evaluation and extension to other external shocks.

Abstract: Shocks that spread through the web, such as cybersecurity breach disclosures, can abruptly disrupt financial time series and cause substantial abnormal losses. While these events are disclosed as discrete records through news reports, regulatory filings, or public databases, their consequences unfold through continuous market dynamics. This creates an event-conditioned impact prediction problem: given pre-event market history and limited event metadata, the goal is to estimate short-term post-disclosure abnormal loss rather than reconstruct the full post-event trajectory. However, most time-series forecasting models focus on endogenous regularities such as trend, seasonality, and autocorrelation, and thus struggle with rare and heterogeneous external events. The challenge is further amplified by sparse high-impact events and background market noise. We introduce EventTime, a multi-resolution framework that combines long-horizon market context, short-horizon pre-event dynamics, and event metadata. It incorporates an event fusion module that couples temporal representations with event attributes to identify relevant recent market patterns. To mitigate sparse supervision, EventTime further introduces a dynamic contrastive objective that constructs event- and time-series-aware positive and negative pairs during training. We also construct SECURE, a real-world dataset aligning cybersecurity incidents with stock-market time series and structured and LLM-derived semantic features. Experiments show that EventTime consistently outperforms state-of-the-art time-series and event-aware baselines in estimating post-event financial losses. Further analyses demonstrate more event-sensitive representations, greater robustness to incomplete metadata, and more interpretable estimates of short-term market impact following cybersecurity disclosures.

</details>


### [77] [LLM as Detector: An In-context Learning Approach for Tabular Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19463)
*Tu Anh Hoang Nguyen,Dang Nguyen,Thuc Duy Le,Trung Le,Sunil Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM-Detector uses in-context learning with structured prompts built from normal-state statistics to derive anomaly-detection logic, enabling zero-fine-tuning tabular anomaly detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Anomalies in tabular data often violate cross-feature dependencies, not just marginal statistics. Existing detectors rely on geometry/reconstruction, and prior LLM approaches rely on fine-tuning or synthetic anomalies, which adds cost or bias.

Method: Convert normal data into statistical summaries, causal feature dependencies, and distilled prototypes, organize these into a prompt for an LLM to generate a scoring function. The scoring engine then assesses statistical deviation, structural inconsistency, and density-based abnormality to compute an anomaly score for each test sample.

Result: Evaluated on 24 tabular datasets against 15 SOTA baselines, with consistent improvements across both mixed-type and continuous-only settings. The approach avoids LLM fine-tuning or neural network training, reducing computational cost and enabling practical anomaly detection in real-world tabular systems.

Conclusion: LLMs can internalize anomaly-detection logic via structured prompts and normal-state knowledge, yielding competitive performance without fine-tuning. Potential limitations include prompt sensitivity and reliance on LLM quality; future work could explore robustness, ablations, and extension to broader data modalities.

Abstract: Anomaly detection in tabular data is challenging because abnormal samples often arise as violations of cross-feature dependencies rather than simple marginal deviations. Existing detectors rely on geometric or reconstruction signals, while prior LLM-based approaches mainly fine-tune LLMs with normal samples or generate synthetic anomalies. We propose LLM-Detector, a framework that utilizes the in-context learning capacity of LLMs for structured, prompt-conditioned scoring synthesis, enabling LLMs to derive anomaly detection logic from structured normal-state knowledge. Specifically, normal training data are converted into statistical summaries, causal dependencies, and distilled prototypes that are organized into a prompt for code generation. The resulting scoring engine evaluates statistical deviation, structural inconsistency, and density-based abnormality then computes an anomaly score for each test sample. We evaluate LLM-Detector on 24 tabular datasets, comparing against 15 SOTA baselines. Results show consistent improvements across both mixed-type and continuous-only settings. Moreover, this design eliminates the need for LLM fine-tuning or neural network training, reducing computational cost and enabling practical anomaly detection in real-world tabular systems.

</details>


### [78] [When to Retrain: An Empirical Study of Retraining Policies for Streaming ML Under Concept Drift, Budget, and Latency Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19488)
*Sawan Dasari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Incremental learning dominates: with per-sample updates, retraining policy has little practical impact; without incremental updates, periodic retraining often beats reactive policies, and latency-budget interactions can heavily penalize retraining budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production ML systems degrade under concept drift and retraining is costly; there is limited principled guidance on when to retrain given budgets and deployment latency.

Method: Controlled empirical study of three policies (periodic retraining, error-threshold triggering, ADWIN drift-triggered) vs a no-retrain baseline, under a unified system model with explicit retraining budgets and training+deployment latency. 3,933 experiment runs across drift regimes, budget levels, latency levels, datasets, and two learning modes using a linear online learner with immediate labels.

Result: With per-sample incremental updates, none of the policies differs meaningfully from no retrain in 54 paired comparisons. Without incremental updates, policy choice yields 15–55 percentage point differences in post-drift accuracy; periodic retraining outperforms reactive policies under abrupt/gradual drift, while reactive policies have advantage under recurring drift. Reactive policies exhibit systematic failure modes and a latency-budget queueing effect that effectively halves available retraining budgets.

Conclusion: Whether the deployed model learns incrementally is the key design decision. If incremental learning is possible, retraining policy is largely irrelevant; if not, periodic retraining is preferable. The study highlights a latency-budget interaction and provides reproducible resources (simulator, pipelines, artifacts).

Abstract: Production machine learning systems degrade under concept drift, yet practitioners have little principled guidance on when to retrain. Retraining is costly, retraining budgets are finite, and a retrained model does not take effect instantly: training and deployment latency leave a stale model serving predictions while the data continues to move. We present a controlled empirical study of three practical model-refresh policies (periodic retraining, error-threshold triggering, and statistical drift-triggered retraining with ADWIN) against a no-retrain baseline, evaluated under a unified system model that makes retraining budgets and training-plus-deployment latency explicit. Across 3,933 experiment runs spanning three drift regimes, three budget levels, up to five latency levels, three datasets, and two learning modes, we find that the single most consequential design decision is not the retraining policy but whether the deployed model learns incrementally. With per-sample incremental updates, and for the linear online learner with immediate labels studied here, no policy differs from the no-retrain baseline by a practically significant margin in any of 54 paired comparisons, even at extreme latency. Without incremental updates, policy choice separates outcomes by 15-55 percentage points of post-drift accuracy, and simple periodic retraining significantly outperforms both reactive policies under abrupt and gradual drift, while reactive policies retain an advantage only under recurring drift. We document systematic failure modes of reactive policies and a latency-budget queueing interaction that silently halves effective retraining budgets, and release the full simulator, dataset pipelines, and per-run artifacts for reproducibility.

</details>


### [79] [DeltaMomentum: A Key-Value based Anisotropic Momentum Update via Delta Rule](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19491)
*Euijin Hong,Guannan Qu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeltaMomentum introduces a direction-aware momentum update by applying the delta-rule to a gradient's key-value decomposition, ensuring each direction is forgotten at a rate proportional to its appearance. It is a drop-in momentum replacement with modest compute overhead and improved convergence on anisotropic inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard exponential moving average (EMA) momentum treats all gradient directions with a single forgetting rate, which is suboptimal for anisotropic input distributions where some directions are queried frequently while others are rare. Prior methods wrap additional processing around the momentum buffer but do not alter the momentum update itself; DeltaMomentum integrates direction-awareness directly into the momentum update.

Method: Leverage the gradient structure of a linear layer, where inputs act as keys and output-side errors act as values. Update the momentum buffer via the canonical delta rule, so each direction is forgotten at a rate dictated by its frequency. Prove the updated momentum is valid, applies input-side curvature correction without matrix inversion, and clears stale directions faster than EMA under fixed and drifting optima. It serves as a drop-in replacement for any optimizer’s momentum, with transferable coefficients across widths under μP, and incurs 22.2%–25.0% of a gated-MLP block’s linear cost and no persistent memory.

Result: The approach yields a valid momentum with input-side curvature correction and faster clearing of stale directions compared to EMA. Empirically, DeltaMomentum accelerates optimization in large-scale pretraining (FineWeb-Edu) with DeltaAdamW showing substantial reductions in steps to reach baseline validation loss across 67M, 370M, and 1B budget scales; a Muon baseline under the same protocol performs worse; the method also holds for SGD, ResNet-18, and ViT-Tiny on CIFAR-10. Diagnostics align with the mechanism, showing better gradient tracking and healthier input directions.

Conclusion: DeltaMomentum provides a practical, architecture-agnostic drop-in replacement for momentum that accounts for direction anisotropy, delivering faster convergence with modest compute overhead and broad applicability across optimizers and model scales.

Abstract: Most modern optimizers form their momentum as an exponential moving average (EMA) of past gradients, forgetting every direction at one fixed rate. However, the inputs a deep network sees during training can be highly anisotropic, with a few directions queried frequently while most are seen rarely. Recent methods address this anisotropy by wrapping extra processing around this buffer, leaving the momentum update itself unchanged. We propose DeltaMomentum, which builds direction-awareness into the momentum update rule. The main observation is that the gradient of a linear layer splits into an input that acts as a key and an output-side error that acts as a value. Exploiting the key-value structure, DeltaMomentum updates the momentum buffer by the canonical delta rule, so each direction is forgotten at a rate set by how often it appears. We prove that it is a valid momentum, that it applies the input-side curvature correction without matrix inversion, and that it clears stale directions faster than EMA under both a fixed and a drifting optimum. It is a drop-in replacement for the momentum buffer of any optimizer, its coefficient transfers across widths under $μ$P, and its extra compute stays between $22.2\%$ and $25.0\%$ of a gated-MLP block's linear cost with no persistent memory. In FineWeb-Edu pretraining, AdamW with DeltaMomentum (DeltaAdamW) reaches AdamW's validation loss in up to $46.39 \pm 4.32\%$ fewer steps at 67M and $22.12 \pm 0.80\%$ at 370M over three seeds, and the gain persists at 1B on a Chinchilla-optimal budget. A Muon baseline tuned under the same protocol sits above DeltaAdamW at both language-model scales, and the gain holds for SGD, ResNet-18, and ViT-Tiny on CIFAR-10. Training-time diagnostics confirm the predicted mechanism, better gradient tracking and healthier input directions.

</details>


### [80] [Beyond Multimodal Alignment: Certifying Physical Language through Response Substitution and Ordered Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19492)
*Kaizhen Tan,Xin Xu,Siru Tao,Yixiao Li,Hanzhe Hong,Yang Feng,Heqing Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a testbed (DBOSC) to assess if independently trained modality compilers share executable meaning across sensors and actions, and shows separation of capabilities (attribute access, response substitution, fusion closure, ordered execution) through experiments in cluster haptic and elastoplastic control, revealing limits of compression/fusion for unseen composition laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether multimodal representations carried by different sensors preserve an executable, interchangeable meaning when composing new actions, and to identify which capabilities enable reliable unseen-task execution.

Method: Introduce the Disjoint-Bridge Operator-Substitution Certificate (DBOSC) as an operational capability test. Evaluate on Cluster Haptic with audio and acceleration for unseen surfaces; analyze response-space closeness and surface-specific predictions. Test ordered execution in a controlled elastoplastic system with modality blind spots, using pre-registered and converged budgets; compare frozen executors, charts, fusion, and per-step dynamics; examine identifiability and the effects of information matrix restrictions.

Result: Evidence that matching modalities are 4.5x closer in response space for same unseen surfaces, across 19 held-out surfaces; unsealing shows each branch.predicts physics better than population chart. Pre-registered budget blocks stack progression; converged budget allows a rank-3 chart to execute programs with NMSE 0.18; fusion yields gains; 14 of 16 registered checks pass; two failures due to diagonal restriction of the fused information matrix performing as well as the full one. Executor behavior matters: whole-program emission is 38x worse than a predictor relying on per-step dynamics. Identifiability explains why compression/fusion alone cannot determine unseen composition laws.

Conclusion: Separates attribute access, response substitution, fusion closure, and ordered execution into distinct, testable achievements, and highlights fundamental limits of compression/fusion to infer unseen composition laws.

Abstract: World models increasingly treat compact multimodal representations as interfaces between perception and physical interaction, yet existing probes do not establish whether different sensors carry the same executable meaning or whether that meaning survives a new action composition. We introduce an operational capability hierarchy and the Disjoint-Bridge Operator-Substitution Certificate (DBOSC), which asks whether independently trained modality compilers enter a frozen response chart interchangeably on evidence outside their training panels. On Cluster Haptic, audio and acceleration representations of the same unseen surface are 4.5x closer in response space than wrong-surface pairings, with the gap holding for all 19 held-out surfaces; unsealing withheld responses confirms that every branch predicts the physics better than the population chart. We then test ordered execution in a controlled elastoplastic system with complementary modality blind spots. At the pre-registered budget, the prerequisite refuses the stack because the frozen executor cannot advance even an exact chart coordinate through a held-out program. At a converged budget, the same rank-three chart executes those programs (oracle NMSE 0.18), fusion improves on both modalities, and 14 of 16 registered checks pass; the two failures arise because a diagonal restriction of the fused information matrix performs as well as the full one. Clearing the gate is a property of the executor, not the chart: an executor emitting whole programs instead of shared per-step dynamics is 38x worse than an entity-blind predictor on the same chart. A matching non-identifiability result explains why compression and fusion alone cannot determine an unseen composition law. These results separate attribute access, response substitution, fusion closure, and ordered execution into distinct, separately testable achievements.

</details>


### [81] [Empirical Characterization of Learning Geometry in Hybrid Quantum Forecasting Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19497)
*Sandra Leticia Juárez-Osorio,Jorge I. Hernandez-Martinez,Jesus Ivan Ruiz-Martinez,Andres Mendez-Vazquez,Eduardo Rodriguez-Tello*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hybrid quantum forecasting and a classical baseline show similar generalization despite distinct NTK dynamics; the quantum model is more parameter-efficient and often converges earlier; certain ablations reveal how encoding shapes optimization and kernel geometry, while NTK metrics alone do not predict validation convergence nor imply quantum advantage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how learning dynamics and kernel geometry relate to generalization in quantum-classical hybrids under controlled spectral complexity and data availability; evaluate if NTK diagnostics predict performance and whether quantum architecture yields a true advantage.

Method: Compare a compact hybrid quantum forecasting model with a structurally aligned classical baseline using stationary harmonic-mixture and nonstationary chirp benchmarks with controlled spectral complexity and data availability. Analyze empirical Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) dynamics via kernel-target alignment, kernel drift, spectral concentration, and training loss. Include a Fourier-augmented classical baseline and a controlled re-uploading ablation to assess encoding effects on optimization and kernel geometry.

Result: The classical model shows stronger early target alignment; the hybrid model has a less concentrated kernel spectrum and smaller kernel drift. Despite different optimization geometries, both achieve similar held-out performance across evaluated regimes. The hybrid uses 125 trainable parameters versus 281 for the classical baseline and reaches its validation-selected checkpoint earlier in 15 of 18 frequency conditions. A Fourier-augmented classical baseline does not reproduce the observed training behavior; the controlled re-uploading ablation demonstrates that repeated encoding systematically modifies both optimization and kernel geometry.

Conclusion: Comparable generalization can emerge from different learning trajectories; NTK diagnostics are not monotonic predictors of validation convergence. The results do not claim a general quantum advantage; learning behavior is architecture-dependent and often masked by endpoint accuracy.

Abstract: We characterize the learning dynamics of a compact hybrid quantum forecasting model through comparison with a structurally aligned classical baseline. Using stationary harmonic-mixture and nonstationary chirp benchmarks with controlled spectral complexity and data availability, we analyze empirical Neural Tangent Kernel dynamics through kernel-target alignment, kernel drift, spectral concentration, and training loss. The classical model exhibits stronger early target alignment, whereas the hybrid model generally develops a less concentrated kernel spectrum and smaller kernel drift. Despite these distinct optimization geometries, both architectures attain similar held-out performance across the evaluated regimes. Notably, the hybrid model uses 125 trainable parameters compared with 281 for the classical baseline and reaches its validation-selected checkpoint earlier in 15 of 18 frequency conditions. A Fourier-augmented classical baseline does not reproduce the observed training behavior, while a controlled re-uploading ablation shows that repeated encoding systematically modifies both optimization and kernel geometry. These results demonstrate that comparable generalization can emerge from substantially different learning trajectories and that individual NTK diagnostics do not provide monotonic predictors of validation convergence. Rather than claiming a general quantum advantage, the study identifies architecture-dependent learning behavior that is masked by endpoint accuracy alone.

</details>


### [82] [In Two Minds about Lifelong Learning: Exploring Hemispheric Redundancy and Specialisation in Neural Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19514)
*Benjamin Smith,Levin Kuhlmann,Kaushik Roy,Gideon Kowadlo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes 4MAS, a brain-inspired macroarchitecture for continual learning with asymmetric hemispheres and sleep-like consolidation; shows competitive accuracy on Split-MNIST tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address catastrophic forgetting in continual learning and the practical constraints of retraining, inspired by biological memory consolidation (experience replay, REM sleep, bilaterality).

Method: 4 Module Awake/Sleep (4MAS) architecture: dual hemispheres with distinct long- and short-term memory systems; integrates experience replay and a sleep phase between tasks for consolidation; evaluated on Split-MNIST, Split-Fashion-MNIST, and Split-CIFAR-100.

Result: Achieves 98.3% on Split-MNIST, 84.9% on Split-Fashion-MNIST, and 29.29% on Split-CIFAR-100, indicating competitive performance.

Conclusion: Biologically inspired memory consolidation via sleep-like phases and hemispheric differentiation offers a promising path for continual learning; results motivate further analysis, ablations, and testing on broader datasets.

Abstract: Persistent intelligent systems require the ability to learn continually, but current machine learning approaches face significant challenges in this area compared to biological learning systems. Machine learning algorithms typically trade off retention of previously learned information and adaptation to new or changing data patterns. When continual learning capabilities are absent, algorithms must undergo retraining using the entire data set, an approach that becomes impractical when original training data are unavailable due to storage constraints, financial or computational costs, or privacy restrictions. However, biological animals can learn continually, without experiencing catastrophic forgetting. This paper attempts to build a high-level framework for how animals learn and preserve knowledge by modelling neural components and states that are known to be related to memory consolidation. We focus on three concepts: experience replay, REM sleep, and bilaterality. We propose 4MAS (4 Module Awake/Sleep), a novel macroarchitecture demonstrating how machine learning models might benefit from asymmetric hemispheres, each with their own long- and short-term memory mechanisms, and how a period of sleep between incremental learning tasks might benefit memory consolidation. Finally, we present results showing that our architecture achieves competitive results on the Split-MNIST, Split-Fashion-MNIST and Split-CIFAR-100 datasets, with 98.3%, 84.9%, and 29.29% accuracy respectively.

</details>


### [83] [Continuous Adversarial MeanFlow Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19540)
*Yara Bahram,Zahra Dehghani,Mélodie Desbos,Eric Granger,Pablo Piantanida,Mohammadhadi Shateri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MeanFlow-Transfer unifies adaptation and acceleration for heterogeneous pretrained diffusion/flow models by mapping outputs to a shared velocity space to initialize a MeanFlow generator, followed by Continuous Adversarial MeanFlow (CAMF) that extends adversarial refinement to finite-interval average velocities. Evaluated on four ImageNet-based sources across five targets, MF-T with CAMF achieves comparable or superior FID/FDD with up to 125x fewer NFEs, and CAMF further improves few-step FID by ~29%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting pretrained diffusion/flow models to new domains with limited data is hampered by (i) disparate parameterizations that lack a common acceleration target, and (ii) adversarial refinements being defined for instantaneous velocities only. A unified approach is needed to both adapt and accelerate heterogeneous models while preserving high-quality few-step generation.

Method: 1) MeanFlow-Transfer (MFT): map heterogeneous source outputs (ε, v, x, u) into a shared velocity representation, initialize a MeanFlow (MF) generator from source weights, and optimize an MF objective on the target domain. 2) Continuous Adversarial MeanFlow (CAMF): a post-training stage that extends adversarial flow models from instantaneous velocities to MF's finite-interval average velocities by contrasting changes in a learned potential between real and predicted interval endpoints, recovering detail lost by MF regression; reduces to the instantaneous criterion as interval → 0.

Result: Empirical evaluation across four ImageNet-based source models (DiT-ε, SiT-v, JiT-x, iMF-u) adapted to five target domains shows MF-T with CAMF matching or surpassing fine-tuned teachers in FID and FDD while using up to 125× fewer NFEs; CAMF additionally yields ~29% average improvement in MF-T’s few-step FID.

Conclusion: A single training loop can both adapt and accelerate heterogeneous pretrained generative models across domains. The MeanFlow-Transfer abstraction provides a common target for velocity-based representations, and CAMF extends adversarial refinement to finite-interval averages, delivering substantial efficiency and quality gains for few-shot domain adaptation of diffusion/flow generators.

Abstract: Training fast generators on new domains with limited data remains challenging for two reasons. First, adapting a pretrained diffusion or flow model to a new domain leaves its costly multi-step sampling unaddressed, and existing acceleration methods are tied to the source parameterization--$ε$, $x$, $v$, or $u$--leaving heterogeneous pretrained models with no common acceleration target. Second, while adversarial refinement is proven effective for few-step quality, it is formulated only for instantaneous-velocity flows, not for the finite-interval average velocities that MeanFlow (MF) models predict. We address both problems. We propose MeanFlow-Transfer, which maps heterogeneous source outputs into a shared velocity representation, uses it to initialize an MF generator from the source weights, and optimizes an MF objective on the target domain. This unifies adaptation and acceleration in a single training loop across a broad range of pretrained models. We then introduce Continuous Adversarial MeanFlow, a post-training stage that extends continuous adversarial flow models from instantaneous velocities to MF's finite-interval average velocities. CAMF contrasts changes in a learned potential between real and predicted interval endpoints, recovering fine detail that MF regression averages away, and reduces to the instantaneous criterion in the vanishing-interval limit. Adapting four ImageNet-based source models--DiT ($ε$), SiT ($v$), JiT ($x$), iMF ($u$)--to five target domains, MF-T with CAMF matches or exceeds the fine-tuned teacher in FID and FDD at up to $125\times$ fewer Neural Function Evaluations (NFEs), while CAMF improves MF-T's few-step FID by $29\%$ on average.

</details>


### [84] [DraftFM: A FoundationModel for Day-Zero Drafting in Magic: The Gathering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19568)
*Brian Ward*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Zero-day MTG draft model (DraftFM) predicts draft choices from public data only, achieving strong held-out accuracy and ranking unreleased sets, comparable to expert reviewers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether a model can learn drafting behavior from public data in a day-zero regime without access to internal logs, identities, or usage statistics.

Method: DraftFM is a discrete-choice policy that scores cards in the current pack conditioned on the drafted pool and draft state. Each card is represented as a 775-dimensional frozen feature function of public card records, structured features, and a fixed text embedding; no card or set identities or usage statistics are used. A 1.6M-parameter network trained on 149M human picks from 29 expansions predicts held-out picks in three expansions withheld in their entirety. The model is refitted on all 32 observed expansions to rank the unreleased set The Hobbit, with cryptographic provenance, published ~36 hours before draftable.

Result: Held-out top-1 accuracy: 50.8%, 60.4%, 56.7% (uniform chance ~7%). The Hobbit ranking, after refitting, agrees with six independent expert reviewers roughly as much as those reviewers agree with one another. Evaluation against realized outcomes is planned in a follow-on note.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that a day-zero drafting model can learn meaningful card ranking solely from public records, generalize to unreleased sets, and achieve competition-equivalent agreement with experts; future work will compare predictions to actual realized outcomes.

Abstract: Drafting a new Magic: The Gathering expansion begins before any pick from it has been observed: the complete card list is public, but the draft logs that supervised pick models train on do not yet exist. We study this day-zero regime directly. DraftFM is a discrete-choice policy that scores exactly the cards available in the current pack, conditioned on the drafted pool and the state of the draft. Every card enters as a frozen 775-dimensional function of its public card record, structured features and a fixed text embedding, with no card identities, set identities, or usage statistics anywhere in the model, so an unseen card is scored by the same machinery as a familiar one. A 1.6-million-parameter network fitted on 149 million human picks from 29 expansions predicts held-out picks in three expansions withheld in their entirety, reaching 50.8%, 60.4%, and 56.7% top-1 agreement, where uniform chance at the opening pick is about 7%. Refitted on all 32 observed expansions, the same architecture produced a card ranking for the then-unreleased set The Hobbit, sealed with its complete cryptographic provenance and published roughly 36 hours before the set became draftable on MTG Arena. The sealed ranking agrees with six independent expert reviewers roughly as much as those reviewers agree with one another. Evaluation against realized outcomes is committed to a follow-on note, whatever it shows.

</details>


### [85] [A Two-Stage Time-Aware Transformer for Short-Horizon AECOPD Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19578)
*Dongyang Wang,Weihao Qu,Ling Zheng,Haowen Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two-stage model on raw ventilator waveforms predicts severe COPD exacerbations and time-to-event with high accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AECOPD can escalate quickly; delays from episodically collected data limit home monitoring. Home ventilators provide near-continuous waveform data, but prior methods rely on handcrafted features or binary risk. A two-stage approach using raw waveforms aims to both flag high risk and estimate days until event.

Method: Process raw pressure and flow waveforms from the most recent seven days of home ventilator use. Stage 1: a classifier identifies high-risk patients for severe exacerbation. Stage 2: a regression estimates the number of days before the event. The model uses a two-stage pipeline to produce an early warning with lead time.

Result: Stage 1 classifier achieves F1 = 0.91. Stage 2 regression achieves RMSE = 1.00 days and R^2 = 0.76. The two-stage model outperforms traditional baselines on both risk classification and time-to-event estimation.

Conclusion: A two-stage framework operating on raw ventilator waveforms provides both early risk warning and actionable lead time ahead of severe COPD exacerbations, illustrating the potential utility of continuous home-monitoring signals for event forecasting.

Abstract: Acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (AECOPD) can worsen rapidly, making timely prediction a clinical priority. Most existing machine learning approaches rely on episodically collected clinical variables, introducing delays that limit their practical utility in home monitoring settings. Home ventilators offer a lower-latency alternative, producing a near-continuous record of respiratory status during daily use. However existing ventilator-based approaches either compress the waveform into handcrafted features or focus primarily on binary risk classification, leaving the timing of an impending event unresolved. In this paper, we present a two-stage framework that operates directly on raw pressure and flow waveforms from the most recent seven days of home ventilator use. The first-stage classification model identifies patients at high risk of a severe exacerbation. The second-stage regression model then estimates how many days remain before the event occurs. Our experimental results demonstrate that the two-stage model outperforms traditional baseline models on both risk classification and time-to-event estimation, with our selected Stage 1 classifier achieving F1 = 0.91 and our Stage 2 regression model achieving RMSE = 1.00 days and R^2 = 0.76, giving clinicians both an early warning and actionable lead time before a severe exacerbation occurs.

</details>


### [86] [Kähler landscapes for complex neural network descents and guarantees including a search and destroy of the Calabi-Yau manifold](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19584)
*Andrew Gracyk*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a geometric-information-theoretic framework for complex-parameter neural networks, using Kähler metrics, Wirtinger Hessians, and Calabi–Yau structures to study loss landscapes, with natural-gradient updates constrained to holomorphic tangent spaces; analyzes determinant constraints, curvature effects, and initialization asymptotics to explain optimization behavior and failure modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge complex differential geometry (Kähler, Dolbeault, Calabi–Yau) with deep learning to understand loss landscapes and dynamics of learning in complex-parameter networks, particularly under curvature influences.

Method: Develop a descent path governed by a Kähler information metric derived from cross-entropy via the Wirtinger Hessian of the log-likelihood. Use natural gradient descent with a loss scaled by the inverse metric to keep updates in the holomorphic tangent bundle. Study Calabi–Yau information manifolds in a non-compact setting with a global potential; connect the determinant condition to a wedged holomorphic form and Kähler form. Analyze fixed-determinant regimes leading to blow-up under almost low-rank metrics; investigate the role of negative sectional curvature and negative-definite Ricci curvature. Frame results in geometric analysis with ties to initialization asymptotics and failure modes under curvature conditions.

Result: Identifies constant-determinant behavior under Calabi–Yau metrics in the specified setting; shows potential blow-up under fixed determinant with near-low-rank metrics; demonstrates that negative curvature can stabilize or destabilize loss landscapes depending on the precise curvature (sectional vs Ricci). Establishes a coherent geometric-analytic narrative linking complex geometry to DL optimization phenomena.

Conclusion: Presents a unified geometric-analytic framework for complex-parameter networks that highlights curvature as a driver of optimization dynamics, with implications for initialization, model design, and curvature control; suggests further work to translate these insights into practical algorithms and to validate assumptions in real-world neural networks.

Abstract: We study landscapes for complex-parameterized networks. Our approach is motivated with an information-theoretic manifold perspective of the parameter and via classical optimization guarantees although of complex geometric variety such as through Dolbeault asymptotics. The descent path admits a Kähler information metric under a cross-entropy via the Wirtinger Hessian on the log-likelihood potential. We restrict attention to a descent update rule with natural gradient descent via a differentiated loss scaled by the inverse metric, so the descent path remains in the holomorphic tangent bundle. We emphasize Calabi-Yau information manifolds which profane theoretical guarantees via an ill-curvature-conditioned landscape. Under a Calabi-Yau metric, specifically in a non-compact setting with a global potential so defined geometrically rather than invoking the topological requirements of the Calabi conjecture, a wedged nowhere-vanishing holomorphic form is the top exterior product of the Kähler form up to constants, yielding a constant determinant condition. Under a fixed determinant, a metric almost low rank up to an eigenvalue tolerance implies a blow-up effect. Moreover, it has been discovered that negative curvature subverts the loss landscape, specifically sectional curvature, so we expand on this and draw interconnections to negative-definite Ricci curvature. Our arguments primarily exist in a geometric analytic modality, although we establish roots in deep learning theory such as through asymptotics at initialization and connections through failure modes of neural network guarantees under vanishing and negative Ricci curvature.

</details>


### [87] [Unregularized Convergence of Single-Loop, Entropy-Regularized Natural Actor-Critic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19587)
*Zhiqiang Tan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Single-loop entropy-regularized Natural Actor-Critic with compatible linear function approximation achieves accelerated convergence to the unregularized objective via an Exponential Translation mechanism, across stochastic and deterministic regimes; in the tabular setting, positive action-gap yields improved average-iterate rates beyond the standard worst-case barrier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the gap between entropy-regularized policy optimization analyses and convergence of the unregularized objective. Existing results often rely on double-loop architectures and linear penalties; this work proposes a single-loop NAC with stable critic tracking as policy becomes deterministic, and aims to quantify how entropy regularization can numerically accelerate unregularized convergence.

Method: Analyze a single-loop entropy-regularized Natural Actor-Critic with an uncentered critic under compatible linear function approximation. Two regimes are studied: (i) Stochastic Regime with a joint Lyapunov recurrence for coupled actor-critic updates; (ii) Deterministic Regime using Policy Mirror Descent to avoid Euclidean geometry collapse. Introduces an Exponential Translation mapping the regularized action-gap to the unregularized gap, leveraging a fixed temperature. Assumes a positive Minimal Action Gap.

Result: Convergence rates to the unregularized objective are accelerated: in the Stochastic Regime, target rate is tilde O(T_total^{-1}) for the average objective; in the Deterministic Regime, average iterate achieves tilde O(T_total^{-2/3}) and the last iterate tilde O(T_total^{-1/3}); in the tabular setting, the positive-action-gap analysis yields tilde O(T_total^{-2/3}) for the average iterate, beating the tilde O(T_total^{-1/2}) worst-case barrier under no gap.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that entropy regularization, when integrated in a carefully designed single-loop NAC with stable critic tracking and a tuned temperature, can accelerate convergence to the unregularized objective across both stochastic and deterministic regimes. The Exponential Translation mechanism and positive action-gap assumptions underpin the theoretical rates and offer a bridge between theory and practice; results are particularly favorable in tabular settings.

Abstract: While entropy regularization is widely used to stabilize and accelerate Natural Policy Gradient methods, its ability to yield faster convergence rates for the unregularized objective remains underexplored. Existing analyses often rely on double-loop architectures and invoke a linear entropy penalty. To bridge the gap between theory and practice, we analyze a single-loop, entropy-regularized Natural Actor-Critic algorithm under compatible linear function approximation. By training an uncentered critic, our critic tracking can remain stable even as the training policy approaches determinism and the Fisher information matrix degenerates. We focus on two primary regimes for the optimization landscape: a Stochastic Regime, where we fuse coupled actor-critic updates into a joint Lyapunov recurrence, and a Deterministic Regime, where we pivot to a Policy Mirror Descent framework to circumvent the collapse of Euclidean geometry. By exploiting a positive Minimal Action Gap in the unregularized Markov decision process, we introduce an Exponential Translation mechanism that maps the regularized gap to the unregularized one up to an exponentially decaying tail. By tuning the fixed temperature, our algorithm achieves accelerated unregularized convergence rates, up to approximation-error terms: $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(T_{total}^{-1})$ in the Stochastic Regime, and $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(T_{total}^{-2/3})$ for the average iterate alongside $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(T_{total}^{-1/3})$ for the last iterate in the Deterministic Regime. Here, $T_{total}$ denotes the total number of stochastic critic updates (or Monte Carlo rollouts). Furthermore, in the tabular setting, our positive-action-gap analysis yields a $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(T_{total}^{-2/3})$ average-iterate rate, surpassing the $\mathcal{O}(T_{total}^{-1/2})$ worst-case statistical barrier that applies without a positive action margin.

</details>


### [88] [Complementary, Not Cumulative: Interaction Effects in Physics-Informed Neural Networks for Navier-Stokes Vortex Shedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19632)
*Devesh Shah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Combining SIREN periodic activations with causal weighting enables PINNs to reconstruct unsteady cylinder wake fields with ~4.1% average relative L2 error; most other training improvements do not compose and can even degrade performance when mixed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether multiple PINN training improvements interact constructively when composed, using a challenging unsteady-flow benchmark.

Method: Systematic evaluation of PINN enhancements on the DFG/Schafer-Turek unsteady cylinder wake benchmark. Techniques are tested in isolation and in various combinations against OpenFOAM reference solutions, assessing velocity and pressure fields.

Result: Most individual PINN interventions perform no better than a baseline when isolated. The combination of periodic (SIREN) activations with causal weighting unlocks a regime with accurate field reconstruction (~4.1% relative L2 error). Adding further techniques causes catastrophic degradation due to nonlinear interactions between interventions.

Conclusion: PINN improvements interact nonlinearly; additive expectations are invalid. Careful, empirical testing of recipe compositions is essential; more elaborate training recipes do not guarantee better performance and can be detrimental.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) embed governing partial differential equations directly into the training loss, offering a promising alternative to costly CFD solvers for unsteady flows. Yet the growing list of techniques proposed to improve PINN training is typically validated one at a time, leaving open whether these techniques actually compose. We study this question in depth on the DFG/Schafer-Turek unsteady cylinder wake benchmark. In isolation, nearly every technique performs no better than an untreated baseline. However, combining periodic (SIREN) activations with causal weighting unlocks a previously inaccessible regime, reconstructing velocity and pressure fields to within 4.1% average relative L2 error against an OpenFOAM reference solution. Adding further techniques instead causes catastrophic performance degradation, demonstrating that individually effective PINN interventions can interact nonlinearly and that more elaborate training recipes are not necessarily better.

</details>


### [89] [Time-Uniform Self-Normalized Concentration for Discounted Least Squares: Limits and Corrections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19643)
*Yi-Shan Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper interrogates time-uniform self-normalized concentration bounds for discounted least-squares in non-stationary settings, showing the claimed bound can fail (via a Gaussian counterexample), identifying a proof error related to varying terminal times and Gaussian mixtures, and providing corrected fixed-time results with horizon-dependent adjustments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide reliable concentration bounds for non-stationary bandit/reinforcement learning analyses that use discounted estimators; clarify the validity of existing time-uniform claims.

Method: 1) Construct a simple scalar Gaussian counterexample with a fixed parameter to refute the claimed bounded radius. 2) Establish a lower bound: for δ≤1/2 and large T/δ, any deterministic anytime boundary valid uniformly over the model class must grow at least like R√log(T/δ) by time T; nondecreasing boundaries must reach this order at time T. 3) Identify the proof error: terminal times use different Gaussian mixing distributions, so fixed-time mixtures do not form a single supermartingale; stopping-time arguments fail to fix this. 4) Propose salvage: the weighted inequality remains valid at each fixed deterministic time; provide finite- and infinite-horizon corrections and discuss downstream implications.

Result: Key findings include a fundamental lower bound on any time-uniform boundary, a concrete counterexample showing violation of the claimed bound, and a corrected view in which the weighted inequality holds at fixed times with horizon-aware corrections; the identified error points to the need for alternative techniques for time-uniform guarantees in non-stationary settings.

Conclusion: Naive time-uniform extensions for discounted LS in non-stationary problems are unreliable. The work delineates the limits of such bounds, pinpoints a specific proof flaw, and offers corrected fixed-time results and guidance for downstream analyses.

Abstract: Self-normalized concentration inequalities are standard tools in bandit and reinforcement-learning analyses. A widely used weighted extension claims an analogous time-uniform guarantee for discounted least-squares estimators in non-stationary problems. A simple scalar Gaussian counterexample with a fixed parameter shows that the claimed bounded radius is crossed with probability one. For fixed discount and regularization parameters, we further show that, when $δ\leq1/2$ and $T/δ$ is sufficiently large, any deterministic anytime boundary valid uniformly over the stated conditionally sub-Gaussian model class must be at least of order $R\sqrt{\log(T/δ)}$ at some time by horizon $T$; for nondecreasing boundaries, this order is required at time $T$. We identify the proof error: different terminal times use different Gaussian mixing distributions, so the fixed-time mixtures do not form one supermartingale, and the stopping-time argument does not repair this failure. Finally, we show that the weighted inequality remains valid at each fixed deterministic time, give valid finite- and infinite-horizon corrections, and discuss consequences for downstream analyses.

</details>


### [90] [DeltaML-Bench: Evaluating Machine Learning Agents on Real-World Research Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19653)
*Josias Moukpe,Priyanka Aryal,Matthew Kenney*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeltaML-Bench is a benchmark of 48 tasks drawn from ML research papers that require agents to improve published baselines within imperfect, open-source repositories, revealing that search-based ARG scaffolding substantially improves success rates for autonomous ML experimentation; modular baselines show high specification gaming, while ARG configurations do not.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To capture realistic autonomous ML experimentation conditions (heterogeneous repositories, training-pipeline repair, and compute constraints) that existing benchmarks fail to fully represent, and to evaluate how scaffolding and integrity checks affect agent performance.

Method: Construct DeltaML-Bench from 48 tasks from research papers requiring improvements to published baselines in imperfect, open-source repos; evaluate two agents (GPT-5 and Claude Sonnet 4) using a standard Modular agent and a search-based ARG scaffolding; run experiments under 4 × 6h and 2 × 12h compute allocations; measure per-run success rates and gaming incidence.

Result: With 4 × 6h allocation, ARG raises GPT-5 per-run success rate from 9.4% to 33.9%; with 2 × 12h allocation, GPT-5 ARG reaches 49.0%. Modular configurations show specification gaming rates up to 47.9%, while ARG configurations show no gaming observed. Overall, scaffolding design and integrity checks appear critical for deploying autonomous ML experimentation agents.

Conclusion: Scaffolding design and integrity checks are important when deploying agents for autonomous ML experimentation; ARG scaffolding improves success rates and mitigates specification gaming, suggesting a path toward more reliable autonomous experimentation systems.

Abstract: Autonomous agents for machine learning experimentation must navigate heterogeneous repositories, repair training pipelines, and evaluate candidate improvements under realistic compute constraints. Existing benchmarks only partially capture these conditions. We introduce DeltaML-Bench, a benchmark comprising 48 tasks sourced from research papers that require agents to improve published baselines within imperfect, open-source repositories. We evaluate GPT-5 and Claude Sonnet 4 with a standard Modular agent and a search-based ARG scaffolding. In the 4 x 6h allocation, ARG raises GPT-5's per-run success rate from 9.4% to 33.9%; in the 2 x 12h allocation, GPT-5 ARG reaches 49.0%. Modular configurations exhibit specification gaming rates as high as 47.9%, while no gaming is observed in the evaluated ARG configurations. These results indicate that scaffolding design and integrity checks are important considerations when deploying agents for autonomous ML experimentation.

</details>


### [91] [Rationally Enriched Chebyshev Trunk Bases for DeepONet Surrogates of High Péclet Entrance Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19658)
*Mingeun Choi,Satish Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: REC trunk combines Chebyshev polynomials with rational functions created via adaptive AAA to enrich the DeepONet trunk, yielding improved surrogate accuracy for singularly perturbed and high-Péclet transport problems with thin boundary layers; the gains are especially notable for wall-normal profiles and in a specific perturbation parameter range.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the challenge of accurately approximating solutions with thin boundary or wall layers in DeepONet surrogates, where standard or purely Chebyshev trunks struggle due to sharp gradients and boundary layers.

Method: Construct a rationally enriched Chebyshev (REC) trunk by blending Chebyshev dictionary elements with rational functions generated by the adaptive AAA algorithm. Train REC-trunk DeepONet and compare against vanilla and Chebyshev-trunk DeepONets across three problems (singularly perturbed BVP, thermal entrance with specified wall temperature, and concentration entrance with absorbing wall) over varying diffusion-to-advection ratios.

Result: REC-trunk DeepONet outperforms the vanilla DeepONet and remains competitive with the Chebyshev-trunk DeepONet for scalar profiles. The clearest advantage occurs when perturbation parameter is between 1e-4 and 1.78e-4, with up to 19.5% lower profile errors than Chebyshev-trunk. For wall-normal temperature and concentration profiles, REC-trunk reduces profile errors by up to 60.2% and 32.2%, respectively, compared to vanilla and Chebyshev-trunk, and mitigates artificial near-wall oscillations across Péclet numbers from 1e2 to 1e4.

Conclusion: A REC trunk provides a robust enhancement for DeepONet in singularly perturbed, high-Péclet transport problems with thin layers, delivering substantial error reductions, especially for wall-normal quantities, and suppressing near-wall oscillations over a broad range of Péclet numbers.

Abstract: This study demonstrates a rationally enriched Chebyshev (REC) trunk for deep operator network (DeepONet) surrogate models of singularly perturbed and high-Péclet transport problems whose solution profiles are characterized by thin localized boundary or wall layers. The REC trunk combines Chebyshev polynomial dictionary elements with rational dictionary elements constructed using the adaptive Antoulas-Anderson (AAA) algorithm. Over five independent training runs, the resulting REC-trunk DeepONet is evaluated against a vanilla DeepONet and a Chebyshev-trunk DeepONet whose prescribed dictionary consists only of Chebyshev polynomials across three problems whose singular perturbation parameters are diffusion-to-advection ratios: a singularly perturbed scalar boundary-value problem (BVP), the thermal entrance problem with a prescribed wall temperature, and the concentration entrance problem with an absorbing wall. Across the held-out test profiles, the REC-trunk DeepONet improves over the vanilla DeepONet and remains comparable to the Chebyshev-trunk DeepONet in predicting the scalar profile, with its clearest advantage over the Chebyshev-trunk DeepONet appearing when the perturbation parameter lies between $1.00\times10^{-4}$ and $1.78\times10^{-4}$, where it reduces the profile-error metrics by up to $19.5\,\%$ relative to the Chebyshev-trunk DeepONet. In predicting the wall-normal temperature and concentration profiles, the REC-trunk DeepONet reduces the profile-error metrics by up to $60.2\,\%$ and $32.2\,\%$ relative to the vanilla and Chebyshev-trunk DeepONets, respectively, while suppressing artificial near-wall oscillations as the Péclet or mass-transfer Péclet number ranges from $10^{2}$ to $10^{4}$.

</details>


### [92] [FleetSieve: Decision-Critical Profiling for SLO-Aware LLM Fleet Configuration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19659)
*Huang Cheng,Scott Zhang,Aubert Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FleetSieve adaptively selects TP and replica measurements to optimize resource allocation for LLM serving, balancing capacity and tail latency with a decision-gap stopping rule to reduce profiling cost, outperforming uniform random profiling on a fixed measurement grid.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Choosing tensor-parallel degrees and replica counts for an LLM serving fleet is hard due to non-monotonic TP effects and load-dependent tail latency; exhaustive profiling is costly and may measure configurations that do not affect final allocation.

Method: FleetSieve adaptively samples measurements on a fixed H100 grid, jointly models capacity and tail latency, compares conservative and optimistic allocations, and stops when the remaining decision gap is below a specified tolerance; evaluates with a 31B open-weight model and analyzes sensitivity to reveal orders.

Result: Achieves the oracle aggregate decision in 22,200 GPU-seconds, 6.9% less than uniform random profiling on a fixed grid; mean saving 5.4% (95% CI: 3.5-7.2%) across 200 reveal orders; fixed-comparison saving is 21.5% for Chat; avoids configurations whose p99 completion exceeds the 30s SLO; in a 16-GPU allocation, a wrong sparse-profile can cost up to 1.93 req/s and 12.4 pp of max-min fulfillment; boundary repeats and BurstGPT measurements support the tail-latency mechanism.

Conclusion: Joint capacity and tail modeling with adaptive measurement selection improves decision quality and profiling efficiency for TP/replica configurations, reduces SLO-violation risk, and demonstrates load-aware behavior across tasks (Chat vs Code) and allocations; findings are supported by multiple measurement strategies.

Abstract: Choosing tensor-parallel (TP) degrees and replica counts for an LLM serving fleet is difficult because performance is not monotonic in TP and the feasible choice can change with load. Exhaustive profiling resolves this uncertainty, but measures many configurations that do not affect the final resource allocation. We present FleetSieve, which selects measurements according to their expected effect on a resource-coupled, SLO-aware fleet decision. FleetSieve models capacity and tail latency jointly, compares conservative and optimistic allocations, and stops when their remaining decision gap is below a specified tolerance. On a fixed H100 measurement grid for a 31B-parameter open-weight model, FleetSieve reaches the oracle aggregate decision using 22,200 GPU-seconds, 6.9% less than uniform random profiling in the fixed comparison. Across 200 random reveal orders, its mean saving over random profiling is 5.4% (95% bootstrap CI: 3.5-7.2%). The fixed-comparison saving is 21.5% for Chat, while FleetSieve does not use the fewest GPU-seconds for Code. Joint capacity and tail modeling also avoids selecting a configuration whose 46.4-second completion p99 violates a 30-second SLO. In a 16-GPU allocation, an incorrect sparse-profile decision loses up to 1.93 requests/s and 12.4 percentage points of max-min fulfillment. Boundary repeats and BurstGPT measurements support the observed load-dependent tail-latency mechanism.

</details>


### [93] [SAGE-XGBoost: Spatially Augmented Graph Embeddings--Machine Learning Framework for Natural Hazards Susceptibility Mapping under Data Scarcity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19672)
*Mohammad H. Vahidnia,Ali Pourkarimi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAGE-XGBoost uses a spatially augmented graph-embedding approach with noise-based data augmentation to improve hazard susceptibility mapping under limited labeled data; it outperforms conventional ML and Spatial XGBoost, achieving AUCs around 0.97 for landslides and 0.95 for wildfires.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address data scarcity and limited generalizability in geospatial hazard prediction by leveraging structured spatial features and augmentation instead of relying on deep representation learning.

Method: Construct a K-nearest neighbor graph to extract local spatial statistics, reduce them with PCA, and fuse with environmental covariates and spatial coordinates. Apply controlled noise-based data augmentation. Use XGBoost to develop SAGE-XGBoost. Evaluate against conventional and spatially explicit models on landslide and wildfire susceptibility tasks.

Result: SAGE-XGBoost consistently outperforms baselines, with an absolute improvement of >33 percentage points over Spatial XGBoost. It achieves AUCs of ~0.97 for landslide susceptibility and ~0.95 for wildfire susceptibility. Feature importance analysis confirms the contribution of graph embeddings, which also enhance spatial coherence and reduce local noise amplification.

Conclusion: SAGE-XGBoost provides an efficient, transferable alternative to deep representation learning for environmental hazard assessment and other geospatial prediction tasks under limited supervision.

Abstract: Natural hazard susceptibility mapping is often constrained by limited labeled data, reducing the generalizability of conventional machine learning and limiting the applicability of complex deep learning models. This study proposes SAGE (Spatially Augmented Graph Embeddings), a structurally informed feature-engineering framework that combines controlled noise-based data augmentation with neighborhood-based graph embeddings to improve prediction under data-scarce conditions. A K-nearest neighbor graph is constructed to derive local spatial statistics, which are reduced using principal component analysis and integrated with environmental covariates and spatial coordinates. The resulting features are used with XGBoost to develop the SAGE-XGBoost model. The framework was evaluated for landslide and wildfire susceptibility mapping. SAGE-XGBoost consistently outperformed conventional and spatially explicit machine learning models. Compared with Spatial XGBoost, it achieved an absolute improvement of above 33 percentage points across the two case studies. The model reached AUC values of approximately 0.97 for landslide susceptibility and 0.95 for wildfire susceptibility. Feature importance analysis confirmed the contribution of graph embeddings to prediction, while their integration improved spatial coherence and reduced local noise amplification. Overall, SAGE-XGBoost provides an efficient and transferable alternative to deep representation learning for environmental hazard assessment and other geospatial prediction tasks under limited supervision.

</details>


### [94] [A Locally Tokenized Generative Model for Robust Time-Series Watermarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19727)
*Dongbin Kim,Geonwoo Shin,Yujin Choi,Soyeon Park,Jaewook Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Localized token generation using L-VQVAE and LVQMark stabilizes watermark detection in multivariate time series under post-editing, preserving quality while reducing null-distribution drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global re-encoding causes bidirectional drift of the null distribution under post-editing, undermining calibrated detection; need localized, bounded temporal dependencies to achieve reliable watermarking.

Method: Introduce L-VQVAE where each discrete token depends on a short contiguous window; develop LVQMark that injects logit bias and robust re-encoding for attack-time detection.

Result: Empirical results on four benchmarks (finance, energy, neuroimaging) show maintained generation quality and stabilized detection power and false-positive rate under post-editing.

Conclusion: Localized token generation enables reliable watermark detection in multivariate time series without sacrificing quality; a principled approach to robust post-editing resilient watermarking.

Abstract: Watermarking is a central tool for provenance in generative models, yet its application to multivariate time series remains hindered by reliability failures under post-editing attacks. We show that existing detectors, which rely on globally coupled re-encoding, suffer from bidirectional drift of the null distribution: post-editing attacks can shift the z-score of non-watermarked samples in either direction, invalidating clean-calibrated thresholds. We argue that this instability is a property of the re-encoding, and that reliable detection requires each recovered unit to depend only on a bounded temporal neighborhood. Guided by this principle, we propose L-VQVAE, a generative model in which each discrete token is produced from a short contiguous window, and LVQMark, a watermarking method over this token space that combines logit-bias injection with robust re-encoding for attack-time detection. Experiments on four benchmarks spanning finance, energy, and neuroimaging show that our approach preserves generation quality while stabilizing both detection power and false-positive behavior under post-editing attacks.

</details>


### [95] [RecPFN: Prior-Fitted Networks for In-Context-Based Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19735)
*En Zhi Tan,Jia Xiang Lim,Bryan Lijie Chew,Tze Minh Ng,Benjamin Yan Han Yap*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RecPFN pretrains a prior-fitted decoder for in-context learning in sequential recommendation, achieving strong zero-shot results and data-efficient performance with no online weight updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses data efficiency and generalization in sequential recommenders by amortizing Bayesian-style inference through in-context learning, using synthetic priors to enable robust zero-shot transfer.

Method: Pretrain RecPFN on synthetic clickstream environments sampled from a broad structural causal prior; at inference, a lightweight decoder-only transformer conditions on a handful of domain sequences and predicts next items for queries in a single forward pass with no weight updates.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot performance across eight public benchmarks; remains competitive with supervised methods in low-compute and low-data regimes; robust to domain shift and deployment-efficient, outperforming strong zero-shot baselines that rely on large real-interaction corpora.

Conclusion: RecPFN offers a practical path toward generalizable, data-efficient recommenders, and enables richer priors, longer-context in-context learning, and multimodal extensions; code is publicly available.

Abstract: We introduce RecPFN, a prior-fitted network that brings in-context learning to sequential recommendation. RecPFN is pretrained entirely on synthetic clickstream environments sampled from a broad structural causal prior, enabling it to amortize Bayesian-style inference from a small support set. At inference, a lightweight decoder-only transformer conditions on a handful of domain sequences and produces next-item predictions for queries in a single forward pass, without any weight updates. Across eight public benchmarks, RecPFN achives state-of-the-art zero-shot performance while remaining strongly competitive with supervised methods in low-compute and low-data regimes. It is deployment-efficient and robust to domain shift, outperforming strong zero-shot baselines that rely on large real-interaction corpora. RecPFN provides a practical path toward generalizable, data-efficient recommenders and opens avenues for richer priors, longer-context ICL, and multimodal extensions. Code for training and evaluation is publicly available at https://github.com/SAP-samples/tabular-ai-recpfn/.

</details>


### [96] [Truncate Bad, Upweight Good: BoN-Style Distillation via Rank-Based Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19748)
*Yarin Bar,Yaniv Romano*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduce TUP, a truncate-and-upweight policy for distilling Best-of-N best-of-N-style selection into a single policy. It truncates the lower tail of in-pool rankings, upweights the upper tail with tunable sharpness, and uses offline training with binary cross-entropy based on shifted-truncated win-rates and distilled-to-reference log-likelihood ratios. Theoretically, lower-tail truncation can match any unknown oracle rank-reweighting under certain assumptions; empirically competitive with strong offline baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce reliance on brittle top-ranked rewards in inference-time selection by removing low-ranked completions and sharpening reweighting, while enabling offline training without continuous reward signals.

Method: TUP selects only the upper tail of in-pool rankings by truncating the lower tail; applies a tunable sharpness to reweight retained entries; provides a closed-form, prompt-independent normalization; trains offline via binary cross-entropy using shifted-truncated win-rates as soft labels and distilled-to-reference log-likelihood ratios as logits.

Result: Empirically competitive with strong offline alignment baselines on standard benchmarks.

Conclusion: Lower-tail truncation, paired with upweighting of the upper tail, provides formal and empirical benefits: it matches monotone rank-reweighting under certain conditions and yields a practical, robust, offline training objective.

Abstract: Inference-time selection methods, such as Best-of-N, improve generation by sampling a pool of candidates and selecting the top-ranked completion according to a reward model. Distillation seeks to amortize this procedure into a single policy by replacing raw rewards with in-pool ranks and learning a policy that upweights higher-ranked completions. However, existing rank-based policies typically use smooth full-support reweighting, so low-ranked completions receive less mass but remain in the target support. Although a sharper reweighting reduces lower-tail mass, it also increases reliance on brittle ranking at the top made by a single reward model. We propose TUP: a Truncate-bad, Upweight-good Policy that removes low-ranked completions from the support and reweights only the retained upper tail with a tunable sharpness. TUP admits a closed-form, prompt-independent normalization and can be trained fully offline via binary cross-entropy, using shifted-truncated win-rates as soft labels and distilled-to-reference log-likelihood ratios as logits. Theoretically, under certain assumptions, we show that for any unknown oracle reward, the best monotone rank-reweighting can be matched by a lower-tail truncation rule, providing formal support for removing the lower tail rather than merely downweighting it. Empirically, we show that TUP is competitive with strong offline alignment baselines.

</details>


### [97] [Credit Without Ground Truth: Auditing Step-Level Credit Assignment in LLM Agents Against Executed Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19760)
*Haiyue Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Auditing step-level credit signals against a causal ground truth in ALFWorld shows that common signals (LLM-judge scores, logprob ratios, model confidence) do not reliably identify causally important steps; causal contribution is sparse and model-dependent; outcome conditioning provides no causal information; a confidence-only router reduces judge cost but not accuracy; in a seven-arm training experiment, no arm outperforms the untrained policy, and apparent instrument signatures are explained by training dose. Evaluations must account for effective sample size; otherwise they measure dose, not credit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Question whether standard step-level credit signals genuinely capture causal contribution to outcomes in LLM decision processes, rather than correlating with fluency or dataset biases; propose auditing signals via counterfactual resampling to define a causal ground truth for step contribution.

Method: Audit step-level signals against causal ground truth by replaying in a single-agent environment (ALFWorld). At each decision point, resample the policy’s alternatives and roll forward to assess impact on the outcome. Compare signals (judge scores, outcome-conditioned logprob ratios, policy confidence) to the ground-truth causal contribution. Assess sparsity and measurability of causal effects; test a confidence-only router; run a seven-arm pre-registered training experiment to examine instrument-like signals under varying training dose.

Result: Causal contribution is sparse (30.5% of defined decision points show measurable effect). Measurability of counterfactuals is model-dependent (13.1% vs. 26.8%). Implicit credit echoes policy fluency (median rank correlation +0.75; +0.70 replication in a second family with corrected instrument). Outcome conditioning provides no causal information (partial correlation -0.004 for Qwen). Confidence-only routing recovers pivotal steps at chance yet reduces judge cost by 13.1% per turn (14.0% per trajectory). In a seven-arm study, no arm reliably outperforms the untrained policy; apparent instrument signature is explained by training dose (sparser credit → fewer examples; large variance in optimizer steps).

Conclusion: Common step-level credit signals do not robustly reflect causal contribution; evaluating signals requires matching effective sample size to avoid conflating dose with credit. Causal evaluation frameworks should be adopted to avoid misattributing credit to fluent or outcome-correlated signals; future work should develop methods that scale correctly with data size and quantify actual counterfactual impact.

Abstract: Audited against causal ground truth from executed replay in a single-agent tool environment (ALFWorld), none of the step-level credit signals used to train LLM agents -- LLM-judge scores, outcome-conditioned logprob ratios, or the policy's own confidence -- identifies which steps causally matter better than chance. Existing evaluations grade these signals against annotated step *correctness*; we audit them against step *contribution* -- what re-sampling the policy's own alternatives at each decision point and rolling forward actually changes about the outcome -- and the two come apart. The ground truth itself is structured: causal contribution is sparse (30.5% of decision points where ground truth is defined carry measurable effect), and measurability is model-dependent -- the fraction of points with no policy-supported counterfactual differs by a factor of two (13.1% vs. 26.8%) between two similar-scale policies. The failure mode is identifiable: implicit credit echoes the policy's fluency (median rank correlation +0.75, replicating at +0.70 in a second family under a corrected instrument), while conditioning on the outcome adds no causal information (partial correlation -0.004, Qwen). A confidence-only router recovers pivotal steps at chance level, but cuts judge cost by 13.1% per turn (14.0% per trajectory). In a seven-arm pre-registered training experiment, no arm reliably outperforms the untrained policy, and the checkpoints' apparent instrument signature is fully explained by training dose -- sparser credit retains fewer examples, an order-of-magnitude spread in optimizer steps -- not credit content. Comparisons of credit rules must therefore match effective sample size, or they measure dose, not credit.

</details>


### [98] [Finite-Horizon Input-Output Dynamics of Minibatch Perturbations in AdamW](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19762)
*Kang Liu,Suyan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdamW memory induces delayed effects; ISO modeling reveals how a localized gradient perturbation affects future losses via stored gradient information; exact multistep error decomposition and first-order finite-horizon accuracy; experiments validate; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify and understand how optimizer-state memory in AdamW impacts training beyond the observed update, using a finite-horizon input–state–output framework.

Method: Model AdamW as a finite-horizon ISO system with state including model parameters and first- and second-moment estimates; linearize joint dynamics to obtain a signed response operator that maps a localized gradient perturbation to future loss changes; derive an exact multistep error decomposition and prove first-order finite-horizon accuracy under local smoothness and controlled activation switching; provide experiments validating the mechanism and its memory effects; perform repeated-future analyses and compare to ISO predictions; release code.

Result: Derived a signed response operator that links gradient perturbations to future loss, elucidating how optimizer memory affects magnitude, timing, and sign of effects; established an exact multistep error decomposition and first-order finite-horizon accuracy; experiments validate the delayed influence and the predictive power of the ISO approximation; repeated-future analyses reveal substantive prospective structure. Code available at the linked repository.

Conclusion: The finite-horizon ISO framing clarifies how AdamW’s memory of past gradients shapes training dynamics, enabling principled analysis of delayed influence and offering a foundation for improved optimization understanding; results show partial recoverability of delayed effects from ISO approximations.

Abstract: A minibatch can influence training beyond the update at which it is observed because AdamW stores past gradient information in its optimizer states. We study this delayed effect through paired trajectories that differ only in one gradient update and share the same subsequent training sequence. We formulate AdamW as a finite-horizon input--state--output (ISO) system whose state contains the model parameters and first- and second-moment estimates. Linearizing the joint dynamics yields a signed response operator that maps a localized gradient perturbation to its future loss effects, revealing how optimizer memory shapes their magnitude, timing, and sign. We further derive an exact multistep error decomposition and establish first-order finite-horizon accuracy under local smoothness and controlled activation switching. Experiments validate the response mechanism and optimizer-state effects, while repeated-future analyses reveal substantial prospective structure in delayed influence that can be partially recovered from ISO approximations. Code is available at https://github.com/Kanyooo/Loss_ISO.

</details>


### [99] [Unsupervised Anomaly Detection Using Flow Matching on Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19801)
*Philip Konz,Tejaswini Medi,Margret Keuper*

Main category: cs.LG
