TL;DR: Anomaly scoring choice is critical for flow-matching-based unsupervised financial anomaly detection under contaminated training data; trajectory-based scores (Deviation, Reconstruction) can outperform the standard Decision score, making Forest-Flow competitive with or superior to TCCM in some scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial transaction logs are often unlabeled and contain contaminants, violating the clean-normal data assumption. Robust anomaly detection in this setting is essential, particularly for flow-matching models.

Method: Empirical comparison of Time-Conditioned Contraction Matching (TCCM) and Forest-Flow under training data contamination, evaluating multiple anomaly scoring functions (Decision, Deviation, Reconstruction) and assessing their impact on detection robustness and performance on financial anomaly detection tasks.

Result: The single-step Decision score is highly sensitive to contamination, whereas trajectory-based Deviation and Reconstruction scores provide more stable anomaly signals. With these scores, Forest-Flow is competitive with, and in some cases outperforms, TCCM under severe contamination.

Conclusion: Anomaly scoring critically influences the robustness of flow-matching methods in contaminated data. Trajectory-based scores substantially improve robustness, enabling Forest-Flow to match or surpass TCCM in financial anomaly detection with severe class imbalance.

Abstract: Financial anomaly detection often relies on large unlabeled transaction logs, where anomalous samples may already be present during training. Such training-set contamination violates the clean-normal data assumption underlying many anomaly detection methods. Although flow matching has demonstrated strong performance in generative modeling, its robustness in unsupervised tabular anomaly detection remains underexplored. In this work, we study flow-matching-based anomaly detection under contaminated training data by comparing Time-Conditioned Contraction Matching (TCCM) with Forest-Flow and evaluating multiple anomaly scoring functions. Our results show that the choice of anomaly score is critical. The original single-step Decision score used by TCCM is sensitive to contamination, whereas trajectory-based Deviation and Reconstruction scores provide more stable anomaly signals. With these scores, Forest-Flow becomes competitive with, and in some cases outperforms, TCCM. These findings highlight the importance of anomaly scoring for flow-matching methods in financial anomaly detection under severe class imbalance.

</details>


### [100] [MileGPO: Milestone Inference with Local Evidence for Graph-Based Policy Optimization of Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19803)
*Bo Qian,Yuting Wu,Shuang Zeng,Huaiyu Wan,Dalin Zhang,Jiqiang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MileGPO proposes process-level credit assignment for long-horizon RL by discovering milestones on on-policy rollouts and weighting them with reliability calibration and progress-contrastive checks, achieving SOTA on ALFWorld/WebShop without extra environment interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the sparse, final-reward supervision problem in long-horizon RL by extracting meaningful intermediate milestones that reveal progress, reducing miscrediting from purely final rewards.

Method: Three design components: (1) Milestone Discovery to identify candidate milestones from successful rollouts and recurring traps from failures; (2) Reliability-Calibrated Shaping (RCS) to weight milestones by outcome-based confidence; (3) Progress-Contrastive Calibration (PCC) to test whether a candidate reflects local progress and whether its incoming transition outperforms observed alternatives from the same state. The approach relies on grouped on-policy rollouts and does not require auxiliary models or extra environment interactions.

Result: Empirical results show state-of-the-art performance on ALFWorld and WebShop, with a small gap in-distribution to out-of-distribution on ALFWorld. Ablations indicate that reliability weighting, local progress, and same-state branch evidence meaningfully contribute to milestone discovery and resolve ambiguous intermediate credit.

Conclusion: MileGPO effectively leverages process-level milestones to improve credit assignment without additional data or models, and the combination of Milestone Discovery, RCS, and PCC yields robust performance and better interpretability of intermediate progress in long-horizon RL.

Abstract: Credit assignment is challenging in long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning, where supervision often comes only from final rewards. Existing methods refine trajectory-level signals into step-level credits through step grouping or graph-based advantage estimation, but can overlook meaningful intermediate milestones. We propose MileGPO (Milestone Inference with Local Evidence for Graph-Based Policy Optimization), which derives process-level credit from grouped on-policy rollouts through three designs. Milestone Discovery identifies candidate milestones on successful rollouts and recurring traps on failed ones. Reliability-Calibrated Shaping (RCS) weights these candidates by outcome-based confidence, strengthening reliable milestones and traps while down-weighting uncertain ones. Progress-Contrastive Calibration (PCC) further tests whether a candidate reflects local progress and whether its incoming ansition outperforms observed alternatives from the same state.MileGPO requires neither auxiliary models nor additional environment interaction. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show state-of-the-art performance and a small in-distribution to out-of-distribution gap on ALFWorld. Ablations and credit diagnostics indicate that reliability weighting, local progress, and same-state branch evidence complement milestone discovery and resolve ambiguous intermediate credit.

</details>


### [101] [Answer-Level Trust Selection for Physical Vision-Language Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19807)
*Rongyu Yu,Ke Niu,Fengxiang He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Answer-Level Trust Selection (ATS) for selective trust of individual VLM predictions in quantitative physical reasoning, without fine-tuning or internal access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks gauge overall accuracy; deployment requires per-answer trust signals since self-consistency and priors can mislead.

Method: Post-hoc, model-agnostic ATS aggregating eight interpretable diagnostic scores derived from repeated queries with controlled interventions; requires no access to model logits or fine-tuning.

Result: ATS identifies stable-but-wrong and prior-tracking predictions that repeated agreement alone misses; improves failure-mode detection but may reduce retention of correct predictions; evaluated on Qwen2.5-VL-7B across 20 backbones; code will be released.

Conclusion: ATS complements model-level evaluation by providing answer-level reliability for quantitative VLM predictions; broadly applicable, though there is a trade-off between precision and coverage.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) can estimate physical quantities such as duration, speed, and acceleration from visual observations, but existing benchmarks primarily assess overall model performance against annotated ground truth. In deployment, a key question is whether an individual prediction can be trusted when its ground truth is unavailable. Self-consistency alone may fail to capture important failure modes: a VLM may produce stable-but-wrong estimates or rely on textual priors rather than visual evidence. We formulate answer-level selective prediction for quantitative physical reasoning and propose Answer-Level Trust Selection (ATS), a post-hoc, model-agnostic framework for accepting or rejecting individual VLM predictions. ATS requires no fine-tuning, auxiliary verifier, or access to the model's internal logits. Instead, it aggregates eight interpretable behavioral diagnostic scores derived from repeated queries and controlled interventions into a unified trust score. We evaluate ATS in depth on Qwen2.5-VL-7B and across 20 VLM backbones, examining selective performance, diagnostic behavior, and targeted failure modes. Our results show that intervention-based diagnostics help identify stable-but-wrong and prior-tracking predictions that repeated agreement alone may miss. However, improved failure-case rejection can come at the cost of lower retention of correct predictions. ATS therefore complements model-level capability evaluation with answer-level reliability assessment for quantitative VLM predictions. Code will be released upon publication.

</details>


### [102] [FAR-DPO: Feasibility-Aware and Robust Direct Preference Optimization for Cyclic Peptide Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19808)
*Guofeng Zhang,Rong Han,Xiaoyu Wang,Zhiyun Li,Zongbo Han,Xiaohong Liu,Guangyu Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FAR-DPO is a feasibility-aware, robustness-focused framework that improves cyclic peptide design by guiding generative models toward structurally and biophysically feasible designs using feasibility-gated preferences and adaptive, group-wise difficulty weighting, boosting success rates and target-wise performance on CPSea LNR benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cyclic peptides have restricted design spaces due to coupled geometric/biophysical constraints and limited training data, making it hard for generative models to produce feasible designs with multi-objective quality; existing methods rely on zero-shot generation or post hoc filtering with low yields and poor trade-offs.

Method: FAR-DPO combines feasibility-aware within-target preference construction with difficulty-aware group-robust optimization in a direct preference optimization framework, enabling architecture-agnostic guidance toward feasible, high-quality cyclic peptide designs.

Result: On the CPSea LNR benchmark with a fixed generation budget, FAR-DPO raises overall success rate from 46.89% to 57.79% on PepGLAD and from 47.96% to 49.57% on PepFlow; improvements extend to the hardest target quartile and are accompanied by better best-per-target binding scores.

Conclusion: FAR-DPO effectively improves feasibility and target-wise robustness in cyclic peptide design, demonstrating architecture-agnostic adaptability and enhanced multi-objective control in challenging design spaces.

Abstract: Cyclic peptides are emerging as promising molecular scaffolds in drug discovery due to their high binding affinity and structural stability. However, extending generative models from linear to cyclic peptide design remains challenging, as cyclization sharply restricts the feasible design space through coupled geometric and biophysical constraints. Moreover, limited training data has led existing approaches to rely largely on zero-shot generation or post hoc filtering, resulting in low yields of feasible designs and limited control over multi-objective trade-offs. To address these limitations, we propose FAR-DPO (Feasibility-Aware and Robust Direct Preference Optimization), an architecture-agnostic framework that steers generative models toward structurally and biophysically feasible cyclic peptide designs, particularly for challenging targets. FAR-DPO integrates feasibility-aware preference construction with difficulty-aware group-robust optimization. Specifically, it constructs within-target preference pairs through feasibility-gated multi-objective dominance and adaptively reweights predefined difficulty groups according to their current preference losses. On the CPSea LNR benchmark, under a fixed generation budget, FAR-DPO increases overall success rate from 46.89% to 57.79% on PepGLAD and from 47.96% to 49.57% on PepFlow. These gains also extend to the hardest target quartile and are accompanied by more favorable best-per-target binding scores. Together, these results demonstrate FAR-DPO's effectiveness in improving feasibility and target-wise robustness.

</details>


### [103] [Adaptive Probabilistic Shielding by Learning MDPs for Safe Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19836)
*Astrid Horn Brorholt,Maris F. L. Galesloot,Nils Jansen,Kim Guldstrand Larsen,Christian Schilling*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive probabilistic shielding for RL with unknown transition probabilities: learn the MDP online and recompute the shield as estimates improve to balance safety and exploration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety in reinforcement learning when the MDP transition probabilities are not known a priori; existing shields assume known probabilities; need an adaptable shield that can be built from online learning.

Method: Assumes knowledge of the MDP transition graph but not the probabilities. The RL agent explores to estimate transition probabilities, and a shield is computed from these estimates. The shield can be conservative initially and becomes less so as estimates improve. Addresses when to recompute the shield and how to trade off exploration and safety. Empirical evaluation of several variants across multiple environments.

Result: The shield adapts over time, becoming less conservative as the model estimate improves. Multiple variants are empirically evaluated across different environments, demonstrating feasibility and practical safety-learning trade-offs.

Conclusion: Adaptive probabilistic shielding is viable and the shield improves in tandem with the learning agent. The approach raises questions about recomputation strategies and the balance between exploration and safety, with empirical results supporting practicality.

Abstract: Probabilistic shielding is a technique for safe reinforcement learning (RL). Typically, a static observer -- called the shield -- constrains the learning agent's actions to those for which acting safely remains feasible. Traditionally, the shield is computed from the transition probabilities of the underlying Markov decision process (MDP). Thus, this technique is not applicable when the MDP model is not given a priori, which, unfortunately, is the case in typical RL applications. In this paper, we study the problem of computing a shield in the setting where the transition graph of the MDP is known, but the transition probabilities are unknown. Our approach integrates probabilistic shielding with online model learning: as the RL agent explores the environment, we estimate the transition probabilities. From this estimate, we compute a shield. While the shield may be conservative initially, it adapts as the model estimate becomes more precise. Thus, the shield improves in tandem with the RL agent. This paradigm of adaptive probabilistic shielding raises a number of challenges, such as when to recompute the shield and how to balance between exploration and safety during learning. We empirically evaluate multiple variants of this paradigm across several environments.

</details>


### [104] [Inadvertent Context Leakage in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19857)
*Jaiden Fairoze,Neal Mangaokar,Kamalika Chaudhuri,Sanjam Garg,Saeed Mahloujifar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: In-context secrets inside a model's window can be covertly leaked via benign outputs; 2-digit secrets reconstructible with near-perfect accuracy, 4-digit secrets at 82% exact match; leakage scales with model capability; two practical attacks demonstrated: semantic predicate inference from outputs and SSN exfiltration via an RL agent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether the mere presence of secrets in a model's context causes hidden correlations that enable leakage in benign outputs, and whether an adversary can actively engineer prompts to amplify this leakage, all under black-box access.

Method: Empirical study across eight proprietary models. Evaluate reconstruction of secrets (2-digit and 4-digit) from outputs to ordinary prompts. Develop an adaptive, black-box attack. Demonstrate two practical attack pipelines: (i) a classifier inferring predicates about user memories from routine outputs, (ii) an RL-based adversary extracting full SSNs from a production-style agent.

Result: 2-digit secrets reconstructed with near-perfect accuracy; 4-digit secrets recovered with 82% exact match. Leakage increases with model capability; stronger instruction-following amplifies sensitivity. Demonstrated practical attacks: semantic predicate inference classifier and SSN extraction via RL agent.

Conclusion: Leakage appears as a byproduct of model capability rather than a patchable bug. Significant security implications for deploying AI agents handling sensitive data; defenses should focus on mitigating in-context leakage and restricting secret exposure, as well as auditing model prompts and outputs.

Abstract: For AI agents to be useful beyond simple chat, they must hold sensitive user context such as calendars, credentials, health records, and financial data. We study whether the mere presence of such secrets in a model's context window introduces hidden correlations into the model's benign outputs, allowing reconstruction even when the model correctly refuses direct extraction. We further study whether an adversary can actively engineer prompts that amplify this effect, using the model as a covert carrier to transmit secrets through seemingly innocuous text. In both cases, this limited leakage is exploited using a novel adaptive attack that assumes black-box access to the underlying model.
  In controlled experiments across eight proprietary models, we find that 2-digit in-context secrets are reconstructed with near-perfect accuracy and 4-digit secrets at 82\% exact match, all from outputs the model produces in response to ordinary, non-adversarial requests. We observe that more capable models leak more: stronger instruction-following amplifies sensitivity to in-context secrets, suggesting leakage is a byproduct of capability as opposed to a patchable bug. We show this leakage enables two practical attacks: (1) a trained classifier that infers semantic predicates about user memories (e.g., health conditions, financial events) from routine natural-language outputs, and (2) an RL-trained adversary that extracts full Social Security Numbers from a production-style agent.

</details>


### [105] [Online Test-Time Adaptation for Generalizable Dynamic Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19858)
*Jialun Zheng,Hanchen Yang,Jiannong Cao,Yankai Chen,Yuanjing Feng,Philip S. Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OTTA-DGAD is an online test-time adaptation method for generalizable dynamic graph anomaly detection. It builds evolving dynamic prototypes from temporal ego-graphs and uses a memory buffer to retain general patterns from source domains and new target patterns; an anomaly scorer uses prototypes to detect both general and domain-specific anomalies on streaming data; adapts by updating the memory with confident pseudo-labels and enriching each chunk with past representations, achieving state-of-the-art on 10 real-world datasets under test-then-adapt OTTA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two key issues: domain-agnostic patterns miss domain-specific evolving signals; and offline full-target-data assumption is impractical; propose online adaptation to handle sequential unlabeled data.

Method: Extract dynamic prototypes from temporal ego-graphs; maintain memory buffer that stores general patterns across source domains and target-domain patterns; anomaly scorer compares incoming edge representations with prototypes to label anomalies; update memory with reliable pseudo-labels via confidence-based detection; enrich target chunks with representations retained from previous chunks.

Result: Extensive experiments under strict OTTA settings show state-of-the-art performance on ten real-world datasets from diverse domains.

Conclusion: Demonstrates effectiveness of memory-based prototype adaptation for DGAD in online settings, capturing both general and domain-specific anomalies while mitigating information loss from sequential data.

Abstract: Generalizable dynamic graph anomaly detection (DGAD) enables pretrained detectors to identify anomalies in unseen target domains without costly retraining. However, existing methods often fail for two reasons. First, they mainly rely on domain-agnostic patterns and miss domain-specific patterns that keep evolving. Second, they assume access to the full target domain data, whereas in more practical online test-time adaptation settings, target data arrive sequentially in unlabeled chunks. To address these limitations, we formulate online test-time adaptation for generalizable DGAD and propose OTTA-DGAD. OTTA-DGAD first extracts dynamic prototypes, i.e., evolving representations of normal and anomalous patterns, from temporal ego-graphs and stores them in a memory buffer. The buffer selectively retains general patterns shared across the source domains used for pretraining while incorporating new patterns from the target domain. An anomaly scorer then compares incoming edge representations against these prototypes to identify both general and domain-specific anomalies. During adaptation, OTTA-DGAD updates the memory buffer using reliable pseudo-labels identified through confidence-based detection. It further enriches each target chunk with relevant representations retained from previous chunks, compensating for information loss resulting from the sequential arrival of data. Extensive experiments under strict test-then-adapt OTTA settings demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on ten real-world datasets from diverse domains.

</details>


### [106] [Separating Covariate Shift from Mechanism Change with Two Discriminators: CJSD, a Conditional Discrepancy with an Exact Covariate-Concept Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19885)
*Kentaro Oda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A principled, anytime-valid decision layer for streaming ensembles that decides reuse, spawn, or defer of expert models using one-sided sequential hypotheses on mechanism-level discrepancies, implemented via a restarted e-detector (bank of betting supermartingales) with logarithmic memory to ensure finite-time guarantees and multiplicity control; achieves zero false spawns/reuses in experiments and matches or surpasses prior heuristics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of statistically principled, finite-time-valid decision mechanisms for managing a pool of expert models in streaming data. The goal is to enable reuse, spawn, or defer decisions with guarantees while controlling multiplicity across potentially unbounded experts.

Method:  formulate reuse and spawn as one-sided sequential hypotheses on a conditional discrepancy with an indifference zone; define defer as the state where neither e-process accumulates sufficient evidence; prove finite-time anytime validity for an observable surrogate discrepancy of a predictable discriminator sequence and a corresponding population transfer. introduce a restarted e-detector: a bank of unwindowed betting supermartingales at geometrically spaced restart times, using O(log t) memory; budget allocation across restarts yields lifetime anytime validity and multiplicity control when experts are created in order.

Result: On synthetic streams (Electricity, Covertype, INSECTS), the instance-accounted restarted bank yields zero false spawns and zero false reuses after switches, matching or exceeding a retired windowed heuristic (INSECTS reoccurring accuracy 0.675). The deployed algorithm and the guaranteed algorithm become one and the same.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a memory-efficient, finite-time-valid decision mechanism for streaming ensembles, enabling principled reuse/spawn/defer decisions with strong empirical performance and exact equivalence to a previously deployed heuristic, while offering lifetime validity and multiplicity control across unbounded expert pools.

Abstract: Streaming systems that maintain a pool of expert models must repeatedly decide whether to reuse an existing expert for arriving data, spawn a new one, or defer. We present a decision layer that makes all three outcomes statistically meaningful. Reuse and spawn are posed as one-sided sequential hypotheses on a conditional (mechanism-level) discrepancy, separated by an indifference zone; defer is exactly the state in which neither betting e-process has accumulated sufficient evidence. We prove finite-time anytime validity for the observable surrogate discrepancy of a predictable discriminator sequence, and an unconditional one-sided transfer to the population quantity in which each side's slack is the excess risk of a single discriminator; an empirically observed downward-bias regularity makes the spawn side exactly conservative. Recency without sacrificing the guarantee is obtained by a restarted e-detector: a bank of unwindowed betting supermartingales at geometrically spaced restart times (O(log t) memory), with the error budget spent over restart instances, which preserves lifetime anytime validity; spending over expert-creation order likewise controls multiplicity for unboundedly many experts. On synthetic multi-concept streams, Electricity, Covertype, and the recurrence-heavy INSECTS benchmark, the instance-accounted restarted bank achieves zero false spawns and zero false reuses after switches and matches or exceeds the retired windowed heuristic (INSECTS-reoccurring accuracy 0.675), making the deployed algorithm and the guaranteed algorithm one and the same.

</details>


### [107] [Evidence Before Expansion: Reuse, Spawn, or Defer in Lifelong Expert Pools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19888)
*Kentaro Oda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A decision layer for streaming ensembles that uses one-sided sequential tests on a mechanism-level discrepancy to decide reuse, spawn, or defer; introduces a restarted e-detector with O(log t) memory to preserve recency; achieves finite-time anytime validity and zero false spawns/reuses on benchmark streams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide principled, finite-time statistical guarantees for dynamic reuse/creation decisions in streaming ensembles, addressing the lack of rigorous error control in heuristic approaches and enabling reliable handling of unbounded numbers of experts.

Method: Formulate reuse/spawn as one-sided sequential hypotheses around a conditional (mechanism-level) discrepancy; define defer as insufficient evidence for either action; prove finite-time anytime validity for an observable surrogate discrepancy via a predictable discriminator sequence; transfer to population quantity with slack equal to excess risk; use a restarted bank of betting supermartingales at geometrically spaced restart times to maintain recency with O(log t) memory and control multiplicity by spending across restarts.

Result: Theoretically, finite-time anytime validity and an unconditional one-sided transfer to population quantities are established; the spawn side is conservatively biased due to downward bias. Empirically, on Electricity, Covertype, and INSECTS benchmarks, the restarted bank achieves zero false spawns and reuses after switches, matching or exceeding the retired windowed heuristic (INSECTS reoccurring accuracy 0.675).

Conclusion: The proposed decision layer yields statistically meaningful, recency-preserving reuse/spawn/defer decisions for streaming expert pools, with finite-time guarantees and competitive empirical performance, effectively unifying deployed and guaranteed algorithms.

Abstract: Streaming systems that maintain a pool of expert models must repeatedly decide whether to reuse an existing expert for arriving data, spawn a new one, or defer. We present a decision layer that makes all three outcomes statistically meaningful. Reuse and spawn are posed as one-sided sequential hypotheses on a conditional (mechanism-level) discrepancy, separated by an indifference zone; defer is exactly the state in which neither betting e-process has accumulated sufficient evidence. We prove finite-time anytime validity for the observable surrogate discrepancy of a predictable discriminator sequence, and an unconditional one-sided transfer to the population quantity in which each side's slack is the excess risk of a single discriminator; an empirically observed downward-bias regularity makes the spawn side exactly conservative. Recency without sacrificing the guarantee is obtained by a restarted e-detector: a bank of unwindowed betting supermartingales at geometrically spaced restart times (O(log t) memory), with the error budget spent over restart instances, which preserves lifetime anytime validity; spending over expert-creation order likewise controls multiplicity for unboundedly many experts. On synthetic multi-concept streams, Electricity, Covertype, and the recurrence-heavy INSECTS benchmark, the instance-accounted restarted bank achieves zero false spawns and zero false reuses after switches and matches or exceeds the retired windowed heuristic (INSECTS-reoccurring accuracy 0.675), making the deployed algorithm and the guaranteed algorithm one and the same.

</details>


### [108] [Reliable Neural Collapse Approximation for Open-World Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19890)
*Jia-Qi Lin,Yuangang Pan,Chang-Dong Wang,Haizhang Zhang,Ivor W. Tsang,Joey Tianyi Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Reliable Neural Collapse approximation (ReNC) for Open-World Test-Time Adaptation uses neural collapse as a prior to treat pre-trained classifier weights as source prototypes, filters OOD samples via prototype similarity, and refines prototypes through neural collapse approximation to adapt to the target while preserving NC structure, achieving strong open-world TTA performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-world TTA faces label distribution shift and unknown classes; existing methods struggle with reliability under OODs. A neural collapse-based prior can provide a principled, stable foundation for adaptation by aligning target updates with class prototypes.

Method: 1) Use the pre-trained classifier weights as source prototypes. 2) For each target sample, measure similarity to prototypes to filter Out-Of-Distribution (OOD) samples before updating the model. 3) Apply a neural collapse approximation mechanism to refine the prototypes so they gradually adapt to the target domain while maintaining the neural collapse structure.

Result: Extensive experiments on several open-world benchmarks show state-of-the-art performance and better preservation of Neural Collapse–related properties in the target domain; ablations validate components. Code is released at the provided URL.

Conclusion: ReNC provides a principled NC-based framework for reliable Open-World TTA, enabling robust adaptation under label distribution shift and offering design insights for future models.

Abstract: Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) methods aim to bridge the domain gap between the source and target domains. However, traditional TTA methods become ineffective when the label distribution shift occurs, a challenge commonly referred to as an open-world scenario. In this paper, we introduce a new method named Reliable Neural Collapse approximation (ReNC) for Open-World Test-Time Adaptation (OWTTA). Specifically, we leverage neural collapse as a structural prior for reliable target-domain adaptation. Guided by this prior, we justify that the pre-trained classifier weights can serve as the prototypes of the source domain. By measuring the similarity between samples and prototypes, we filter out the Out-Of-Distribution~(OOD) samples for reliable updates. Furthermore, we propose a neural collapse approximation mechanism to refine these prototypes, ensuring they can gradually adapt to the target domain while maintaining the neural collapse structure. Extensive experiments on several open-world benchmarks demonstrate the superiority of the proposed method. Our empirical analysis suggests that ReNC better preserves NC-related properties in the target domain, providing useful evidence for explaining reliable OWTTA and offering new insights for model design. Code is available at https://github.com/JiaqiLin-AI/ReNC.

</details>


### [109] [PETA:Parameter-Efficient Test-Time Adaptation for Virtual Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19906)
*Jia-Qi Lin,Yinghua Yao,Chang-Dong Wang,Yew-Soon Ong,Yuangang Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PETA enables test-time, parameter-efficient pocket-specific adaptation for pretrained virtual screening models by updating only LayerNorm, generating pocket-specific negatives via diffusion and filtering, and using embedding-space mixup with a retrieved ligand to sharpen ranking, achieving better performance than pretrained or fully retrained models with ~0.03% of parameters updated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the inefficiency of full retraining for pocket-specific customization; leverage test-time adaptation to tailor embeddings to individual pockets without large computational cost.

Method: Test-time adaptation updating only LayerNorm parameters. Generate pocket-specific negatives via molecular diffusion and chemical validity filtering. Align with reference ligand via embedding-space mixup to create harder ranking tasks. Use a ranking objective that emphasizes suppressing high-scoring invalid candidates.

Result: Across diverse benchmarks, PETA outperforms both pretrained and fully retrained baselines, while updating only ~0.03% of the model parameters.

Conclusion: Lightweight, pocket-specific test-time adaptation is effective for improving virtual screening performance without requiring full re-training.

Abstract: Accurately ranking active ligands for a target protein pocket from massive chemical libraries remains a central challenge in virtual screening. DrugCLIP and its recent extensions substantially accelerate this process by encoding protein pockets and molecules into a shared embedding space. Despite this progress, further performance improvements typically require retraining the entire model, incurring substantial computational overhead and making target-specific customization inefficient. In this work, we formulate the specialization of pretrained virtual screening models to individual pockets as a test-time adaptation problem and propose PETA, a parameter-efficient framework that directly adapts pretrained model at test time. Given a target pocket, PETA constructs pocket-specific negatives through molecular diffusion and chemical validity filtering, and further moves them toward the reference ligand retrieved from structural databases via embedding-space mixup to create more challenging ranking tasks. A ranking objective then places greater emphasis on suppressing high-scoring invalid candidates that could contaminate the top-ranked screening results, providing structured supervision for lightweight adaptation. Experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate that this lightweight, pocket-specific adaptation outperforms both pretrained and fully retrained baselines while updating only the LayerNorm parameters, which account for approximately $0.03\%$ of the full model.

</details>


### [110] [Multi-Source Wasserstein Distributionally Robust Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19914)
*Chuansen Peng,Yifan Xia,Jinshan Zhong,Xiaojing Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MS-WDRO fuses heterogeneous sources for graph learning via a weighted Wasserstein barycenter and distributionally robust optimization; provides finite-sample guarantees and an end-to-end, differentiable calibration framework; shows improved graph recovery, sample efficiency, and diagnostic utility on synthetic data and ABIDE I.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Target-domain samples are scarce while heterogeneous sources are abundant; naive Euclidean averaging collapses distinct geometries as source divergence grows. Wasserstein-based fusion preserves each source's geometry and distribution.

Method: Compute a weighted Wasserstein barycenter across sources as the nominal distribution; surround it with an ambiguity ball to hedge residual uncertainty; minimize worst-case risk to obtain a robust regularized Laplacian estimator solved via a provably convergent ADMM; derive non-asymptotic guarantees (finite-sample barycenter concentration, pooling bias lower bound, out-of-sample excess risk at parametric rate with log source-count dependence); unroll the solver into a differentiable architecture for end-to-end hyperparameter calibration; validate on synthetic benchmarks and the multi-site ABIDE I neuroimaging dataset.

Result: The approach yields a robust graph learning method with provable guarantees and practical performance gains; it consistently outperforms seven baselines in graph recovery, sample efficiency, and downstream diagnostic utility, with the largest gains in sample-scarce settings.

Conclusion: MS-WDRO provides a principled, scalable framework for multi-source, geometry-preserving graph learning under distributional uncertainty, offering theoretical guarantees and empirical advantages; end-to-end differentiable calibration enhances adaptability beyond cross-validation.

Abstract: Network topology inference from graph signals is central to graph signal processing with applications in neuroscience, sensor, and social networks. In practice, target-domain samples are scarce while heterogeneous source-domain data are abundant. Fusing these sources is challenging: Euclidean averaging works for homogeneous sources but degrades sharply as inter-source divergence grows, collapsing distinct geometries into an inflated, biased consensus. We exploit the Wasserstein metric's distribution-preserving properties to counter heterogeneity while preserving each source's intrinsic geometry. We propose MS-WDRO, a multi-source Wasserstein distributionally robust graph learning framework that fuses heterogeneous sources via their weighted Wasserstein barycenter, a geometrically principled nominal distribution, then builds an ambiguity ball around it to hedge residual uncertainty. Minimizing worst-case risk yields a tractable regularized Laplacian estimator solved efficiently via a provably convergent ADMM scheme. We establish non-asymptotic guarantees: a finite-sample concentration bound for the empirical barycenter, a pooling bias lower bound proving naive aggregation is suboptimal, and an out-of-sample excess risk bound decaying at a parametric rate with only logarithmic dependence on source count. To calibrate hyperparameters governing robustness, sparsity, and source fusion, we unroll the solver into a differentiable architecture trained end-to-end, achieving data-adaptive calibration beyond cross-validation while retaining interpretability. Experiments on synthetic benchmarks and the multi-site ABIDE~I neuroimaging dataset show MS-WDRO consistently outperforms seven baselines in graph recovery, sample efficiency, and downstream diagnostic utility, with the largest gains in the sample-scarce regime.

</details>


### [111] [Auditing Recorded Predictive Lead Service-Line Classifications Against Physical Verification: A Statewide Study of New York](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19922)
*Muhammad Sarmad Sohail*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper assesses NYC and NY state use of predictive models to classify service-line material under the Lead and Copper Rule Revisions, finding substantial misclassification risk, limited verification, and policy reliability concerns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate reliability and policy implications of substituting model-based classifications for physical verification in regulatory contexts; quantify misclassification and data quality issues across New York localities.

Method: Screen 153 New York localities that classified at least 100 addresses with a model; compare model-recorded materials to physical verification; analyze NYC boroughs vs rest; compute counts, variances, and 95% upper bounds; distinguish 'Unknown', 'Known Other', and model-driven classifications; assess sampling gaps and potential data copying; apply era-aware estimates.

Result: Among 75 of 153 localities (49%), 125,990 addresses (57%) have a single value; zero variance often coincides with corroboration or insufficient data. In NYC, 43,215 addresses are model-based with material recorded as 'Known Other'; 121,779 'Unknown'; 1,880 already excavated; lead on 120,692. Model bucket has zero counts; 95% upper bound for lead rate is 0.85%. Outside NYC, 12.21% of 176,888 addresses use model-derived lead or 'Unknown but could be lead'; era-adjusted estimates place true lead lines at 1,150–1,450. Additional findings: 7,782 addresses in pre-1940 buildings; archived 2025 snapshot shows public-side determinations were copied from a customer-side model output.

Conclusion: Model-based determinations show limited agreement with physical verification and may underreport lead prevalence; substantial data quality and transparency issues exist. Relying solely on predictive models for regulatory decisions is risky; recommends improved verification, documentation, and cautious policy use with enhanced data practices.

Abstract: Under the US Lead and Copper Rule Revisions, a utility may determine a service line's material with a predictive model instead of inspecting it. New York State publishes, per address, which method was used. Almost no address carries both a model classification and a physical verification, so the check is between populations within a utility rather than paired addresses. We screen all 153 New York localities that classified at least 100 addresses this way. Seventy-five (49%), covering 125,990 addresses or 57% of those screened, record one value. Zero variance alone is not misconduct: 68 of the 75 match their own verification or have too little to test. Seven are contradicted by their own crews, six beyond any sampling explanation. Five are boroughs of New York City, which file as one system; one is East Rochester, 550 km away. New York City is the largest case: a predictive model is the recorded basis for 43,215 addresses, and on all of them the recorded material is "Known Other". The city records "Unknown" on 121,779 addresses, 1,880 already excavated, and lead on 120,692. In the model bucket both counts are zero, and the 95% upper bound on the rate is 0.0085%. Across the rest of New York the same method records lead or the hedge "Unknown but could be lead" on 12.21% of 176,888 addresses, a comparison whose weaknesses we report. The model-cleared population is newer, median year built 1984 against 1930, and construction era accounts for about a third of the gap and not the rest: holding era fixed, records-based classification finds lead at 4.3-31.9%, physical verification at 1.5-14.5%, the model in no era. Six era-aware estimators place the expected lead lines among them at 1,150-1,450. Two findings need no comparison: 7,782 of these addresses are in pre-1940 buildings, and the archived 2025 snapshot shows the public-side determination was copied from a customer-side model output.

</details>


### [112] [G-MARK: Grounded Multi-Agent Reasoning for Cooperative Driving via Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19964)
*Bhavya Gupta,Onat Gungor,Tajana Rosing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Grounded multi-agent reasoning with provenance-aware knowledge graphs (G-MARK) for cooperative object-centric observations in autonomous driving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Partial observability and occlusion in autonomous driving; current methods obscure provenance by compressing evidence into latent features, hindering traceability of observations and planning decisions.

Method: Construct provenance-aware knowledge graphs from cooperative object-centric observations, encoding object hypotheses, source attribution, ego/partner visibility, uncertainty, conflicts, spatial relations, and planning context; derive shared KG-based features for lightweight task heads enabling object reasoning, motion prediction, control selection, and trajectory forecasting.

Result: Occlusion reasoning accuracy improves by 42.2%; control-selection error reduced by 13.1%; trajectory-planning accuracy comparable to baselines; communication payload reduced by 25.6x; code released.

Conclusion: Provenance-aware, grounded reasoning via knowledge graphs enhances multi-agent cooperation under partial observability, offering accurate reasoning and efficient communication.

Abstract: Autonomous driving systems must operate under partial observability, where safety-critical objects may be occluded or visible only to neighboring connected vehicles. Vehicle-to-vehicle cooperation can reduce this uncertainty, but existing cooperative driving methods often compress multi-agent evidence into latent features or hidden multimodal states. As a result, they obscure which agent observed each object, whether the object is visible to the ego vehicle, and how conflicting evidence affects downstream decisions. We propose G-MARK, a grounded multi-agent reasoning framework that converts cooperative object-centric observations into explicit provenance-aware knowledge graphs (KGs). The resulting KGs preserve object hypotheses together with their source attribution, ego-versus-partner visibility, uncertainty, conflicts, spatial relations, and planning-relevant context. G-MARK then derives a shared feature representation from these KGs, enabling lightweight task heads to support object reasoning, motion prediction, control selection, and trajectory forecasting. Compared with the state-of-the-art baseline, GMARK improves occlusion reasoning accuracy by 42.2%, reduces control-selection error by 13.1%, and achieves comparable trajectory-planning accuracy with a 25.6x smaller structured communication payload. Our code is available at https://github.com/bhavyagupta98/g-mark.

</details>


### [113] [Green BOA: Determining the environmental break-even point for ML-based data compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19994)
*Caterina Doglioni,Akshat Gupta,Thomas Elliott,Hanzila Hussain,Sanjiban Sengupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Assesses the environmental break-even of ML-based lossless data compression by comparing CO2 from training/inference with CO2 savings from reduced storage, using two Manchester internship projects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify the environmental trade-off of applying machine-learning-driven compression to data storage, to determine when it is beneficial overall.

Method: Estimate carbon footprints of ML training and inference infrastructure and compare them to the carbon savings from smaller storage requirements; analyze break-even conditions for an ML-based lossless compressor.

Result: Preliminary analysis suggests that break-even is sensitive to workload, data size, compression ratio, hardware efficiency, and energy mix; scenarios exist where training/inference CO2 dominates or storage savings offset it.

Conclusion: Environmental viability of ML-based data compression is contingent on specific use-cases; careful consideration of compute lifecycle and storage costs is required to determine when such ML approaches are beneficial.

Abstract: We summarise the outcome of two summer internship projects based at the University of Manchester, focused on the break-even point in terms of environmental sustainability for ML-based data compression algorithms. Using the example of a ML-based lossless compression algorithm, we compare estimates for the carbon-equivalent of the infrastructure needed for ML training and inference with the carbon-equivalent savings from reduced disk storage requirements, and discuss their break-even point.

</details>


### [114] [Scale-Aware Pretraining of Time Series Foundation Models via Multi-Patch Token Alignment and Hybrid Masking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20005)
*Taihua Chen,Xiang Ma,Yixin Zhang,Tailin Zhan,Manyu Sun,Lizhen Cui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SATS introduces scale-aware token alignment and a hybrid masking strategy for time-series pretraining across heterogeneous sampling frequencies, achieving state-of-the-art performance and significantly improved efficiency on LSTF benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretraining time-series foundation models across datasets with different sampling rates is challenging because fixed patch sizes or dataset-specific architectures cause fragmented representations and poor cross-scale transfer.

Method: SATS treats patch size as explicit scale, employing a scale-aware token alignment with a contrastive-inspired regularizer to align representations across scales while preserving modeling capacity. It also uses a hybrid masking strategy that combines random and contiguous masking to capture multi-scale temporal structures.

Result: On LSTF benchmarks, SATS achieves a 9.2% improvement in MSE and an 8.3% gain in GIFT-Eval MASE over competitive baselines, delivering state-of-the-art performance and a 65.6% increase in model efficiency compared to advanced baselines.

Conclusion: SATS provides an effective, scalable solution for pretraining time-series models across heterogeneous data, enabling unified representations and improved efficiency across scales.

Abstract: Pretraining time series foundation models across heterogeneous datasets necessitates effective handling of varying sampling frequencies. Current methods either employ dataset-specific patch sizes and separate FFNs, leading to fragmented representations, or enforce a fixed patch size that neglects inherent temporal variations. To address this, we propose SATS, featuring a scale-aware token alignment mechanism that treats patch size as an explicit notion of scale. By incorporating a contrastive-inspired alignment regularizer, SATS aligns representation spaces across scales while preserving distinct modeling capacities. Furthermore, a hybrid masking strategy combining random and contiguous masking is introduced to capture multi-scale temporal structures. Experimental results on LSTF benchmarks demonstrate that SATS achieves a 9.2% improvement in MSE and an 8.3% gain in GIFT-Eval MASE compared to competitive baselines. Notably, SATS consistently delivers SOTA performance while achieving a 65.6% increase in model efficiency over advanced baselines, highlighting its effectiveness and scalability in time series pretraining.

</details>


### [115] [Systematic Evaluation of TabPFN-TS for Zero-Shot Probabilistic Heat Load Forecasting in District Heating Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20024)
*Ben Spoek,Karim K. Ben Hicham,Kai Derzsi,Philipp Althaus,Alexander Mitsos,Dirk Müller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TabPFN-TS offers competitive probabilistic heat-load forecasting for district heating using synthetic pretraining, achieving near-competitiveness with Chronos-2. An hourly 24-hour horizon with a 12-week rolling context and ambient temperature is the preferred setup; longer contexts don’t help. Chronos-2 often has better full-year accuracy, but TabPFN-TS provides better calibration. A hybrid Multi-Resolution Residual-Correction Forecaster is proposed to improve longer-horizon accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the burden of retraining forecasting models when district heating networks change, by evaluating zero-shot time-series foundation models (TabPFN-TS) that adapt at inference and compare against time-series foundations and traditional ML baselines for probabilistic heat-load forecasting.

Method: Systematic empirical evaluation of TabPFN-TS against time-series foundation models and trained ML baselines across covariates, context length, temporal resolution, and prediction horizon. Validation over a full year, transfer test on a second network, with ablations to identify the best configuration. Comparison with Chronos-2 for deterministic accuracy and calibration metrics; development of a diagnostic leading to a hybrid forecaster.

Result: Best configuration identified: hourly 24-hour forecast with a 12-week rolling context and ambient temperature as a key covariate. Longer context windows do not improve accuracy. TabPFN-TS achieves CVRMSE of 13.06% vs Chronos-2 12.48% (deterministic accuracy) and is within the critical-difference threshold in daily ranking; Chronos-2 has lower full-year error but TabPFN-TS shows better empirical calibration. The findings motivate a Multi-Resolution Residual-Correction Forecaster combining a low-frequency Base Forecaster with a short-horizon Residual Forecaster to enhance longer-horizon planning.

Conclusion: Zero-shot TabPFN-TS is a viable alternative to trained baselines for probabilistic heat-load forecasting in district heating networks, offering competitive accuracy and superior calibration. However, a hybrid, multi-resolution approach is proposed to improve longer-horizon accuracy and planning.

Abstract: District heating energy hubs require reliable heat load forecasts for efficient operational scheduling. Conventional forecasting workflows train system-specific models on historical data, which can become burdensome when networks change through new consumers, retrofits, or changing operating regimes. Zero-shot time-series foundation models and in-context forecasting offer a promising alternative: they can adapt at inference time from recent observations rather than by repeated retraining. This study systematically evaluates TabPFN-TS against time-series foundation models and trained machine-learning baselines for probabilistic heat load forecasting in district heating networks. Unlike foundation models pretrained on large collections of real time series, TabPFN-TS relies on synthetic pretraining data, which avoids direct pretraining-test overlap but raises the question of whether the learned prior captures district heating dynamics. We analyze covariate choice, context length, temporal resolution, and prediction horizon on representative operating weeks, validate the selected configuration over a full year, and test transferability on a second network. The results identify hourly 24-hour forecasting with a 12-week rolling context and ambient temperature as a parsimonious high-performing configuration; longer context windows do not improve accuracy. TabPFN-TS remains close to Chronos-2 in deterministic accuracy, reaching CVRMSE values of 13.06% versus 12.48% on the main dataset, and lies within the critical-difference threshold in the daily-rank comparison. Although Chronos-2 achieves the lowest aggregate full-year error, TabPFN-TS shows better empirical calibration. Finally, the diagnostic findings motivate a Multi-Resolution Residual-Correction Forecaster that combines a low-frequency Base Forecaster with a short-horizon Residual Forecaster to improve longer-horizon planning accuracy.

</details>


### [116] [CLaST: Context-aware Contrastive VAE for Probabilistic Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20025)
*Alexander Marusov,Dmitry Anikin,Petr Sokerin,Vitaliy Pozdnyakov,Ilya Kuleshov,Alexey Zaytsev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CLaST is a contrastive VAE for probabilistic multivariate time series forecasting that preserves temporal structure via a contrastive loss, outperforming baselines across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Probabilistic forecasts must capture internal temporal dependencies; existing generative models yield limited expressive latent representations.

Method: Introduce CLaST, a VAE-based framework with a contrastive loss that enforces contextual similarity in embeddings for multivariate time series.

Result: Measured on nine benchmarks, CLaST outperforms strong baselines; short-term gains: CRPS up to 16.4%, NMAE up to 14.4%; long-term gains: CRPS up to 48.6%, NMAE up to 25.1%.

Conclusion: CLaST provides improved probabilistic forecasting, especially for long-horizon predictions, by learning context-preserving embeddings.

Abstract: Probabilistic forecasting models are widely used for time series forecasting in domains such as energy systems, finance, medicine, and transportation. In recent years, deep generative models have shown strong results on probabilistic forecasting, yet many conventional approaches struggle to capture internal temporal dependencies, leading to latent representations with limited expressive power. To address this limitation, we propose \textit{CLaST}, a VAE framework for probabilistic multivariate time series forecasting. Unlike existing generative models, CLaST learns embeddings that preserve contextual similarity between observations through our contrastive loss function. Experiments across nine widely adopted benchmarks demonstrate that CLaST consistently surpasses strong baseline methods. In short-term forecasting tasks, our approach achieves improvements of up to $16.4\%$ in CRPS and $14.4\%$ in NMAE over the second-best method. Furthermore, in long-term prediction CLaST attains superior overall performance, exceeding the second-best method by up to $48.6\%$ and $25.1\%$ in CRPS and NMAE, respectively.

</details>


### [117] [An Inclusive and Lightweight Approach to Federated Continual Learning for Cultural Heritage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20038)
*Ioannis Theologitis,Debin Meng,Stylianos Eleftheriadis,Vasileios Lolis,Konstantinos Votis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedCurv-DR is a lightweight regularisation-based Federated Continual Learning method for cultural heritage image classification that reduces forgetting and balances performance, fairness, and energy efficiency by aggregating parameter importance across clients and updating at fixed intervals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cultural heritage data are distributed across institutions, restricted by ownership/access, and evolve over time. Federated Continual Learning is well-suited to learn from distributed, sequential data without sharing raw data.

Method: FedCurv-DR accumulates parameter-importance estimates across clients and experiences and updates them only at fixed intervals to minimize communication and computation overhead, using a regularisation-based approach to protect learned knowledge.

Result: Evaluated on the WikiArt image dataset for genre classification with evolving styles, reporting performance, energy, and fairness metrics. FedCurv-DR reduces forgetting and balances performance, fairness, and energy efficiency.

Conclusion: FedCurv-DR contributes to sustainable AI in cultural heritage by enabling effective federated continual learning with lower communication/computation cost while maintaining performance and fairness.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence can support cultural heritage and digital humanities through large-scale retrieval and analysis of digitized collections. However, cultural heritage data are often distributed across institutions, constrained by ownership and access restrictions, and continuously evolving over time. Federated Continual Learning (FCL) is well suited to this setting, as it enables models to learn from distributed and sequential data without sharing raw collections. In this paper, we propose FedCurv-DR, a lightweight, regularisation-based FCL strategy. The method accumulates parameter-importance estimates across clients and experiences to protect learned knowledge, while updating them only at fixed intervals to minimize communication and computation overhead. We evaluate FedCurv-DR in a continual learning scenario using the WikiArt image dataset for genre classification with evolving styles, reporting performance, energy, and fairness metrics. Our results show that FedCurv- DR reduces forgetting and balances performance, fairness, and energy efficiency for sustainable AI in cultural heritage.

</details>


### [118] [End-to-end Early Classification of Time Series in Non-Stationary Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20044)
*Aurélien Renault,Alexis Bondu,Antoine Cornuéjols,Vincent Lemaire*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A non-stationary early-time series classifier using end-to-end RL (DQeND) that jointly learns representation, classification, and triggering, outperforming separable baselines across drift scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitations of separable, stationary-assuming ECTS methods under concept drift and to improve robustness in evolving environments.

Method: Propose DQeND, a unified reinforcement-learning architecture that simultaneously learns representation, classification, and triggering decisions; compare with state-of-the-art separable baselines across controlled drifting scenarios; include ablations.

Result: DQeND demonstrates strong robustness across various non-stationary drifts and consistently outperforms separable baselines; ablations show joint representation and decision updates are essential for gains.

Conclusion: End-to-end learning can enhance adaptation of ECTS to non-stationary environments and motivates exploring non-separable designs and alternative RL-based approaches.

Abstract: Early Classification of Time Series (ECTS) requires making accurate decisions as early as possible in inherently online and evolving environments. Yet, most existing methods assume stationarity and rely on separable designs, where classification and triggering are optimized independently, an assumption that fundamentally limits their adaptability under drift. In this work, we challenge this paradigm and study ECTS under non-stationary conditions. We provide the first systematic comparison between separable and end-to-end approaches across controlled drifting scenarios. Building on Reinforcement Learning, we introduce DQeND, a unified architecture that jointly learns representation, classification, and triggering decisions, while remaining directly comparable to state-of-the-art separable baselines. Across a wide range of drifts, DQeND demonstrates strong robustness across various non-stationary scenarios, consistently outperforming separable baselines. An ablation study further highlights that jointly updating representation and decision modules is critical to these gains. Overall, our results indicate that end-to-end learning can offer improved adaptation capabilities for ECTS in dynamic environments, and motivate further investigation of alternatives to separable designs.

</details>


### [119] [DecoVAE: a Lightweight Interpretable Trend-Seasonal VAE Framework for Efficient Probabilistic Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20052)
*Alexander Marusov,Dmitry Anikin,Alexey Zaytsev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DecoVAE is a lightweight, interpretable VAE for probabilistic time-series forecasting that decomposes series into trend and seasonal components using domain-specific biases; it combines a Hodrick-Prescott-like differential regularizer for the trend with a frequency-domain complex Gaussian VAE for seasonality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address challenges in probabilistic forecasting due to coupling of trend and seasonal dynamics, limited interpretability, and high memory/runtime costs. Introduces inductive biases to separately model trend and seasonal patterns.

Method: A two-stream VAE framework: (1) a trend stream enforcing smooth latent trajectories via a differential regularizer akin to the Hodrick-Prescott filter; (2) a seasonal stream operating in the frequency domain with a complex Gaussian VAE to capture amplitude and phase of periodic components. The model is lightweight and aims to be interpretable through explicit decomposition.

Result: Across seven real-world benchmarks, DecoVAE outperforms strong baselines, achieving up to 14.96% CRPS and 23.30% NMAE improvements for short-term forecasts, and up to 52.68% CRPS and 26.51% NMAE improvements for long-term horizons. It also dramatically reduces model size (up to 93% fewer weights) and speeds up computation (up to 74% faster) relative to the second-best method.

Conclusion: DecoVAE delivers consistent accuracy gains with substantial efficiency and interpretability, by explicitly decomposing time series into trend and seasonal components using domain-informed inductive biases. Potential limitations include reliance on the chosen biases and applicability to diverse non-stationary patterns; future work could explore broader validations, ablations, and extensions to multivariate or nonstationary contexts.

Abstract: Probabilistic time series forecasting remains challenging, largely because modeling distinct trend and seasonal dynamics requires specialized approaches. Existing methods often fail to capture the unique inner properties of these components, lack interpretability, or suffer from heavy memory and runtime overhead. To address these limitations, we propose DecoVAE, a lightweight interpretable trend-seasonal VAE framework that explicitly decomposes time series into trend and seasonal components by applying domain-specific inductive biases. The trend stream enforces structural smoothness using a differential regularizer on the latent trajectory, analogous to the Hodrick-Prescott filter. Concurrently, the seasonal stream operates in the frequency domain via a complex Gaussian VAE, natively capturing the amplitude and phase of periodic patterns. Extensive evaluations across seven real-world benchmarks show that DecoVAE consistently outperforms strong baselines. It achieves reductions of up to 14.96\% in CRPS and 23.30\% in NMAE for short-term forecasting, and up to 52.68\% and 26.51\% for long-term horizons. Crucially, DecoVAE yields these accuracy gains while remaining highly efficient, reducing model weight by up to 93\% and accelerating speed by up to 74\% compared to the second-best method.

</details>


### [120] [Let's Scale Step by Step: Compute-Efficient Hyperparameter Transfer for Large-Scale Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20061)
*Nayeon Kim,Hojin Lee,Yunju Bak,Jaesun Park,Boseop Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two-step, compute-efficient hyperparameter transfer for MoE models: (1) transfer learning-rate across width scaling using μP adaptation with MLA and Muon optimizer; (2) extrapolate LR to trillion-token horizons via a token-budget scaling law using small proxy models. Validation on a 155B/17B-active-parameter foundation model shows high fidelity (R^2=0.95) in predicting optimal LR, enabling stable large-scale training with minimal ablations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MoE models offer large capacity at modest compute but hyperparameter tuning, especially learning rate, becomes prohibitively expensive at extreme scales. A principled, compute-efficient method to transfer optimal learning-rate settings across model widths and token budgets can dramatically reduce training costs and enable scalable pretraining of very large MoEs.

Method: 1) Adapt Maximal Update Parameterization (μP) to MoE architectures that use Multi-head Latent Attention (MLA) with the Muon optimizer, demonstrating consistent transfer of optimal learning rates across width-scaled variants. 2) Establish a predictive scaling law along the token dimension: perform linear regression on optimal LR values obtained from small proxy models trained under limited budgets and extrapolate to trillion-token horizons. 3) Validate by pretraining a large foundation model (155B total, 17B active parameters) and show stable training/evaluation with predicted LR settings.

Result: Learning-rate optima transfer reliably across width scaling under μP adaptation for MoE with MLA and Muon. The token-budget scaling law, learned via linear regression on small proxies, accurately predicts the LR for ~10 trillion tokens (R^2=0.95). Proxy-based LR selection yields stable large-scale training with minimal ablations.

Conclusion: Proxy training on small MoE models suffices to determine near-optimal learning rates for very large MoE pretraining. The two-step transfer—width-based μP adaptation and token-budget extrapolation—enables efficient, scalable hyperparameter selection validated by successful pretraining of a 155B model.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures significantly expand model capacity without a proportional increase in computational cost. However, optimizing their hyperparameters---particularly the learning rate---at extreme scales of both model size and token budget via sweeping remains computationally prohibitive. In this paper, we propose a compute-efficient, two-step hyperparameter transfer framework that estimates optimal learning rates for training large MoE models by transferring them across scaling model widths, and subsequently extrapolating to trillion-token horizons. First, we formulate a Maximal Update Parameterization ($μ$P) adaptation for MoE architectures utilizing Multi-head Latent Attention (MLA) and the Muon optimizer, demonstrating that optimal learning rates transfer consistently across width-scaled models. Second, we extend this transferability along the token dimension by establishing a predictive scaling law. By applying linear regression to the optimal values derived from small proxy models on limited budgets, we successfully extrapolate the ideal learning rate to massive training horizons (e.g., 10 trillion tokens) with high fidelity ($R^2=0.95$). Consequently, this indicates that proxy training on small models is sufficient to determine the optimal learning rate for the extensive training of large-scale MoEs. We apply the proposed methodology to pretrain our foundation model (155B total, 17B active parameters) from scratch, and the stable training and evaluation results validate that optimal configurations for full-scale target models can be accurately predicted with minimal ablation costs.

</details>


### [121] [Orthogonal JEPA: Factorized Predictive States for Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20065)
*Taoyong Cui,Pheng Ann Heng,Wanli Ouyang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces orthogonal predictive factorization for joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) to decompose targets into independent components with dedicated predictors, enabling richer, disentangled latent states for robust forecasting and planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard JEPAs rely on a single target embedding, which can allocate excessive capacity to dominant signals and provide weak gradients for secondary predictive structure; a factorized, orthogonal approach can yield more robust, interpretable latent representations.

Method: Learn basis matrices to factorize each target state into multiple components; each component has a dedicated prediction branch from a shared context. Predictive regression preserves factor magnitudes; enforce orthogonality to avoid redundant directions; apply factor-activity regularization to maintain variation; online variance regularization prevents encoder collapse. Predicted components are synthesized into a complete latent state usable by readouts, decoders, planners, or rollouts. The approach applies to temporal futures, spatially hidden targets, or other partial observations.

Result: Empirical evaluation across controlled vision, single-cell transcriptomics, longitudinal health records, continuous control, and molecular dynamics shows improvements in representation quality, forecasting accuracy, planning capability, and long-horizon stability.

Conclusion: Orthogonal predictive factorization yields a scalable, robust latent world-modeling framework that enhances JEPAs by disentangling predictive targets into orthogonal components, with broad applicability and improved downstream performance.

Abstract: World models construct latent states that support prediction, planning, and reasoning about an underlying system. Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) offer a direct way to learn such states by predicting targets in representation space instead of reconstructing every detail of the observation. Standard JEPAs, however, organize all predictable content through one target embedding and one prediction pathway. In complex systems, this monolithic state can allocate redundant capacity to dominant signals while providing weak or conflicting gradients to less dominant predictive structure. We introduce \method, a latent world-modeling framework based on orthogonal predictive factorization. Learned basis matrices analyze each target state into multiple components, and a dedicated prediction branch estimates each component from a shared context representation. Predictive regression preserves the factor magnitudes required for state synthesis, an orthogonality objective discourages repeated directions, factor-activity regularization maintains variation in projected targets, and online variance regularization discourages coordinate-wise encoder collapse. Predicted components are synthesized into a complete latent state that can be used by a readout, decoder, planner, or autoregressive rollout. The same predictive-state mechanism applies when the target is temporally future, spatially hidden, or another partial observation of the same system. Experiments on controlled vision, single-cell transcriptomics, longitudinal health records, continuous control, and molecular dynamics evaluate representation quality, forecasting, planning, and long-horizon stability.

</details>


### [122] [SAE-Xplainers: Rule-Based Feature Interpretation for Extreme Earth Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20117)
*Hugo Porta,Emanuele Dalsasso,Chang Xu,Theo Gnassounou,Devis Tuia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Geography-aware modulation of Sparse Autoencoder inputs combined with an ensemble of rule-based SAE-Xplainers to interpret high-dimensional, multi-modal Weather & Climate data for extreme Earth events; shows improved reconstruction and interpretable, literature-consistent rules across fires, tropical cyclones, and atmospheric rivers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interpretability is a critical bottleneck for deploying deep learning on Weather & Climate datasets. Conventional SAEs struggle to capture local geographical semantics and to provide human-understandable explanations for complex ExEE patterns.

Method: (i) Introduce geographic location-based modulation of SAE inputs to capture local semantic meaning of environmental patterns; (ii) develop an ensemble of rule-based SAE-Xplainers to translate high-dimensional, multi-modal predictors into human-readable rules.

Result: SAE input modulation improves reconstruction performance and feature utilization; SAE-Xplainers yield faithful interpretations that unfold complex climatic patterns into rules aligned with the scientific literature and help identify feature absorption.

Conclusion: The framework enhances interpretability of DL on W&C data, producing explanations consistent with domain knowledge and supporting potential operational deployment and scientific discovery.

Abstract: The emergence of large-scale Weather and Climate (W&C) datasets offers new opportunities for modeling extreme Earth events (ExEE) and their impacts using deep learning. However, their adoption in operational settings remains limited by the lack of models' interpretability. While for conventional text and image modalities, tools such as Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) have proven effective for extracting human-understandable concepts, their use for the analysis of ExEE remains challenging due to the nature of W&C data. To address this, we introduce (i) a geographic location-based modulation of the inputs of SAE to capture the local semantic meaning of environmental patterns, and (ii) an ensemble of rule-based SAE-Xplainers to interpret the resulting high-dimensional features derived from complex, multi-modal environmental predictors. We evaluate our method on three ExEE types: the prediction of fires, and the detection of tropical cyclones and atmospheric rivers. We show that SAE input modulation improves both reconstruction performance and feature utilization, and that our SAE-Xplainers enable faithful interpretation of complex climatic patterns by unfolding them into human-understandable rules that are consistent with the scientific literature, while also supporting the identification of feature absorption.

</details>


### [123] [Evaluating Neural Cartographic Relief Shading for Urban Environments: A Downtown Calgary Study Using High-Resolution DEM and DSM Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20149)
*Emmanuel Stefanakis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Exploratory comparison of analytical hillshading vs. Eduard neural shading in Calgary’s urban relief; parameter tuning can yield strong visual results; neural shading shows some strengths but is limited by training bias toward alpine terrain; calls for urban-focused training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether a neural shading model trained on mountainous terrain can be adapted to urban relief shading and identify the limits, strengths, and opportunities for cartographically useful urban visualization.

Method: Compare single-direction and multi-direction analytical hillshading with relief shading produced by Eduard across high-resolution DEM/DSM of downtown Calgary; systematically vary terrain type, micro/macro generalization, and flat-area detail while keeping large-scale shading style constant; exploratory, not a universal benchmark.

Result: Analytical methods remain reliable in certain contexts; Eduard can produce visually strong results for some urban features after parameter tuning; neural shading exhibits unexpected strengths but often falters due to its alpine-leaning training bias; the study maps out where each approach excels or fails in urban relief shading.

Conclusion: Targeted retraining and evaluation for urban relief shading are warranted; future work should develop urban-specific datasets and metrics to assess and improve neural shading performance in built environments.

Abstract: This article explores the performance of analytical and neural-based hillshading methods in a dense urban environment using high-resolution digital elevation model (DEM) and digital surface model (DSM) data for downtown Calgary. The study compares single-direction and multi-direction analytical hillshading with relief shading generated in Eduard, a machine-learning system originally developed to emulate Swiss-style shaded relief trained primarily on mountainous landscapes. Because Eduard was not designed for buildings, bridges, streets, trees, and other urban infrastructures, the central question is not whether it perfectly reproduces urban morphology, but whether parameter tuning can nevertheless produce visually strong, cartographically useful, and in some cases superior results when compared with conventional analytical methods. The analysis focuses especially on terrain type, micro and macro generalization, and flat-area detail parameters, while keeping the large-scale shading style constant throughout the neural experiments. The article is structured as an exploratory comparison rather than a benchmark of universal best practice. It aims to identify where analytical hillshading remains more reliable, where Eduard offers unexpected strengths, and where neural shading fails because of its training bias toward alpine terrain.
