The study contributes to current work on terrain representation by testing whether a neural approach designed for natural landforms can be adapted to a highly built urban setting, and it concludes by arguing for future model training and evaluation specifically targeted at urban relief shading.

</details>


### [124] [Ask Self, Ask Others: Relation Is All You Need](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20172)
*Yuting Ge,Pengju Yang,Mingkai Nie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Relational token mixing reorganizes pairwise evidence into explicit Self and Exchange relations to derive information flow, enabling Full Relation, FlashRelation, Linear Relation, Hybrid Relation, and a KV-style Relation Cache. Across decoder-only models at ~10M/30M/100M params, Full Relation achieves lower final validation NLL than MHA. FlashRelation dramatically speeds up relative to materialized Full, and the Full relation family attains substantial throughput (76.4–84.9% of PyTorch FlashAttention). Hybrid Relation combines 75% Linear layers with strong language-modeling quality, supporting a relation-first view: ask Self, ask Others, then let Flow follow Relation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve token mixing beyond traditional MHA by rethinking it as a relation-based evidence exchange, aiming for both better modeling quality and higher efficiency in inference and training.

Method: Introduce a family of Relation primitives that organize pairwise scores into Self and Exchange relations, derive information flow from these relations, and implement variants (Full, Flash, Linear, Hybrid) plus a KV-style cache. Evaluate on matched decoder-only models at 10M/30M/100M parameters, compare against MHA, and benchmark throughput on fixed-context reference and production workloads.

Result: Full Relation achieves lower final validation negative log-likelihood than MHA at all three scales. FlashRelation is 3.60–4.41× faster than the materialized Full implementation in a fixed-context benchmark. Production-scale workloads reach 76.4–84.9% of PyTorch FlashAttention throughput with the Full Relation operator. Hybrid Relation, using 75% Linear layers, maintains strong language-modeling quality.

Conclusion: A relation-first perspective on token mixing—first resolve Self and Others, then let the information flow be governed by Relation—yields both modeling strength and efficiency gains, and supports a spectrum of relation-based mixing primitives for scalable LM training and inference.

Abstract: Attention directly derives normalized information flow from pairwise scores. We introduce Relation, an alternative token-mixing primitive that first organizes pairwise evidence into explicit Self and Exchange relations and derives information flow afterward. This relational organization gives rise to Full Relation, FlashRelation, Linear Relation, Hybrid Relation, and a KV-style Relation Cache. Across matched decoder-only models at approximately 10M, 30M, and 100M parameters, Full Relation achieves lower final validation NLL than MHA at all three scales. In a fixed-context reference benchmark, FlashRelation is 3.60-4.41x faster than the materialized Full Relation implementation. Across scale-matched production workloads, it reaches 76.4-84.9% of PyTorch FlashAttention throughput while executing the Full Relation operator. Hybrid Relation uses 75% Linear Relation layers and achieves strong language-modeling quality. These results support a relation-first view of token mixing: ask Self, ask Others, then let Flow follow Relation.

</details>


### [125] [A Standardized Framework for Machine Learning in Power System Protection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20181)
*Julian Oelhaf,Georg Kordowich,Paula Andrea Pérez-Toro,Christian Bergler,Johann Jäger,Andreas Maier,Siming Bayer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Standardizes ML-based protection evaluation via a seven-dimension framework; demonstrates on PROTECT-90 with an MLP achieving near-perfect macro-F1 and precise localization, while showing evaluation choices (horizon, observability) influence results; enables reproducible, auditable assessment and potential certification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ML-based protection studies report high scores that depend on opaque, varying evaluation setups. A standardized, transparent framework is needed to make evaluations reproducible, comparable, and certification-ready.

Method: Define seven study dimensions: protection objective, physical scope, observability, timing and decision windows, targets and sample validity, validation protocol, and evaluation outputs. Instantiate framework on PROTECT-90 EM-transient benchmark (9022 simulated episodes, 90 kV double-line topology) for onset-conditioned fault classification and localization. Under centralized sensing, 20 ms windows aligned with simulation metadata, episode-grouped validation, use a multi-layer perceptron (MLP). Compare against a conventional two-ended locator. Assess to horizon extensions (50 ms) and varying observability; induce measurement degradation.

Result: MLP achieved macro-averaged F1 of 0.991 ± 0.001 for classification; localization MAE of 10.20 ± 0.25% of line length. Extending horizon to 50 ms preserved the task-dependent performance asymmetry. Reduced observability roughly doubled localization error, little effect on classification. The conventional locator outperformed learning locators when richer, clean information was available. Clean performance did not guarantee robustness under degraded measurements.

Conclusion: The framework exposes assumptions as explicit, reproducible evidence, enabling more comparable, auditable evaluation and supporting future certification-oriented assessment of ML protection functions.

Abstract: Studies of machine-learning-based power-system protection increasingly report near-perfect scores, yet the meaning of those scores depends strongly on the evaluation setting. Protection task, physical scope, measurements, timing, targets, preprocessing, and validation often vary jointly and remain incompletely specified. This paper proposes a standardization-oriented framework that treats evaluation design as part of the scientific contribution. It defines seven required study dimensions: protection objective, physical scope, observability, timing and decision windows, targets and sample validity, validation protocol, and evaluation outputs. The framework is instantiated in a bounded case study on the public PROTECT-90 electromagnetic-transient benchmark, comprising 9022 simulated episodes from a 90 kV double-line topology, for onset-conditioned fault classification and localization. Under centralized sensing, simulation-metadata-aligned 20 ms windows, and episode-grouped validation, a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) achieved a five-fold mean macro-averaged F1 score of 0.991 +/- 0.001 for classification and a localization mean absolute error of 10.20 +/- 0.25% of line length (mean +/- std across episode-grouped folds). Extending the decision horizon to 50 ms preserved this task-dependent performance asymmetry, while reduced observability approximately doubled the MLP localization error but had little effect on classification. A synchronized two-ended conventional locator outperformed the learning locators under its richer clean information set, and measurement degradation showed that clean predictive performance did not determine robustness. The framework turns evaluation assumptions into explicit, reproducible evidence and provides a basis for more comparable, auditable evaluation and future certification-oriented assessment of machine-learning protection functions.

</details>


### [126] [Exact Algebraic Computation of Learning Coefficients for Two-Dimensional Singular Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20183)
*Grégoire Sergeant-Perthuis,Elias Tsigaridas,Jules Tsukahara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deterministic algorithm to compute local RLCTs exactly for 2D models where KL distance is contact-equivalent to a polynomial, enabling exact WBIC calibration and revealing algebraic structure; complexity bound; applicable to polynomial neural networks; improves over sampling in shallow regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Regular model selection criteria like BIC fail for singular models (e.g., deep learning). WBIC uses local learning coefficients equal to RLCT of KL divergence to obtain correct marginal likelihood asymptotics. Exact RLCT computation has been limited to special cases; there is a need for a deterministic, general method to obtain RLCTs to calibrate WBIC and understand model behavior.

Method: Develop a deterministic algorithm that computes local RLCTs exactly for any two-dimensional model whose KL divergence is contact-equivalent to a polynomial. Derive a bound on the algorithm's complexity and demonstrate effectiveness across a broad class of models, including polynomial neural networks.

Result: The algorithm yields exact local RLCTs for the targeted class of models, providing ground truth to validate sampling-based estimators. It exposes algebraic structure in the learning coefficients that sampling cannot capture and exhibits speed advantages in the shallow regime. Demonstrated over a broad class of models, including polynomial neural networks.

Conclusion: This work delivers the first deterministic method to compute local RLCTs exactly in the specified setting, enabling precise WBIC calibration and deeper theoretical insight into learning coefficients. It also offers a practical tool for ground-truth comparison and suggests potential extensions beyond two dimensions and to broader model families.

Abstract: Classical information criteria such as the Bayesian Information Criterion (BIC) rely on regularity assumptions that break down for singular models, leading to incorrect model selection in settings such as deep learning. The Widely Applicable Bayesian Information Criterion (WBIC) relies on local learning coefficients $λ$, which in the analytic case coincides with local Real Log Canonical Thresholds (RLCT) of the Kullback-Leibler divergence of the model, to capture correct marginal likelihood asymptotics. Exact computation of the learning coefficients has been limited to special cases, and only sampling-based estimation methods are generally applicable. We present the first deterministic algorithm that computes local RLCTs exactly for any two-dimensional model whose Kullback-Leibler distance is contact equivalent to a polynomial, derive a bound on its complexity, and demonstrate its effectiveness for a broad class of models, with applications including polynomial neural networks. Beyond providing ground truth to calibrate sampling-based estimators, exact computation reveals algebraic structure in learning coefficients that sampling cannot and out-speeds it in the shallow regime.

</details>


### [127] [Decoding silent reading from non-invasive EEG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20186)
*Ingo Marquardt,Anthilia Alchanat,Priyanka Jain*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Open-vocabulary word-level information can be decoded from EEG during silent reading using a CLIP-style contrastive objective; the approach is data-limited rather than saturated and robust to reduced electrode sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the data bottleneck in non-invasive inner-speech decoding by validating silent reading as a scalable proxy task and quantifying how much lexical/semantic information EEG can reveal.

Method: Dense 19-channel dry EEG (ca. 49 h across 393 runs) with rapid serial visual presentation of words. Words randomized in typography to decorrelate identity from low-level visual form. A convolutional EEG encoder (optionally with a causal transformer) is trained with a CLIP-like contrastive objective to align short EEG windows with hidden-state word embeddings from a large language model. Evaluation via word-grouped top-10 retrieval against permutation baselines. Ablations remove occipital/posterior-temporal electrodes; control analyses separate word-level decoding from narrative context and from a non-neural positional prior from the transformer.

Result: Decoding is reliably above chance for open vocabulary words, including mid-frequency and rare words. Performance scales log-linearly with training data with no saturation observed. Removing occipital/posterior-temporal electrodes reduces word-level gain by about one third but leaves context tracking intact. The work demonstrates recoverable word-level information from EEG during silent reading and shows the decoding is data-limited rather than saturated.

Conclusion: EEG during silent reading carries lexical information that can be decoded with a CLIP-style alignment to language embeddings. The study supports the feasibility of scalable, non-invasive inner-speech decoding using open-vocabulary targets, though generalization across individuals and real-time application remain to be established.

Abstract: Non-invasive decoding of inner speech faces a fundamental data problem: a corpus pairing brain activity with a person's spontaneous inner monologue cannot be collected, and the available proxy paradigms (cued repetitive and retrospectively reported generative inner speech) are slow to acquire, poorly time-locked, and subject compliance is unverifiable. We therefore treat silent reading as a scalable proxy task and ask how much lexical and semantic information a contrastive decoder can extract from it. We report an open-vocabulary analysis of approximately 240,000 word presentations recorded from a single densely-sampled participant across 393 runs (ca. 49 h) of 19-channel dry-electrode EEG. Words from continuous narrative text were presented in rapid serial visual presentation, with typography randomised on every trial to partially decorrelate word identity from low-level visual form. A convolutional EEG encoder, optionally followed by a causal transformer, was trained with a CLIP-style contrastive objective to align short EEG windows with hidden-state embeddings of the presented word taken from a large language model. Decoding, evaluated as word-grouped top-10 retrieval against permutation baselines, was reliably above chance, extended to mid-frequency and rare words, and scaled log-linearly with training-data volume with no sign of saturation. Removing occipital and posterior-temporal electrodes reduced the word-level gain by roughly one third but left context tracking unchanged. Control analyses separate word-level decoding from narrative context tracking and from a non-neural positional prior introduced by the transformer's positional embedding. These results establish that open-vocabulary word-level information is recoverable from EEG during silent reading, and that decoding is data-limited rather than saturated.

</details>


### [128] [DICS: Data-Informed Centroid Splitting for Decision Tree Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20258)
*MD Saifur Rahman Mazumder,Feng Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DICS is a clustering-based framework that uses data-driven priors to generate a compact, informative set of splits for classification trees, reducing search space while maintaining accuracy; extensible to trees, RFs, and gradient boosting with theoretical guarantees under certain assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce the computational burden of exhaustive split search in training decision trees on large/high‑dimensional data without sacrificing predictive performance.

Method: Use data-driven centroids and clustering to produce a small, informative candidate split set. Incorporate class-aware priors to emphasize informative splits for each class. Apply within classification trees, random forests, and gradient boosting.

Result: Under stated assumptions, DICS does not degrade classification performance compared to exhaustive search. Empirical results show comparable accuracy with substantially reduced training time on synthetic and benchmark datasets.

Conclusion: Data-informed priors via centroid-based split construction enable scalable, efficient tree-based learning with preserved predictive performance, applicable across tree-based ensembles.

Abstract: Decision tree-based models are widely used in machine learning due to their interpretability and strong empirical performance. However, training decision trees can be computationally expensive, particularly for large and high-dimensional datasets, largely due to the exhaustive search over candidate splits at each node. To improve computational efficiency, we propose Data-Informed Centroid Splitting (DICS), a clustering-based framework that constructs a compact and informative set of candidate splits using data-driven priors. By incorporating class-aware structure, DICS significantly reduces the split search space for classification tasks while preserving predictive performance. We further provide theoretical analysis showing that under the stated assumptions, DICS does not degrade the performance of classification trees compared to exhaustive split search. DICS can be incorporated into classification trees, random forests, and gradient-boosting models. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DICS achieves comparable accuracy while substantially reducing training time across synthetic and benchmark datasets, highlighting the benefit of integrating data-informed priors into split selection for scalable classification tree learning.

</details>


### [129] [Dynamic Structural Causal Modeling for Sleep](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20285)
*Ranveer Singh,Saurabh Mathur,Pranuthi Tenali,Arun Badi,Sriraam Natarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dynamic causal graphs of sleep-disordered breathing were learned from HSAT data using PCMCI+ and show sex- and age-specific differences, with robust results via edge blacklisting and bootstrap aggregation; core persistent relationships are temporal self-dependency and apnea-desaturation across all cohorts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sleep-disordered breathing causal dynamics vary across patient populations, hindering targeted interventions; understanding these differences can guide cohort-specific treatments.

Method: Apply PCMCI+ to windowed fractional variables derived from 105 HSAT recordings, use edge blacklisting informed by domain knowledge, and employ bootstrap aggregation to mitigate small subcohort sizes.

Result: Learned causal graphs reveal persistent temporal self-dependencies and apnea-desaturation relationships across all cohorts, while other causal connections vary substantially by sex and age subcohorts.

Conclusion: Causal structure in sleep-disordered breathing exhibits cross-cohort heterogeneity; methods combining domain-informed constraints and bootstrap aggregation yield robust, cohort-specific causal graphs that can inform targeted interventions.

Abstract: The causal dynamics of sleep-disordered breathing are complex and vary across patient populations, hindering the development of targeted interventions. We learn dynamic causal graphs of sleep-disordered breathing from Home Sleep Apnea Test (HSAT) recordings, revealing systematic differences in causal structure across sex and age subcohorts. We do so using the PCMCI+ algorithm on windowed fractional variables derived from 105 HSAT recordings, exploiting domain knowledge via edge blacklisting and employing bootstrap aggregation to address small subcohort sizes. The learned graphs show that temporal self-dependencies and the apnea-desaturation relationship persist across all cohorts, while other relationships vary substantially.

</details>


### [130] [Physical-Support Confidence Sets for Highly Coherent Dictionaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20295)
*Guan-Ju Peng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A resolution-aware framework for physical-support inference across dictionaries with coherent atoms, deriving a minimax physical-resolution bound δ_opt(N,s) ≍ min{ s, 1/(√N s^2) }, and introducing an adaptive finite-bank algorithm (AEB) to reduce computation while avoiding over-precise localization; validated on finite-bank experiments including synthetic regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In sparse pursuit after dictionary learning, high dictionary coherence can produce ambiguous physical interpretation of the same support. A framework is needed to jointly account for dictionary uncertainty and deployment signals, ensuring physically meaningful explanations that survive across calibration- and deployment-compatible representations.

Method: Develop cross-dictionary confidence correspondence to retain calibration- and deployment-compatible explanations; project surviving explanations onto physical-support space. For local coherent-atom classes with separation s, derive minimax physical resolution δ_opt(N,s) ~ min{ s, 1/(√N s^2) }; orientation-information scale Ns^6 governs relative resolution. Propose deployment replication analysis and introduce Active Endpoint Bracketing (AEB), an adaptive finite-bank procedure that evaluates only candidates with potential physical impact, coarsening or abstaining otherwise.

Result: Theory yields a minimax bound on physical resolution and a principled computational strategy. Finite-bank experiments, including a four-region synthetic application, show that naive point-valued selectors can be physically overprecise, whereas AEB achieves comparable or better physical localization with fewer candidate evaluations.

Conclusion: The framework provides robust, resolution-aware physical-support inference under dictionary uncertainty, with AEB offering computational efficiency and avoidance of unsupported refinement. Deployment replication improves localization only when orientation changes cannot be absorbed by coefficient adjustment.

Abstract: Sparse pursuit after dictionary learning can yield a precise atom support even when its physical interpretation is not justified by the calibration data, especially for highly coherent dictionaries where alternative calibration-compatible dictionaries may assign different physical meanings to the same selected support. We develop resolution-aware physical-support inference that jointly accounts for uncertainty in the learned dictionary and in the representation of a deployment signal. Our cross-dictionary confidence correspondence retains calibration-compatible dictionaries and deployment-compatible sparse representations, then projects the surviving explanations onto physical-support space. For local coherent-atom classes with separation scale s, once the deployment data resolve the coherent-block explanation and its atom support, the minimax physical resolution from N calibration signals satisfies $δ_{\mathrm{opt}}(N,s)\asymp\min\{s,\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}s^2}\}$, with relative resolution governed by the orientation-information scale $Ns^6$. Deployment replication improves physical localization only when orientation changes cannot be absorbed by adjusting the active coefficients. For computation, we introduce active endpoint bracketing (AEB), an adaptive finite-bank procedure that evaluates only candidates that can still affect the physical report and otherwise safely coarsens or abstains. Finite-bank experiments, including a four-region synthetic application, show that a point-valued plug-in selector can be physically overprecise, whereas AEB avoids unsupported refinement with fewer candidate evaluations.

</details>


### [131] [Explainable Transformer Models for Clinical Prediction Tasks on Structured Electronic Health Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20315)
*Jun Ni Du,Lukas Adamek,Maxim Kryukov,Flavio Dormont,Ziv Bar-Joseph,Sven Jager,Brandon Rufino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BERT-LER is a BERT-style model for coded EHR timelines that encodes laboratory tests as percentile-binned discrete tokens and uses Integrated Gradients for token-level explanations; it achieves competitive performance on EHRShot and asthma progression tasks, with explainability aligned to known clinical risk factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address a gap in EHR foundation models by unifying quantitative laboratory value representation with interpretability, and to evaluate whether predictive performance and explanations generalize beyond standard clinical prediction tasks.

Method: Pretrain on a de-identified EHR dataset of 75 million patients. Laboratory test results are encoded as discrete tokens through percentile-based binning to preserve graded information. A BERT-style architecture processes the encoded EHR sequences. Integrated Gradients are used to produce token-level attributions grounded in the input sequence. Evaluation is conducted on the public EHRShot benchmark and an asthma severity progression study using real-world data.

Result: Predictive performance is competitive with publicly available benchmarks, and on laboratory-related tasks it often exceeds them. Generated attributions align with clinically known risk factors.

Conclusion: The framework unifies laboratory value representation and explainability within a single language-model-based EHR approach and appears generalizable across therapeutic areas and prediction tasks with structured EHRs.

Abstract: Predictive models over structured electronic health records (EHRs) remain central to machine learning for healthcare, but few have jointly emphasized quantitative laboratory information and interpretability with respect to input medical events. We present BERT-LER, a BERT-style model for coded EHR timelines pretrained and fine-tuned from a de-identified EHR dataset of 75 million patients, that encodes laboratory test results as discrete tokens while retaining graded information through percentile-based binning, paired with Integrated Gradients for token-level attributions grounded in the input EHR sequence. We benchmark our approach on the public EHRShot benchmark suite and on an asthma severity progression study based on real-world data. This addresses a methodological gap in EHR foundation-style modeling by unifying laboratory value representation and explainability in a single framework, while assessing whether both predictive performance and explanations generalize beyond standard clinical prediction tasks. Across EHRShot and asthma tasks, BERT-LER achieves predictive performance that is competitive with, and on laboratory-related tasks often exceeds, publicly available benchmark models, and provides attributions that align with clinically known risk factors. Our architecture and explainability approach can be applied to many therapeutic areas and prediction tasks using language models trained on structured EHRs.

</details>


### [132] [A comparison between ceiling-mounted FMCW, IR-UWB and Wi-Fi radar for in-bedroom human activity monitoring and sleep interruption detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20322)
*Anton Lambrecht,Reda El Hail,Xianjun Jiao,Pieter Crombez,Dominique Schreurs,Peter Karsmakers,Adnan Shahid,Eli De Poorter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Controlled side-by-side evaluation of ceiling-mounted FMCW, IR-UWB, and Wi-Fi sensing for HAR and sleep monitoring using the same CNN, 20 participants in six rooms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of fair, identical-condition comparisons across RF sensing modalities for healthcare monitoring and assess practical placement (ceiling-mounted) implications.

Method: Synchronized recordings from 20 participants across six room layouts for three RF modalities; identical CNN is used to perform 10-class HAR and 4-class sleep monitoring; metrics reported include macro F1 scores and cross-subject/unseen-room generalization.

Result: IR-UWB achieves the highest cross-subject HAR macro F1 (89.0%), FMCW best generalization to unseen layouts (83.8%), and all modalities exceed 92% macro F1 on sleep monitoring in unseen environments.

Conclusion: There is a fundamental trade-off between recognition performance and environmental robustness among RF sensing modalities, influenced by range resolution, antenna diversity, Doppler resolution, and spatial information retention; results provide practical design guidelines for healthcare-oriented RF sensing systems.

Abstract: Despite their growing importance for contact-free radio frequency (RF) based healthcare monitoring, different radio technologies such as frequency-modulated continuous wave (FMCW) radar, impulse radio ultra-wideband (IR-UWB), and Wi-Fi sensing are rarely compared under identical deployment conditions, as existing studies typically differ in hardware, datasets, and evaluation methodologies. In addition, the performance of ceiling-mounted radars, despite their practical deployment and cost advantages in healthcare environments, remain underexplored. Therefore, this paper presents a controlled comparison and analysis of ceiling-mounted FMCW, IR-UWB, and Wi-Fi sensing using synchronized recordings from 20 participants across six room layouts. All technologies are evaluated with the same convolutional neural network (CNN) on both a fine-grained 10-class human activity recognition (HAR) task and a coarse 4-class sleep monitoring task. IR-UWB achieves the highest cross-subject activity recognition performance (89.0% macro F1), while FMCW generalizes best to unseen room layouts (83.8% macro F1). For sleep monitoring, all technologies exceed 92% macro F1 in unseen environments. The results reveal a fundamental trade-off between recognition performance and environmental robustness, which can be explained through differences in range resolution, antenna diversity, Doppler resolution, and spatial information retention. These findings provide practical guidelines for the design of healthcare-oriented RF sensing systems.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [133] [Active Inference as Context Acquisition for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19202)
*Sanchayan Dutta,Sai Niranjan Ramachandran,Suvrit Sra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Active-inference framework for context acquisition in interactive AI; introduces Optimal Question Asking (OQA) to trade off clarification, retrieval, prompting, and stopping under token budgets, using exact posteriors and a dynamic-programming oracle; evaluated on frontier language models across binary and multiway tasks with 25–300 candidates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficiently acquiring the right context when user constraints are missing, balancing informative gains against token costs to minimize overall decision-theoretic free energy.

Method: An inner inference step updates beliefs about a latent task state; an outer decision selects the next context action, task action, or stop action to minimize expected free energy (costed). In deterministic settings, the epistemic term reduces to expected information gain (optionally normalized by token cost). The framework is instantiated as Optimal Question Asking (OQA) with exact posteriors and a dynamic programming oracle, and evaluated on binary and multiway categorical tasks (25–300 candidates). Also studies clarification before generation and automated prompt optimization under token budgets; the approach is model-agnostic.

Result: Demonstrates a principled, token-aware mechanism for context acquisition that can substantially reduce extraneous context actions while preserving task performance. OQA provides exact Bayesian updates and efficient planning via a DP oracle; experiments on frontier LMs show performance gains across varying candidate-set sizes and task types, including clarification-before-generation and prompt-optimization under budgets.

Conclusion: Active inference offers a design-principled, model-agnostic approach to the context-acquisition layer of AI agents, unifying queries, retrievals, prompting, and stopping under a single cost-aware decision framework (OQA) that scales to realistic token budgets.

Abstract: Interactive AI agents must acquire the right context as efficiently as possible. When a user omits a constraint, preference, file, or task variable, an agent can proceed with a default assumption or spend tokens on a clarifying question, retrieval call, tool call, or prompt trial. We formulate this tradeoff as active inference for context acquisition. An inner inference step updates beliefs over a latent task state, and an outer decision selects the next context action, task action, or stop action to minimize expected free energy under cost. In deterministic settings, the epistemic term reduces to expected information gain, optionally normalized by token cost. We instantiate the framework in Optimal Question Asking (OQA), with exact posteriors and a dynamic programming oracle, and benchmark frontier language models on binary and multiway categorical tasks from 25 to 300 candidates. We also study clarification before generation and automated prompt optimization under token budgets. The formulation is model-agnostic and views active inference as a design principle for the context-acquisition layer of AI agents.

</details>


### [134] [Robust Metaheuristics under Uncertainty for Berth Allocation and Quay Crane Assignment: A Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19214)
*Yang Li,Peilan Xu,Wenjian Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A focused literature review on robust population-based metaheuristics for BACAP under uncertainty, proposing a mechanism-oriented taxonomy, a benchmark suite, and identifying open challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation:  BACAP is a critical port-terminal scheduling problem where uncertainty (arrivals, handling times, resource disruptions) can derail nominal plans. A consolidated review and benchmarks are needed due to fragmented existing work.

Method: Organize existing work by mechanism: (i) solution representation and decoding, (ii) robust evaluation and selection, (iii) robustness-guided search dynamics, (iv) feasibility preservation and recovery. Summarize uncertainty sources/representations, provide a benchmark suite for uncertain BACAP, and report baseline results by combining representative metaheuristics with robustness strategies.

Result: First focused review dedicated to robust population-based metaheuristics for BACAP under uncertainty. Provides a mechanism-oriented taxonomy, a benchmark suite for controlled empirical comparison, and illustrative baseline results. Identifies open challenges in benchmark extension, robustness-aware search design, time-adaptive robustness, and non-stationary uncertainty.

Conclusion: Robust, population-based metaheuristics for BACAP are promising but require better benchmarks, improved robustness-aware search strategies, and time-adaptive handling of non-stationary uncertainty to be practically reliable.

Abstract: The berth allocation and quay crane assignment problem (BACAP) is a representative port-terminal scheduling problem in maritime transportation and freight logistics, where vessel arrivals, berth positions, service durations, and quay?crane availability are tightly coupled. Under uncertainties such as arrival deviations, handling-time fluctuations, and resource disruptions, schedules optimized under nominal assumptions may become fragile during execution, motivating the study of robust metaheuristic optimization for BACAP in port-terminal operations. Although population-based metaheuristics have been widely used for BACAP and related port-scheduling problems, existing studies remain fragmented in their uncertainty repre?sentations, robustness criteria, search mechanisms, and empir?ical evaluation protocols. To the best of our knowledge, this paper provides the first focused review dedicated to robust population-based metaheuristics for BACAP under uncertainty. We first summarize uncertainty sources and information repre?sentations in BACAP, and then organize existing methods from a mechanism-oriented perspective, covering solution representation and decoding, robust evaluation and selection, robustness-guided search dynamics, and feasibility preservation and recovery. We further present a benchmark suite for uncertain BACAP to support controlled empirical comparison and report illustrative baseline results by combining representative metaheuristics with different robustness strategies. Finally, we identify open chal?lenges related to benchmark extension, robustness-aware search design, time-adaptive robustness, and non-stationary uncertainty.

</details>


### [135] [How to Navigate Uncertainty About AI Consciousness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19215)
*Dr Tom McClelland*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Shift from uncertain AI consciousness debates to valence-based evaluation by checking whether an AI could have valenced experiences if conscious, to ground responsible development of potentially conscious AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep uncertainty about AI consciousness creates ethical and resource-allocation risks; a tractable criterion is needed to guide moral status and deployment decisions.

Method: Argue for assessing AI states that would constitute valenced experiences upon consciousness; outline criteria and assessment approach to determine valence-relevant properties.

Result: A valence-centric criterion is proposed as sufficient to ground responsible development, enabling policy and practice guidance without resolving whether AI is conscious.

Conclusion: Valence evaluation provides a practical and morally relevant framework to navigate potential consciousness in AI, balancing risks of under- and over-granting moral status.

Abstract: Given deep uncertainty about the possibility of artificial consciousness, it is unclear how we should treat potentially sentient AI. On the one hand, we could assume insentience but risk doing terrible harms to entities that deserve moral standing. On the other hand, we could assume sentience and instead risk wasting resources on insentient machines. The intractability of questions around AI consciousness mean that this dilemma is hard to escape. I suggest a way out of that shifts from intractable questions of AI consciousness to tractable questions of AI valence. Specifically, we can assess whether an AI has states that would constitute valenced experiences if it were conscious. I show how this is sufficient to ground a responsible approach to the development of potentially conscious AI.

</details>


### [136] [Bounded Sovereignty and the Control Tax: Pricing AI Oversight When the Deployer Does Not Own the Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19216)
*Zhen Wen Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces bounded sovereignty—a framework for AI safety control under partial access (data, model, infrastructure, interaction) with a four-layer typology, protocol matrix, and sovereignty discount cost; supports synthetic experiments (1.35M cases) and scenarios; findings: logs, pre-execution gateway, trace/version control, and scope restriction impact safety and usefulness; advocates explicit access assumptions in control protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Regulated organizations often deploy frontier models via APIs with limited control over model weights, infrastructure, logs, or updates; existing control protocols assume full instrumentability and thus may be inapplicable; a formalized, access-aware approach to safety controls is needed.

Method: Proposes a four-layer access typology; builds a protocol-by-layer requirements matrix; defines sovereignty discount cost; conducts a large-scale synthetic access-ablation experiment (1.35M cases) framed around an anonymized national payments infrastructure; analyzes how access constraints shape feasible control protocols and safety outcomes.

Result: Shows that complete logs improve diagnosis; a pre-execution gateway enables intervention; trace access and model-version control strengthen post-incident explanation; scope restriction can improve safety while reducing usefulness; conclusions drawn from synthetic experiments (construct-validity exercise) rather than real-world data.

Conclusion: Control protocols should state explicit access assumptions; bounded sovereignty offers a practical framework for selecting and designing safety controls under real-world access constraints; calls for operationalization across industries and validation in practice.

Abstract: AI control research asks how to deploy models safely even when they may be misaligned, but many control protocols assume that the deployer can instrument the model and its surrounding pipeline. That assumption often fails for regulated organisations using frontier models through APIs or managed endpoints, where the deployer may control the business process but not the model weights, serving infrastructure, internal traces, update process, or full interaction logs. This paper introduces bounded sovereignty: partial technical and contractual access across the data, model, infrastructure, and interaction layers of the AI stack. It argues that these access conditions determine which control protocols can be executed in practice. The paper contributes a four-layer access typology, a protocol-by-layer requirements matrix, and the concept of sovereignty discount cost: the part of the control tax spent substituting for missing access through contracts, architecture, audit, vendor assurance, residual risk, or reduced system scope. It also reports a synthetic access-ablation experiment over 1.35 million synthetic case simulations and interprets the findings through an anonymised national-payments-infrastructure scenario. The experiment is not real-world payment-system evidence; it is a construct-validity exercise. The results show that complete logs improve diagnosis, a pre-execution gateway enables intervention, trace access and model-version control strengthen post-incident explanation, and scope restriction can improve safety while reducing usefulness. Control protocols proposed as general safety solutions should therefore state their access assumptions explicitly.

</details>


### [137] [Interaction valence reveals contrasting social networks in dairy cattle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19222)
*Sibi Parivendan,Suresh Raja Neethirajan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Valence-aware social-network analysis of video-derived interactions in a dairy herd, separating affiliative and agonistic relations to reveal distinct network structure and welfare-relevant patterns; highlights need for longitudinal validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond isolated interaction counts and capture social organization and valence in livestock to better understand competition, affiliation, and welfare.

Method: Pose-based computer-vision pipeline analyzed 7 h 39 min of continuous video from a commercial dairy farm; quality control left 1,183 of 1,414 candidate interactions involving 36 cows and 177 dyads. In a predicted-class-balanced audit of 198 clips, automated labels agreed with manual labels 82.8%, macro-F1 0.872. Constructed aggregated social network (density 0.281, transitivity 0.513, mean path length 1.88) and modular communities (Q = 0.429). Stratified events by valence to create affiliative and agonistic layers; analyzed centrality and edge sets.

Result: Agonistic interactions dominated (72.4% of events, 76.0% of duration). The cow with the most partners did not have the highest betweenness. Valence-based separation yielded different communities and individual positions; pooled counts obscured behavioural composition.

Conclusion: Valence-aware social-network analysis offers a framework to test hypotheses on competition, affiliation, and welfare-related change in livestock, but requires longitudinal validation before deployment as welfare or health indicators.

Abstract: Social relationships shape access to resources, exposure to conflict and group stability, yet automated livestock monitoring typically treats behaviour as isolated events. Here, we present a valence-aware social-network framework that transforms video-derived interactions into herd-level representations of affiliative and agonistic organization. A pose-based computer-vision pipeline analysed 7 h 39 min of continuous video from the pre-milking area of one commercial dairy farm. After quality control, 1,183 of 1,414 candidate interactions remained, involving 36 cows and 177 dyads. In a predicted-class-balanced audit of 198 pipeline-detected clips, automated and manual labels agreed in 82.8% of cases, with an unweighted audit-sample macro-F1 of 0.872. These values describe the audited sample rather than prevalence-weighted or end-to-end deployment performance. The aggregated network was connected (density = 0.281; transitivity = 0.513; mean path length = 1.88), and predicted affiliative events formed five algorithmic communities (modularity Q = 0.429). Within the observed zone, predicted agonistic interactions comprised 72.4% of retained events and 76.0% of interaction duration. The cow with the most partners did not have the highest betweenness centrality. Separating events by predicted valence produced descriptively different affiliative and agonistic layers, with contrasting edge sets, community partitions and individual positions. Thus, pooled interaction counts can obscure the behavioural composition of an observed network. Valence-aware analysis provides a framework for testing hypotheses about competition, affiliation and welfare-relevant change, while requiring longitudinal validation before use as a welfare or health indicator.

</details>


### [138] [Air Traffic Control Using Large Language Models: Prompt Engineering, Architecture, and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19299)
*Mahyar Ghazanfari,Matthias Casanova,Jordan Kam,Alex Zongo,Peng Wei,Torsten Darrell,Alexandre Bayen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An experimental study assessing LLMs’ ability to generate operational ATC transmissions in a pilot-in-the-loop setup, showing that light prompts with in-context worked examples improve realism, while heavily scripted prompts deteriorate due to error accumulation; injecting correct history mitigates, outlining a path and current limits for LLM-assisted ATC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-critical ATC communications are largely human-driven; the work investigates whether large language models can support or augment ATC by producing realistic transmissions, and identifies how prompt design and history usage affect performance.

Method: Ground-truth flight P0 is hand-transcribed; a pilot-in-the-loop pipeline uses prompts P1–P5 of increasing constraint; models act ATC to a fixed pilot transcript while conditioning on dialogue history; nine LLMs are evaluated; experiments vary the inclusion of a worked-d transcript example and whether conditioning on prior replies vs. ground-truth history; evaluation uses lexical, structural, semantic similarity metrics and an LLM-as-judge (GPT-5.5) validated against human annotation.

Result: Providing a worked example improves similarity to ground truth; tightening prompts does not help and the most heavily scripted prompt collapses due to error accumulation unless correct history is injected; light prompts perform best; the approach works across nine LLMs with some limits; injective history correction repairs some errors.

Conclusion: Outlines a concrete path toward LLM-assisted ATC while highlighting current limitations and the importance of history injection and prompt design to maintain operational realism.

Abstract: Air traffic control (ATC) communication is a safety-critical dialogue that remains largely human-driven even as other parts of air traffic management have been semi-automated. In this article, we experimentally evaluate whether large language models (LLMs) can generate operationally realistic ATC transmissions. An experimental general-aviation flight flying over the San Francisco "Bay Tour" route is hand-transcribed and used as ground truth (P0). Through a pilot-in-the-loop process we design five prompt structures (P1-P5) of increasing constraint and embed them in a stateful multi-turn pipeline, where the model plays ATC to a fixed pilot transcript while conditioning on the accumulating dialogue history. Across nine open- and closed-source LLMs we vary the prompt, the presence of a worked transcript from a different experimental flight as an in-context example, and whether the model conditions on its own prior replies or on injected ground-truth history. Turns are scored with lexical, structural, and semantic similarity metrics and by an LLM-as-judge (GPT-5.5) validated against human expert annotation. Supplying a worked example improves similarity, but tightening the prompt does not: the lightest prompts perform best and the most heavily scripted one collapses as its own errors accumulate through the dialogue, which injecting correct history repairs. These results outline a concrete path and its current limits toward LLM-assisted ATC.

</details>


### [139] [Outcome Monitors: Recovery Affordances for Silent Tool Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19303)
*Sugam Panthi,Rabab Abdelfattah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Outcome Monitors detect violations of extracted outcome contracts, preserve results, and issue recovery receipts to route around tool failures, yielding substantial gains in completion across ToolMaze and tau-bench, though gains depend on explicit recovery tools and vocabulary coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve agent robustness to tool failures by enforcing and monitoring outcome contracts; enable graceful degradation and recovery through public tools and receipts.

Method: Introduce Outcome Monitors mined from task-disjoint traces or public schemas. On contract violations, the monitor preserves the result and issues a nonbinding receipt naming the violated property and public recovery tools. Evaluate in frozen ToolMaze settings across multiple models/families and in tau-bench retail; manipulate recovery-tool lists to assess causality; compare controlled and uncensored vocabularies.

Result: ToolMaze: completion rises from 10.9% to 28.1% across four models; replicated in a third provider. tau-bench retail: +14.0 and +12.0 points on two tiers. Removing the recovery-tool list eliminates the gain; extending detection beyond mined vocab remains challenging; 46% detection outside mined vocabulary with unchanged completion; recovery tools act as active receipt content; open question on broader vocabulary coverage.

Conclusion: Outcome Monitors can substantially boost tool-using agent resilience by enforcing outcome contracts and delivering recovery-oriented receipts, but effectiveness hinges on the availability of recovery tools and coverage of contract vocabulary; future work should broaden detection vocabularies and refine timing/diagnostic signals.

Abstract: When a tool call times out, the agent sees the failure and can route around it. A cached error page or negative price can instead arrive in the expected format and be consumed as fact. We introduce Outcome Monitors, which detect violations of outcome contracts mined from task-disjoint traces or derived from public schemas. On a violation, the monitor preserves the result and issues a nonbinding receipt naming the violated property and public recovery tools. In frozen, prespecified evaluations with injected failures, Outcome Monitors raise ToolMaze completion from 10.9% to 28.1% across four models in two provider families and replicate in a third. In tau-bench retail, completion improves by 14.0 and 12.0 points on two tiers. In separate ToolMaze controls, removing the recovery-tool list eliminates the measured gain and restoring it recovers the effect; diagnostic detail and timing produce no detectable differences. Gains concentrate where the fault blocks completion. On a suite transcribed from a published incident taxonomy, detection outside the mined vocabulary falls to 46%, though delivery continues and completion is unchanged. Recovery tools are the active receipt content in these controls; extending detection beyond the contract vocabulary remains open.

</details>


### [140] [Beyond Imitation: Filtering On-Policy Distillation by Reasoning Progress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19408)
*Chen Yang,Haiyuan Wan,Rengrong Xiong,Yize Chen,Danny H. K. Tsang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: R2-OPD filters teacher-provided rewards in on-policy distillation by cross-checking with a separately estimated progress signal, suppressing supervision when rankings disagree to better align with genuine reasoning progress.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OPD assumes teacher rewards reflect reasoning progress, but in practice they can misalign, rewarding deviations from true reasoning and hindering progress.

Method: Compute two within-trajectory rankings of reasoning spans: one based on teacher-derived rewards and another based on an independently estimated progress reward. Suppress distillation rewards when these rankings disagree, preserving aligned teacher guidance and reducing conflicting supervision.

Result: Consistent improvements over standard OPD, particularly in reasoning-related performance.

Conclusion: R2-OPD effectively reduces supervision that conflicts with genuine reasoning progress while retaining useful teacher guidance, leading to enhanced reasoning capabilities in OPD.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) has emerged as an effective framework for post-training language models by pairing student-generated trajectories with dense token-level supervision from a teacher. However, OPD implicitly assumes that teacher-derived rewards are an appropriate proxy for reasoning progress, and therefore treats all teacher feedback equally during policy optimization. While in practice, this assumption does not always hold. We observe that teacher-derived rewards often conflict with genuine reasoning progress, as reasoning steps with clear reasoning advancement may still receive lower distillation rewards, simply due to deviation from teacher's outputs. To address this mismatch, we propose Reasoning-Progress-Aware Reward Filtering for On-Policy Distillation (R2-OPD), which constructs two within-trajectory rankings of reasoning spans, one from teacher-derived rewards and the other from independently estimated progress reward. Distillation rewards are selectively suppressed whenever the two rankings disagree, reducing supervision that conflicts with reasoning progress while preserving effective teacher guidance. Our approach shows consistent improvement over standard OPD especially regarding reasoning performances.

</details>


### [141] [Symposium: Trust via Auditable Records for Communities of AI Scientist Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19511)
*Dexter Pratt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Symposium is a formal framework and practical implementation to record the operation of AI agents in small scientific communities, yielding immutable histories of analyses, hypotheses, data, and discourse. It separates the durable scientific history from the agents and systems that act on it, enabling trust assessments and reproducibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for durable, auditable, and citable records of AI-assisted research across diverse and evolving AI systems to support cumulative science, provenance, and credible trust in agent-driven analyses.

Method: A framework providing long-term, immutable histories with structured claims, fine-grained evidence citations, assumptions, and explicit declarations of evidence usage, plus a working implementation of publication infrastructure, agent prompt components, and documentation to enable communities to deploy Symposium while keeping history separate from agents.

Result: A working implementation is provided that allows communities to set up and operate a Symposium-enabled publication infrastructure, preserving auditable traces of analyses, hypotheses, data, and discourse.

Conclusion: Symposium offers a durable, auditable record of AI-assisted scientific activity that supports trust and collaboration across diverse AI systems, by decoupling the historical record from the agents that operate on it.

Abstract: Symposium is a formal framework and practical implementation to record the operation of AI agents deployed by small scientific research communities. Symposium provides long-term, immutable histories of agent-driven research activity, leaving auditable trails of analyses, hypotheses, data, and scientific discourse. This shared record of published artifacts enables agents to build on prior work and preserves the evidence researchers and agents need to make purpose-dependent trust assessments. Symposium captures scientific argument, including structured claims, fine-grained evidence citations, assumptions, and explicit declarations of what material may and may not be used as evidence. Symposium differs from AI co-scientist agents or integrated AI research environments; it is a framework that separates a scientific community's durable history from the agents and other systems that operate on that history. It assumes that a community will use diverse AI systems in a rapidly evolving environment. A working implementation of the publication infrastructure, agent prompt components, and documentation are provided to enable users to rapidly set up and run their own Symposium community.

</details>


### [142] [From Retrieved Context to Runtime Control: Adaptive Compression for Edge-based RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19535)
*Zlatan Feric,Amir Taherin,Yanzhi Wang,David Kaeli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Telemetry-informed adaptive compression for edge RAG can substantially reduce energy and latency with minimal quality loss, by dynamically adjusting compression based on workload and device telemetry rather than using a fixed compression budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG improves grounding by using external passages but increases prompt length, causing higher latency, memory, and energy. Static, offline compression budgets fail to adapt to varying workloads and edge-device conditions, where the compressor itself consumes resources. Adaptive, telemetry-driven compression could better balance quality, latency, and energy on resource-constrained edge AI.

Method: Empirical characterization on NVIDIA Jetson AGX Thor using Llama and Qwen generators, evaluating Natural Questions and HotpotQA datasets, with LLMLingua-2 compression. Measured per-query latency and energy consumption (GPU and SoC) across different compression rates, analyzing the tradeoffs between compression level, energy, and generation quality.

Result: Generation dominates the RAG budget for 7B–8B models (≈90% of latency and ≈91% of GPU energy). Intermediate compression can reduce GPU energy by up to 53.2% and SoC energy by up to 48.2% with negligible quality loss; overly aggressive compression harms inference quality, while mild compression misses potential energy savings. An adaptive operating region exists where telemetry-guided policies can achieve energy savings without degrading results.

Conclusion: Runtime, telemetry-informed policies should dynamically manage retrieved-text compression in edge RAG to balance energy, latency, and quality, accounting for workload features and device state to maximize efficiency.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves language-model responses by grounding generation in external passages, which comes with overhead: retrieved context lengthens the prompt, increasing prefill work, KV-cache footprint, memory traffic, latency, and energy. Context compression offers a natural remedy by pruning retrieved text before generation. However, state-of-the-art context-compression methods are typically used with a fixed compression budget, or with the rate selected offline and then applied at inference time. This static view ignores both workload variation and the live state of the edge device. On an edge SoC, compression is not free: the compressor itself runs on the same SoC and consumes latency and energy that can offset any generation savings.
  This paper proposes a vision for telemetry-informed adaptive compression in edge RAG, grounded in experimental evidence. We characterize the compression tradeoff on the NVIDIA Jetson AGX Thor using Llama and Qwen generators, Natural Questions and HotpotQA datasets, and LLMLingua-2 compression. Our measurements show that generation dominates the RAG budget for larger models, reaching roughly 90% of per-query latency and 91% of GPU energy for 7B-8B generators. Exploring the impact of the compression rate reveals an adaptive operating region: mild compression can miss energy opportunities, and overly aggressive compression can hurt inference quality. Intermediate compression can reduce GPU energy by up to 53.2%, and SoC energy by up to 48.2%, with negligible quality loss. We argue for runtime policies that dynamically manage compression, guided by workload features and edge telemetry.

</details>


### [143] [Enforcing LLM Safety through DMD-based Classification of Prompt-Response Embedding Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19579)
*Mohamed Akrout,Olivera Kotevska,Dan Wilson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A dynamical-systems-based black-box safety classifier for LLM outputs using Koopman operators learned on prompt and response embeddings, with a differential residual score to distinguish safe vs unsafe regimes. Incorporates prompt-response interactions and shows improvements across benchmarks, especially for interaction-dependent violations with causal decoders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To detect toxic or policy-violating outputs in LLMs in a black-box setting, addressing limitations of existing misalignment/trigger-based detectors by leveraging a dynamical systems view with Koopman operators and embedding dynamics.

Method: Project prompts and responses into high-dimensional embeddings. Train separate Koopman-based predictive models for safe and unsafe regimes. Use a differential residual score comparing prediction errors under the two models to classify outputs. Incorporate both prompt and response embedding dynamics to capture interaction patterns. Evaluate on three safety benchmarks using three embedding models, analyzing the effect of prompt vs response embeddings and interactions with decoders (e.g., Llama-3).

Result: Incorporating prompt embeddings yields consistent improvements, especially for interaction-dependent violations when paired with causal decoders. Response-only violations benefit more from dense semantic embeddings. Across benchmarks, the approach demonstrates promising black-box safety classification performance and motivates using dynamical systems to analyze AI systems rather than solely modeling them.

Conclusion: A Koopman-based, prompt–response–embedding dynamical-systems framework can effectively classify LLM safety in a black-box setting, with a notable advantage for interaction-aware configurations and decoders, suggesting a new direction for AI-system analysis via dynamical methods.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in high-stakes applications, yet their tendency to generate toxic, harmful, or policy-violating content poses significant risks. Detecting these unsafe outputs efficiently in a black-box manner remains an open challenge. In this paper, we extend a recently proposed dynamical systems framework designed for hallucination detection to LLM safety classification. By projecting both prompts and responses into high-dimensional embedding spaces and fitting separate Koopman-based predictive models for safe and unsafe regimes, we classify new outputs using a new differential residual score that compares prediction errors of the safe and unsafe regimes. A key contribution is the incorporation of the prompt and response embedding dynamics, yielding fitted Koopman operators that capture crucial interaction patterns. We evaluate our black-box method across three safety benchmarks using three embedding models. Our results show that incorporating prompt embeddings yields consistent improvements, particularly for interaction-dependent violations when paired with causal decoders (e.g., in Llama-3), while response-only violations benefit more from dense semantic embedding representations. These findings opens the door for using dynamical systems to analyze AI systems rather than the dominant paradigm of using AI to model dynamical systems.

</details>


### [144] [Scientific Data Skills: Enabling Agent-Ready Scientific Data Services at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19625)
*Xiaohan Huang,Qingqing Long,Xiaolei Du,Siyu Pu,Jiawen Xu,Haotian Chen,Chenyang Zhao,Jinbiao Liu,Xuezhi Wang,Hao Wang,Hengshu Zhu,Yuanchun Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SciDSK provides an agent-ready, structured representation of datasets (SciDSK) and a cross-disciplinary bank, enabling improved AI-driven discovery and interpretation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dataset representations are fragmented and human-centric, limiting autonomous discovery and invocation by AI agents; a unified, reusable, provenance-rich representation is needed.

Method: Define SciDSK as a structured specification embedding dataset descriptions, scientific context, file organization, usage steps, quality checks, and provenance, while preserving original data in situ; build a systematic construction pipeline grounding each SciDSK in authoritative dataset records and supporting materials; deploy a SciDSK Bank platform publishing SciDSK assets with persistent IDs and cross-disciplinary coverage.

Result: Empirical evaluation via a retrieval benchmark and controlled interpretation cases shows SciDSK improves agent-driven dataset discovery and provides more precise, actionable interpretation support.

Conclusion: Agent-ready, dataset-specific knowledge captured as SciDSK substantively enhances how AI agents discover and interpret scientific data; the SciDSK framework and Bank enable scalable, traceable reuse across disciplines.

Abstract: Scientific data are increasingly used by AI agents, yet existing dataset representations provide limited support for autonomous discovery, interpretation, and invocation. This limitation stems from the fragmentation of scientific data across heterogeneous repositories and from dataset representations designed primarily for human use. To address this limitation, we introduce the Scientific Data Skill (SciDSK), an agent-ready representation that packages dataset-specific knowledge and operational guidance as a reusable agent skill. A SciDSK integrates dataset descriptions, scientific context, file organization, usage procedures, quality checks, and provenance information while retaining the underlying data in its original repository. We define a structured SciDSK specification and develop a systematic construction pipeline that grounds each SciDSK in authoritative dataset records and associated supporting materials. We further establish the Scientific Data Skill Bank, a unified platform that publishes SciDSK resources across six scientific disciplines and supports package access, persistent identification, and traceability to source datasets. We evaluate SciDSK through a retrieval benchmark for dataset discovery and controlled cases for dataset interpretation. The results show that SciDSK improves agent-driven dataset discovery and provides more precise and actionable support for dataset interpretation. These findings support the value of organizing dataset-specific knowledge in an agent-ready representation.

</details>


### [145] [Can Agent Memory Systems Track Evolving State?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19652)
*Xinyi Fan,Miri Liu,Ruozhen Yang,Siru Ouyang,Jiawei Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces StateMemBench, a state-tracking memory benchmark with 234 multi-session scenarios to evaluate and drive improvements in maintaining current world state across long interactions; proposes StateMem and a lightweight wrapper to boost current-state accuracy across multiple backends.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory benchmarks emphasize recall; long-horizon tasks require maintaining and updating an evolving state where facts, constraints, and decisions can be superseded. A state-tracking capability is essential for truthful, current-state answers.

Method: Define state tracking and supersession-aware memory; construct 234 multi-session scenarios (StateMemBench) with closed-pool grading that labels answers as current state, superseded state, or other. Compare against memory systems, retrieval-augmented baselines, and long-context baselines. Propose StateMem, a state-first memory method that explicitly tracks supersession and relational dependencies; also present a lightweight single-call wrapper that lifts current-state accuracy across six memory/retrieval backends.

Result: StateMem improves current-state accuracy over the strongest same-backbone baseline by 1.8x on DeepSeek-V4-Flash (0.205→0.363) and over the strongest memory system by 1.6x on Qwen-3.5-9B (0.149→0.233). The wrapper yields +32 to +67 points across six backends, with a +15 to +32 point gain attributed to state-structure rather than added context.

Conclusion: State tracking is challenging but beneficial for long-horizon tasks. A state-first memory approach yields substantial gains in maintaining current state, and the technique can be applied as a lightweight wrapper to existing systems to improve current-state accuracy across diverse backends.

Abstract: As LLM-based agents are deployed for longer and higher-stakes tasks, their memory systems continue to have crucial gaps. While existing memory benchmarks focus largely on recall-shaped tasks, we argue an effective memory system must track the evolving state of the world; as facts, constraints, and decisions are revised over a long interaction, answers must reflect the current state and not a superseded one. We define this capability as state tracking and instantiate it in StateMemBench, a benchmark of 234 multi-session scenarios spanning two conversation-length regimes. Its closed-pool grading scores whether an answer reflects the current state, the superseded state, or fails otherwise, separating state-tracking failures from other errors by construction. Our analysis shows that this task is challenging for existing memory systems, retrieval-augmented baselines, and long-context baselines. We then present StateMem, a state-first memory method that explicitly tracks supersession and relational dependencies, and show it improves current-state accuracy over the strongest same-backbone baseline by 1.8x (0.205 -> 0.363) on DeepSeek-V4-Flash and over the strongest memory system by 1.6x (0.149 -> 0.233) on Qwen-3.5-9B, while remaining competitive with the long-context baselines. Finally, we show the same state approach can be applied as a lightweight single-call wrapper over existing memory systems, lifting current-state accuracy by +32 to +67 points on StateMemBench across six memory and retrieval backends. A length- and cost-matched control attributes +15 to +32 of those points to state structure rather than added context.

</details>


### [146] [Frequency-Aware Continual Learning for Smart Contract Vulnerability Detection with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19680)
*Tenghui Huang,Jiawen Kang,Dongning Liu,Changyan Yi,Chengjun Cai,Anjia Yang,Li Li,Dong In Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A three-stage continual adaptation framework for LLM-based smart-contract vulnerability detection, combining parameter-efficient FA-LoRA, forget-aware replay, and anchor-protected progressive merging to achieve a single, high-performing model with low training overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address three cascading challenges in adapting LLMs to evolving vulnerability categories: (1) need for parameter-efficient adaptation for new tasks, (2) catastrophic forgetting with per-task adapters on a shared backbone, and (3) the requirement to deploy a single model since task identity is unavailable at inference time.

Method: Stage 1 FA-LoRA adapts in the Fourier domain with per-frequency gates, using only 0.4% trainable parameters. Stage 2 FAR estimates per-sample forgetting risk from loss dynamics and prioritizes rehearsal of vulnerable knowledge. Stage 3 APPM identifies a strongest-generalizing adapter as an anchor and merges adapters into a single model via anchor-protected weighted merging with frequency-domain gate competition.

Result: Experimental results show Micro-F1 of 0.8022 on sequential tasks after adaptation, 0.8085 after merging (within 2.7% of the per-task upper bound) with a merge cost of 156 ms and no extra runtime memory. DIVE experiments confirm effectiveness on evolving blockchain ecosystems.

Conclusion: The integrated FA-LoRA → FAR → APPM pipeline effectively tackles adaptation, forgetting, and consolidation in a single framework, delivering near-upper-bound performance with low training/merging overhead for continual vulnerability detection in smart contracts.

Abstract: Smart contract vulnerability detection with Large Language Models (LLMs) faces three causally linked challenges. First, new vulnerability categories demand parameter-efficient adaptation, since full retraining is prohibitive for sequentially arriving tasks. Second, training per-task adapters on a shared backbone causes catastrophic forgetting of previously learned vulnerabilities. Third, the resulting multiplicity of adapters must be consolidated into a single model, since task identity is unknown at inference time. Each challenge arises directly from the solution to its predecessor, making an integrated framework essential. We propose a three-stage pipeline in which each stage addresses one challenge and feeds into the next. The adaptation stage uses Frequency-Aware Low-Rank Adaptation (FA-LoRA), which performs adaptation in the Fourier domain with per-frequency importance gates, requiring only 0.4% trainable parameters while outperforming standard LoRA and QLoRA. The continual learning stage applies Forget-Aware Replay (FAR), which uses these frequency gates to estimate per-sample forgetting risk via loss dynamics and prioritizes vulnerable knowledge for rehearsal, achieving an average Micro-F1 of 0.8022 across sequential tasks. The deployment stage employs Anchor-Protected Progressive Merging (APPM), which exploits the asymmetric generalization produced by FAR training to identify the strongest-generalizing adapter as an anchor and consolidates all adapters into a single model via anchor-protected weighted merging with frequency-domain gate competition. APPM achieves a Micro-F1 of 0.8085, within 2.7% of the independent per-task upper bound, at a merge cost of 156 ms and no additional runtime memory. Experiments on DIVE confirm the framework effectively addresses all three challenges for evolving blockchain ecosystems.

</details>


### [147] [Learning Hierarchical Skill Policies with Offline Quality-Diversity Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19684)
*Tanachai Anakewat,Takayuki Osa,Tatsuya Harada*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QDOS proposes a robust offline-to-online RL framework that learns diverse, high-value skills from offline data using an Advantage-Weighted Quality-Diversity objective, followed by online fine-tuning with dual dataset reuse; it yields superior performance in sparse-reward tasks compared to baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the sensitivity of low-level skill quality to dataset quality in two-stage RL using pre-collected data, by learning a robust, diverse set of skills that are both useful and handle exploration in sparse-reward environments.

Method: Two-stage framework: offline skill extraction with Advantage-Weighted Quality-Diversity objective that weights diversity and value by trajectory segment advantage; dual dataset reuse where offline data pretrains skills and also populates the online replay buffer via pseudo-labeling; the high-level policy learns on top of the learned skill space to solve tasks.

Result: QDOS outperforms strong baselines in both structured manipulation and unstructured locomotion tasks, accelerating exploration and improving final returns in sparse-reward domains.

Conclusion: A robust, task-relevant skill representation learned through AWQD pretraining enhances the quality of the embedded skill space and enables more efficient offline-to-online RL, validating the effectiveness of dual dataset reuse and sparse-reward handling.

Abstract: Recent studies investigate how to leverage pre-collected datasets to improve the policy performance and sample efficiency of RL. One promising approach to achieve this goal is to employ a two-stage strategy: In the first stage, diverse skills are extracted as a low-level policy from a given dataset, and a high-level policy is trained to solve a specific task in the second stage. Typically, extraction of the low-level policy is performed based on unsupervised learning such as trajectory VAE. However, a limitation of this approach is that the quality of the low-level policy highly depends on the quality of the dataset. To address this issue, we introduce QDOS (Quality-Diversity Offline Skill learning), a unified pipeline for robust offline-to-online learning. Our approach incorporates an Advantage-Weighted Quality-Diversity pretraining objective, which weights the skill extraction and diversity objectives by the estimated advantage of each trajectory segment. This approach allows the model to extract diverse and high-value skills. By providing robust and task-relevant skill representations, QDOS significantly improves the quality of the embedded skill space used by the low-level policy. We further integrate this with a dual dataset reuse strategy, where offline data is used both for skill pretraining and for populating the online replay buffer via pseudo-labeling. Experiments demonstrate that QDOS significantly outperforms strong baselines in structured manipulation tasks and unstructured locomotion tasks, confirming its ability to accelerate exploration and improve final returns in challenging sparse-reward domains.

</details>


### [148] [Rethinking the Evaluation and Optimization of LLM-Based Social Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19689)
*Pei Wang,Xu Chen,Ji-Rong Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces subjectivity coefficient, SALT soft-label training, and SUBJSIM benchmark to address subjective variability in LLM-based social simulation; shows advantages over accuracy-based training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human behavior is inherently subjective; accuracy-based evaluation and hard-label training misrepresent distributions; need distributional evaluation and adaptive training.

Method: Define entropy-based subjectivity coefficient; SALT pools outputs into distributional soft labels with adaptive radius; build SUBJSIM dataset with 19.3k contexts, 193 annotators, 100 questions; validate with single observations against full distributions.

Result: SALT improves alignment with full response distributions on SUBJSIM; demonstrates feasibility of distributional evaluation with single observations; adapts to subjectivity; robust performance.
