Conclusion: ExPhy and PhyODE enable joint evaluation of trajectory forecasting and physical-property estimation, highlighting the importance of explicit physical-property interfaces and revealing gaps between trajectory fidelity and underlying physics; they also support cross-benchmark transfer.

Abstract: Understanding object dynamics requires not only predicting future trajectories but also examining whether a model captures the physical properties that govern motion. However, existing benchmarks rarely expose object-level physical properties as explicit evaluation targets alongside trajectory forecasting. To address this gap, we introduce \emph{ExPhy}, a multi-object trajectory forecasting benchmark containing 24,000 simulated physical scenes with explicit object-level labels for mass, friction, and restitution. ExPhy provides observed and future trajectories together with an in-distribution (ID) split and two out-of-distribution (OOD) splits over physical parameters (OOD-Parameter) and initial states (OOD-Initial) for jointly evaluating trajectory forecasting and physical property estimation. We further instantiate \textsc{PhyODE}, a physics-guided model with an explicit property interface that estimates physical properties from observed trajectories and uses them for differentiable future rollout. On the long-horizon OOD-Initial setting, \textsc{PhyODE} reduces ADE and FDE by 33.1\% and 31.0\%, respectively, compared with the strongest baseline. Zero-shot evaluation on ComPhy further assesses cross-benchmark transfer. Property-level analyses reveal that accurate trajectory forecasting does not necessarily imply accurate recovery of the underlying physical properties. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Zest86/ExPhy.

</details>


### [174] [Manifold Drift in Flow Preference Optimization: A Root Cause of Reward Hacking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20011)
*Yansen Han,Shengyi Liao,Yuanxing Zhang,Pengfei Wan,Tao Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ThermoDPO introduces a temperature-controlled objective for preference-based fine-tuning of continuous-time generative models to prevent manifold drift in flow matching; it provides ThermoDPO and a weighted variant that anchor updates to preferred samples, bridging rejection sampling fine-tuning and FlowDPO, with empirical gains on toy and SD3.5-M benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preference-based optimization for alignment in continuous-time dynamics can move samples off the pretrained data manifold (manifold drift). A mechanism is needed to anchor updates to the pretrained manifold while preserving beneficial preference-driven updates.

Method: Define ThermoDPO with a temperature parameter that controls how strongly preferences are anchored to preferred samples. Establish connections between rejection sampling fine-tuning and FlowDPO across temperatures. Introduce ThermoDPO-weighted to counteract diminished signals at low temperature via a weighted objective and a surrogate for the manifold distance (pointwise).

Result: ThermoDPO-weighted achieves higher task scores: on the main toy benchmark, StrictScore 0.899 (vs FlowDPO 0.629; FlowDPO+RFT 0.857). On SD3.5-M with CFG=4.5, OCR improves by 47.5% and the average of four metrics improves by 16.0%.

Conclusion: ThermoDPO effectively anchors preference-based updates to the pretrained data manifold across temperature regimes, mitigating manifold drift and offering a practical, robust alternative that outperforms FlowDPO variants in both toy and SD3.5-M benchmarks; the weighted variant enhances stability at low temperatures.

Abstract: Preference optimization is a standard alignment method for generative models, yet extending it to continuous-time dynamics remains non-trivial. In flow matching, reward-driven updates modify transport trajectories without an inherent constraint to the pretrained data manifold and can move terminal samples off the pretrained support. We formalize this failure mode as manifold drift. Theoretically, we show that optimal flow matching recovers the terminal data distribution, whereas a preference update leaves the pretrained manifold whenever its induced terminal displacement has a nonzero normal component. As a remedy, we propose ThermoDPO, a temperature-controlled objective that anchors pairwise preference optimization on preferred samples. Across temperature regimes, this objective connects rejection sampling fine-tuning and FlowDPO and controls a pointwise reconstruction-based surrogate for manifold distance. To counteract diminished signals at low temperatures, we further introduce a weighted variant, ThermoDPO-weighted. On the main toy benchmark, ThermoDPO-weighted attains a StrictScore of 0.899, compared with 0.629 for FlowDPO and 0.857 for FlowDPO+RFT. On SD3.5-M at CFG = 4.5, it improves OCR by 47.5% and the average of four metrics by 16.0%.

</details>


### [175] [Contrastive Mixed Prompt Learning for Incomplete Multimodal Sentiment Analysis with Unseen Modality Combination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20019)
*Kaixin Xu,NaiJin Liu,Yulin Kang,Tangyue Jin,Zixuan Yu,Wenxi Zhao,Yibei Liu,Qianle Zhang,Yangyang Wu,Mengying Zhu,Meng Xi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CMPL tackles IMSAUMC by using label-guided contrastive learning and modality-combination prompts with a soft router to generalize to unseen modality combinations, achieving significant accuracy gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Incomplete multimodal sentiment analysis often encounters unseen modality combinations during testing, causing poor generalization; existing methods assume missing-at-random patterns or fixed missingness, failing in real-world scenarios.

Method: CMPL introduces (1) label-guided contrastive feature learning for robust cross-modal representations, (2) modality-combination prompts with a soft router to handle various modality combinations, and (3) three prompt contrastive learning strategies to learn prompts for unseen modality combinations.

Result: Experiments on three datasets show CMPL improves accuracy by more than 5% over state-of-the-art methods.

Conclusion: CMPL enhances generalization to unseen modality combinations in IMSAUMC, demonstrating strong performance gains and offering a viable approach for real-world incomplete multimodal sentiment analysis.

Abstract: Incomplete multimodal sentiment analysis has garnered significant attention in recent years. Existing approaches typically assume that data is missing at random or are designed specifically for certain missing patterns, ignoring the modality combination inconsistency between training and testing phases. However, in real-world scenarios, the testing phase often encounters modal combinations that were not present during the training phase, which leads to insufficient generalization capabilities and unstable performance. In this paper, we introduce the problem of Incomplete Multimodal Sentiment Analysis with Unseen Modality Combinations (IMSAUMC), aiming to enhance model generalization for unseen modality combinations. To address this challenge, we propose the model named $\textbf{C}$ontrastive $\textbf{M}$ixed $\textbf{P}$rompt $\textbf{L}$earning ($\textsf{CMPL}$) for IMSAUMC. It introduces a label-guided contrastive feature learning mechanism to learn robust and discriminative cross-modal representations. Additionally, we design modality-combination prompts with a soft router to facilitate better learning of various modality combinations. Furthermore, we introduce three prompt contrastive learning strategies, which enable effective learning of prompts corresponding to unseen modality combinations, thereby significantly strengthening the model's generalization capabilities in diverse testing scenarios. Extensive experiments on three widely used datasets demonstrate that $\textsf{CMPL}$ achieves more than a 5% improvement in accuracy compared to state-of-the-art approaches.

</details>


### [176] [A three-dimensional typology of agency for advanced AI systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20041)
*Willem Fourie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A three-dimensional typology of agency for frontier AI systems is proposed, with dimensions: nature (moral vs legal), mode (individual vs collective), and locus (human vs non-human). This yields eight instantiations, classified as conventional, contested, or controversial. The framework separates legal from moral agency and accommodates non-human/legal agency without assuming AI are moral agents, aiding attribution in instrumental AI action.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is no comprehensive framework to classify the type of agency instantiated by advanced AI, especially for non-moral forms. Existing work concentrates on normative agency and on agentic AIs, but lacks a typology that addresses how agency is instantiated across moral/legal dimensions, modes of agency, and loci.

Method: Theoretical synthesis drawing on philosophy, ethics, legal theory, and sociology to define three dimensions of agency, derive eight possible instantiations, and classify them as conventional, contested, or controversial.

Result: A structured typology of frontier AI agency with three axes (nature, mode, locus) yielding eight instantiations; a taxonomic separation between legal and moral agency; recognition of non-human/non-human-centered agency; a framework to discuss attribution without presuming AI as moral agents.

Conclusion: The typology clarifies how agency can be distributed across humans, legal entities, and non-human actors, informing governance and accountability by enabling analysis of when actions are attributable to particular actors, even when instrumental goals blur responsibility.

Abstract: Research on the agency of advanced artificial intelligence (AI) systems focuses on agency as a normative concept and on the agency of particularly agentic AI systems. While recent work also focuses on the different profiles of agentic systems, no framework exists to address the question of the type of agency instantiated by advanced AI systems, particularly when considering non-moral forms of agency. Based on established theoretical positions in philosophy, ethics, legal theory and sociology, we develop a typology of agency for frontier AI systems consisting of three dimensions: the nature of agency (moral or legal), its mode (individual or collective) and its locus (human or non-human). Combining these dimensions produces eight possible instantiations of agency, which we classify as conventional, contested or controversial. The typology separates legal from moral agency and thereby creates conceptual space for considering individual, legal, non-human agency without presupposing that advanced AI systems are moral agents. We argue that this distinction is increasingly relevant where instrumental goal pursuit complicates the attribution of AI actions to particular human actors.

</details>


### [177] [On the Applicability of Safety Nets: A Safety-By-Design Solution for Certifying Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20053)
*Johann Maximilian Christensen,Thomas Stefani,Elena Hoemann,Frank Köster,Sven Hallerbach*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Safety nets combining compact neural networks with lookup tables can achieve 100% correctness on a discretized input space while dramatically reducing system size; architectures with 3–5 hidden layers of ~50–100 nodes and one-hot encoding perform best, achieving ~97% neural-network data coverage with LUTs handling the rest.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Certification challenges for AI in safety-critical aviation systems; EASA Safety-by-Design requires guaranteed behavior; existing Safety Nets have not been comprehensively evaluated for design trade-offs; the study aims to systematically characterize NN vs LUT size trade-offs to guide certifiable implementations.

Method: Systematic empirical analysis comparing diverse neural-network architectures and lookup-table configurations; evaluate trade-offs between storage/memory and certification compliance across a discretized input domain; measure NN coverage and total accuracy; identify design parameters that minimize memory while ensuring 100% correct outputs on the discretized domain.

Result: Architectures with 3–5 hidden layers, each with ~50–100 nodes, combined with one-hot encoding, offer best balance. Neural networks cover at least ~97% of data; remaining errors are captured by compact lookup tables. Overall system size reduces by ~three orders of magnitude, fitting current avionics memory budgets and delivering 100% correct outputs across the discretized input space to meet EASA requirements.

Conclusion: First open-source implementation of Safety Nets for HCAS and VCAS with replicable results; demonstrates a practical, certifiable Safety-by-Design pathway for AI in aviation and positions Safety Nets as a viable solution for safety-critical applications.

Abstract: The integration of Artificial Intelligence (AI) in safety-critical aviation systems presents significant challenges for certification and deployment. Aviation, often regarded as the safest form of transportation, relies on numerous safety-critical systems. For future safety-critical AI-based systems, EASA requires a Safety-by-Design approach, which can be achieved by using Safety Nets that combine neural network compression with lookup tables to ensure 100 % correct runtime behavior across the discretized operational design domain. Although Safety Nets have been studied, no comprehensive study of their performance characteristics and system design trade-offs has been conducted. This work presents the first systematic analysis of the trade-off between neural network and lookup table size in Safety Nets. By systematically comparing neural networks with diverse architectures, this study identifies optimal design parameters that minimize overall storage and memory requirements while maintaining certification compliance. Results demonstrate that architectures with 3 to 5 hidden layers, each with approximately 50 to 100 nodes, combined with one-hot encoding, achieve the best balance. In these configurations, neural networks accurately represent at least 97 % of the data, while compact lookup tables handle the remaining errors. The resulting Safety Nets reduce the system size by almost three orders of magnitude, fitting within the memory budget of current avionics hardware while guaranteeing 100 % correct outputs across the entire discretized input space, as required by EASA guidelines. This work provides the first-ever open-source implementation of Safety Nets for HCAS and VCAS with replicable results, demonstrating a practical pathway toward certifiable AI-based systems in aviation and establishing Safety Nets as a viable Safety-by-Design solution for safety-critical applications.

</details>


### [178] [What You Can't See Is What You Learn: Restricted Evidence Visibility Favors Compositional Generalization in Shared-Genome Language-Model Societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20054)
*Narcis Marincat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Restricting evidence visibility in multi-module systems can improve gradient-based composition generalization, but preregistered tests fail to meet performance floors; restricted-global pair comparisons reveal robust, reusable interfaces and sensitivity to communication channels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether limiting information flow between modules affects the solutions discovered by gradient-based training, particularly for compositional tasks requiring inter-module interaction.

Method: Construct four-cell societies sharing a frozen pretrained language model and a low-rank adapter, connected via two fixed-width continuous relay vectors. Train ten matched restricted/global model pairs with identical initializations, training order, token layouts, parameters, and computation; only the attention-mask differs. Evaluate on a prospective NL function-composition task, across depth levels, and perform ablation analyses (removing communication, packet transplants, counterfactuals).

Result: Restricted societies outperform global twins in 9/10 pairs at both depths (median gains ~0.765 and ~0.605). Cutting communication drops restricted systems to chance; depth-3 advantage persists (0.558) even for unseen composite functions. Across six restricted configurations, same-value packets reliably preserve behavior (0.94–1.00); destructive interventions collapse performance; counterfactual packets steer outputs to mathematically predicted answers. The top-performing global model also relies on communication, but its same-value packets are not interchangeable across episodes. Restricted visibility is not required for composition and can increase the likelihood of a generalizable relay and a reusable, value-indexed interface.

Conclusion: Under this protocol, restricted visibility enhances compositional generalization and interface reuse, but preregistered results fail to surpass the pre-specified performance floor; overall, restricted evidence flow helps learn reusable interfaces, though not universally sufficient for task gates and ordinary-language preservation.

Abstract: Multi-module systems often expose every module to the full input. We test whether restricting evidence visibility changes which solutions gradient-based training discovers. Four-cell societies share one frozen pretrained language model and one low-rank adapter, communicating only through two model-width continuous vectors in a fixed relay. On a prospectively sealed natural-language function-composition task, we train ten matched restricted/global pairs sharing initialization bytes, training order, token layout, parameters, and computation; only the attention mask differs. Restricted societies outperform their globally visible twins by at least 20 points at both depths in 9 of 10 pairs, with median paired advantages of 0.7648 and 0.6050. Cutting communication reduces every restricted society to chance, and the depth-three advantage remains 0.558 on programs whose composite function never appeared in training. Across six audited restricted societies, same-value packet transplants preserve behavior at 0.94-1.00 across all tested interfaces; destructive interventions collapse performance; and counterfactual packets redirect outputs toward the mathematically predicted answer. The sole high-performing global model also requires communication, but its same-value packets are not interchangeable across episodes. Restricted visibility is thus not necessary for composition; under this protocol it substantially increases the probability of a generalizing relay and favors a reusable, value-indexed interface. The complete preregistered battery nevertheless formally fails because restricted-arm median depth-three accuracy is 0.6988, below the 0.70 floor. An earlier qualification cohort likewise yielded 0/10 complete passes: one model met every task-performance gate, but all ten failed ordinary-language preservation, confining the system to explicitly task-gated use.

</details>


### [179] [DARS: Dual-Level Credit Assignment RL with Structured Reasoning for Instruction-Based Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20161)
*Haoxiang Cao,Jiajiong Cao,Xuanpu Zhang,Changqian Yu,Chaoqun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DARS is a reinforcement learning framework for dual-level credit assignment in instruction-based image editing, combining a planner and renderer in a vision-language diffusion setup, with multi-level rollouts and a four-field planner output to localize supervision; it outperforms a Joint RL baseline, especially on reasoning-heavy edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training solely with final-image rewards is inefficient for planner-renderer pipelines because it obscures where optimization should focus (planner vs. renderer) and makes localization of reasoning traces difficult.
This work seeks to decompose credit across modules and provide localized feedback to improve learning efficiency and interpretability.

Method: DARS performs dual-level credit assignment across planner and renderer using multi-plan and multi-render rollouts to estimate between-plan and within-plan reward variability for soft module routing. It uses an adaptive curriculum based on rollout mean rewards. Within the planner, a four-field structured reasoning output enables a prefix-gated reward and token-level advantage reweighting, converting outcome-level feedback into localized supervision.

Result: Experiments on five benchmarks show DARS outperforms a Joint RL baseline using the same backbone, data, reward model, and rollout budget, with the largest gains on reasoning-intensive edits.

Conclusion: DARS demonstrates effective dual-level credit assignment for instruction-based image editing, enabling better credit localization and more efficient learning, particularly for edits requiring complex reasoning.

Abstract: Instruction-based image editing uses a planner-renderer pipeline: a vision-language model (VLM) first converts the instruction into an edit plan, and a diffusion model then executes that plan. Training such systems with only final-image rewards is inefficient because a poor edit does not reveal whether additional optimization should place more emphasis on the planner or the renderer, and even planner-dominant cases remain difficult to localize within a free-form reasoning trace. We present DARS, a reinforcement learning framework for dual-level credit assignment in this two-stage setting. Across modules, multi-plan multi-render rollouts estimate between-plan and within-plan reward variability for soft module routing, while rollout mean rewards provide hardness estimates for an adaptive curriculum. Within the planner, a four-field structured reasoning output enables a prefix-gated reward and token-level advantage reweighting, turning outcome-level feedback into localized supervision. Experiments on five benchmarks show that DARS outperforms a Joint~RL baseline with the same backbone, data, reward model, and rollout budget, with the largest gains on reasoning-intensive edits.

</details>


### [180] [The Third Restructuring of Software Form: From the Three-Tier Architecture to Storage, Models, and Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20201)
*Wei Lin,Tao Zhou,Zhaofei Xie,Changgui Hong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The abstract advances Software 3.0: a context- and reasoning-driven paradigm that converges on three elements—a unified persistent database, a large model, and an agent—reorganizing UI/logic/data layers and outlining applicability conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecast the next software paradigm after Software 2.0 and provide a formalized architecture with boundaries for when it holds.

Method: Formalize the convergence thesis; propose a minimal reference architecture; present prototypes and live-model evidence; analyze determinism, cost, security, and verifiability; specify domains where it applies (expressible, verifiable, externally stateful, tool-complete).

Result: Proposes a triad of components (database, large model, agent) and a re-partitioned three-tier architecture where UI is model-generated, logic is partitioned by expressibility x criticality, and data becomes the sole persistent layer; outlines conditions under which the thesis holds and its boundaries.

Conclusion: The thesis holds in specific task domains and will reshape roles in software development, the database industry, and software engineering, while recognizing limitations and areas for future work.

Abstract: Software form has undergone two paradigm shifts since its inception: Software 1.0, in which instructions determine behavior, and Software 2.0, in which data determines behavior (machine learning). This paper argues that a third shift - Software 3.0, in which context and reasoning determine behavior - is now underway, and contends that its terminal form converges to three elements: a generalized database (the unified abstraction of all persistent state and memory), a large model (the intelligence core that performs reasoning and generation), and an agent (the execution loop connecting the first two). The core argument is as follows: in the traditional three-tier architecture, the user-interface layer will be absorbed by the model's ability to generate interfaces on demand, the business-logic layer will be re-partitioned along "expressibility x criticality" into model reasoning and storage constraints (with residual deterministic logic retained as tools), and only the data layer will be elevated into the sole persistent infrastructure. We formalize this convergence thesis, present a minimal reference architecture, report evidence from real prototypes and a live model, and systematically analyze both the conditions under which it holds and the boundaries where it fails - determinism, cost, security, and verifiability delimit the thesis's domain of applicability. We argue that the thesis holds in task domains that are expressible, verifiable, externally stateful, and tool-complete, and that it will reshape the roles of developers, the database industry, and the software-engineering discipline.

</details>


### [181] [MemTrapBench: Benchmarking Cognitive Traps in LLM Memory Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20202)
*Mengru Wang,Haozhe Luo,Zhenqian Xu,Zhixiang Cui,Haoming Xu,Qu Yang,Jizhan Fang,Junfeng Fang,Ningyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MemTrapBench reveals that memory retrieval can trigger cognitive traps in LLMs—Reasoning Fixation and Belief Distortion—leading to degraded performance, and AdaptiveMem at inference time mitigates these traps while maintaining or improving standard memory benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although memory augments LLM capabilities, current benchmarks emphasize retrieval correctness rather than how memories influence reasoning and beliefs. There is a need for systematic evaluation of memory-induced cognitive traps and their impact on task performance.

Method: The paper introduces MemTrapBench to characterize two cognitive traps: Reasoning Fixation and Belief Distortion. It evaluates two model families and five memory frameworks and then proposes AdaptiveMem, an inference-time method that instructs LLMs to avoid memory traps. Experiments show memory strategies often underperform the no-memory baseline, while AdaptiveMem mitigates traps and preserves or improves standard benchmarks across frameworks.

Result: MemTrapBench proves challenging: all memory-based strategies underperform the no-memory setting, with even the strongest methods causing more than 10% performance drops. AdaptiveMem mitigates cognitive traps and either preserves or improves performance on standard memory benchmarks across diverse memory frameworks.

Conclusion: Memory-augmented LLMs are susceptible to cognitive traps that can harm current-task performance. Inference-time strategies like AdaptiveMem can effectively mitigate these traps while maintaining or enhancing performance on standard memory benchmarks.

Abstract: Memory has become a key component of large language models, enabling them to retain information and learn from long-term interactions. However, existing memory benchmarks mainly evaluate whether information is correctly extracted, stored, and retrieved, while largely overlooking how retrieved memories reshape model reasoning and affect performance on the current task. We identify memory-induced cognitive traps: even faithfully recorded and semantically relevant memories can distort model reasoning or beliefs and degrade current task performance. To systematically evaluate these failure modes, we introduce MemTrapBench, which covers two forms of cognitive traps: Reasoning Fixation and Belief Distortion. Experiments across two model families and five representative memory frameworks show that MemTrapBench is challenging: all evaluated memory strategies underperform the no-memory setting, with even the strongest methods suffering drops of more than 10%. To mitigate these cognitive traps, we propose AdaptiveMem, a simple yet effective inference-time method that instructs LLMs to avoid memory traps. AdaptiveMem mitigates cognitive traps on MemTrapBench while preserving or improving performance on standard memory benchmarks across diverse memory frameworks.

</details>


### [182] [ContractScrub: A benchmark for final review of legal contracts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20204)
*Yejin Bang,Kirsty Fielding,Brandan Oliver,Brian Birke,Nabeel Seedat,Andrew M. Bean*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces ContractScrub, a domain-specific benchmark for evaluating LLMs on contract scrubbing. Frontier models show limited capability (best macro recall ≈0.75), highlighting gaps between general benchmarks and real-world legal text processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legal work involves extensive text processing; contract scrubbing is routine but error-prone and benefits from automation. There is a lack of formal evaluations of LLMs on contract scrubbing, despite its potential impact.

Method: ContractScrub comprises contracts handcrafted by experienced lawyers with diverse error categories (misuse of defined terms, incorrect references, inconsistent language). Evaluation uses macro-average recall to assess model performance across categories, testing frontier LLMs on long-context reasoning and NER-like tasks.

Result: Frontier models perform poorly overall; only one model reaches ~0.75 macro average recall, failing to meet expectations set by related general benchmarks.

Conclusion: Domain-specific benchmarks are essential to measure real-world impact; current frontier models have practical limits for contract scrubbing, underscoring the need for targeted datasets and potentially specialized models or training to bridge the gap.

Abstract: Legal work, with its heavy reliance on processing large amounts of text, is often considered one of the domains most exposed to the use of LLMs. Contract ``scrubbing,'' the final review of transactional agreements for errors and inconsistencies, is a particularly suitable task for automation, because it is routine, painstaking work requiring detailed attention to long documents. Scrubbing also seems to align naturally with the general capabilities expected of frontier LLMs around long-context reasoning, consistency checking, and named entity recognition (NER). Despite the economic value and potential for automation, no formal evaluations of LLMs performing contract scrubbing have been conducted. We introduce ContractScrub, the first benchmark designed to evaluate contract scrubbing capabilities, comprising contracts hand-crafted by experienced lawyers over diverse error categories such as misuse of defined terms, incorrect references, and inconsistent language. Frontier models perform surprisingly poorly with only one model reaching 0.75 macro average recall despite strong performance on seemingly related general benchmarks, demonstrating the practical limits of current models and the importance of narrowly targeted, domain-specific benchmarks for measuring real-world impact.

</details>


### [183] [Electronic Navigational Chart Change Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20218)
*Jacob Arndt,Abhishek Potnis,Alexandre Sorokine*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes ML-based automated classification of ENC changes using a structured encoding with spatial context and attribute embeddings; achieves high accuracy on two operational datasets, showing viability and a baseline for advanced spatial representation learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automate ENC change risk assessment to reduce manual review bottlenecks and inconsistencies, improving maritime safety.

Method: Encodes vector chart changes into a tabular format using a spatial context encoder to capture surrounding features and an ENC attribute encoder for nuanced attribute-value descriptions, then trains gradient-boosted trees.

Result: On two datasets (1,308 chart pairs; >100,000 modifications), tuned gradient-boosted trees achieved 90% and 94% accuracy, outperforming encodings without spatial context/attribute embeddings by 5-7%.

Conclusion: ML integration is viable for ENC maintenance; simple spatial aggregation methods are effective and provide a baseline for exploring more sophisticated spatial representation learning.

Abstract: Electronic Navigational Charts (ENCs) are geospatial vector datasets used in maritime navigation systems that represent hydrographic and navigational information such as depths, navigational aids, traffic schemes, and hazards. A major challenge for hydrographic offices is determining whether a given chart change poses a critical or non-critical risk to maritime safety. Existing workflows rely heavily on manual review and verification, which is labor-intensive, scales poorly with the volume of incoming chart updates, and introduces inter-analyst inconsistencies. To address this challenge, we propose a method for automated classification of ENC changes. We establish a baseline encoding scheme to translate complex vector data changes into a structured tabular format for classification models. The two crucial components of the encoding scheme include a spatial context encoder to enrich the change representations with surrounding geographic features, and an ENC attribute encoder to represent nuanced attribute-value descriptions of the modified objects. We evaluate the proposed approach across two distinct operational datasets, comprising 1,308 chart pairs containing over 100,000 individual chart modifications. Tuned gradient-boosted trees leveraging the proposed encoding schemes achieve accuracies of 90% and 94% on the two datasets, yielding a 5-7% improvement over default hyperparameterized models trained on encodings without spatial context and attribute embeddings. These results demonstrate the viability of integrating machine learning into operational geospatial pipelines to improve ENC maintenance and enhance maritime safety. Finally, our experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of simple location and spatial aggregation methods, providing a foundation for evaluating more sophisticated spatial representation learning techniques for this application.

</details>


### [184] [InsufficiencyBench: Evaluating LLM legal advice on underspecified user queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20220)
*Samuel J. Vincent,Daniel Calloway,Fangyi Yu,Andrew M. Bean,Nabeel Seedat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces InsufficiencyBench, a legal-IR benchmark for query-side insufficiency evaluating whether models detect missing legally material information, identify what's missing, and avoid premature answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing legal-AC benchmarks assume fully specified queries; real users omit material facts, causing risk of incorrect outcomes; need evaluation of model's recognition and handling of insufficiency.

Method: Taxonomy of eight missing-element categories across three failure modes (switch, gating, fatal prerequisite); 202 items (58 base + 144 deficient variants) across six legal domains and 24 US jurisdictions; annotated by practicing attorneys; evaluated ten frontier models.

Result: No model surpasses F2=0.46 on missing-element identification; median recall 0.44; models hedge or answer despite missing data; none both identify deficits and address complete queries effectively.

Conclusion: Demonstrates substantial gap in current models' ability to detect and properly handle insufficient queries; suggests future work on explicit recognition of insufficiency and safe response behavior, and broader coverage across jurisdictions/domains.

Abstract: Legal AI systems are increasingly used to answer legal questions, yet existing benchmarks assume queries arrive fully specified. In practice, users omit facts that materially determine the legal outcome. We introduce InsufficiencyBench, the first legal benchmark targeting query-side insufficiency: whether a model recognizes when a query lacks legally material information, identifies what is missing, and refrains from premature conclusions. We formalize a taxonomy of eight canonical missing-element categories across three structural failure modes---switch, gating, and fatal prerequisite--- and construct 202 benchmark items (58 base queries, 144 deficient variants) spanning six legal domains and 24 US jurisdictions and annotated by practising attorneys. Evaluating ten frontier models, we find that no model exceeds F2 = 0.46 on missing-element identification and that the median recall is 0.44. Models either hedge indiscriminately or answer silently under fabricated presumptions. No model both identifies and qualifies responses to deficient queries while directly addressing complete ones.

</details>


### [185] [Rule-Compliant Visual Spatial Planning for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20237)
*Yu Chen,Ting Lei,Yaoyi Li,Jia Cai,Zhecen Wu,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RuleMaze introduces a controllable benchmark for rule-compliant spatial planning in multimodal LLMs, paired with a Language-Logic-Function Hybridization that automates rule generation and formalization, and a Disentangled Multimodal Planning (DMP) framework for modular, interpretable planning. Empirical results show substantial gains in rule compliance and planning success over end-to-end baselines, with transparent planning traces; code available at GitHub.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs currently struggle to perform spatial planning under explicit or unseen natural-language rules. There is a need for controllable, scalable rule construction, formal representations, and verifiable validators to ensure reliable, rule-consistent behavior across diverse tasks.

Method: RuleMaze provides a benchmark environment for rule-based spatial planning. Language-Logic-Function Hybridization automatically generates natural-language rules and translates them into logical representations and executable validators, enabling scalable rule engineering. Disentangled Multimodal Planning decomposes perception, execution, and rule verification into interpretable primitives, producing intermediate planning traces and facilitating generalization to more complex or unseen rules.

Result: Experiments show that DMP substantially improves rule compliance and planning success relative to end-to-end textual planning baselines. The approach yields transparent intermediate traces and better generalization to novel rules.

Conclusion: RuleMaze establishes a principled benchmark for grounded, interpretable, rule-based spatial planning in multimodal LLMs and demonstrates that modular, interpretable planning enables systematic generalization. The work provides a scalable pipeline for rule construction, formalization, and validation, with code released.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) combine linguistic reasoning with visual perception, yet their ability to perform visual spatial planning under explicit or previously unseen rule constraints remains underexplored. This setting requires models to jointly understand spatial layouts, interpret natural-language rules, and plan valid actions accordingly. To address this gap, we introduce RuleMaze, a controllable benchmark in which MLLMs must navigate mazes while obeying natural-language rules of varying complexity. RuleMaze isolates rule-compliant spatial planning by requiring accurate perception, rule interpretation, and constrained action planning. To enable scalable and systematic rule construction, we propose Language-Logic-Function Hybridization, which automatically generates natural-language rules and translates them into logical representations and executable validators, eliminating manual rule engineering. To improve rule following and generalization, we introduce Disentangled Multimodal Planning (DMP), which separates perception, execution, and rule verification through interpretable reasoning primitives. By disentangling these components, DMP facilitates systematic generalization to more complex and previously unseen rules, while providing transparent intermediate planning traces. Experiments demonstrate that DMP substantially improves rule compliance and planning success compared to end-to-end textual planning baselines. Overall, RuleMaze establishes a principled benchmark for studying grounded and interpretable rule-based spatial planning in MLLMs. Code is available at https://github.com/oceanflowlab/RuleMaze.

</details>


### [186] [QUASAR: A Quantum-Classical Neural Network for SAR Satellite Physical-Layer Authentication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20240)
*Vincenzo Sammartino,Nathanael Denis,Roberto Di Pietro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Quantum-classical hybrid CNN+VQC for X-band SAR physical-layer authentication; data-efficient, with higher accuracy than classical baselines at the same data budget, and strong spoofing rejection across multiple attack types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: X-band SAR physical-layer authentication is needed but current PLA methods are data-hungry, often limited to sub-6 GHz, and underutilize IQ nonlinearities; a quantum-classical approach may improve data efficiency and robustness.

Method: Introduce QUASAR, a hybrid architecture that fuses a CNN spectrogram encoder with a variational quantum circuit to classify SAR signals for PLA; demonstrates data efficiency (≈10% of training data) and improved performance; evaluated under replay, crafted-IQ injection, and space-borne spoofing.

Result: QUASAR rejects spoofing attempts with accuracies of 89.7%, 94.1%, and 81.3% under the three attack scenarios; claims data-efficiency and superiority over classical baselines at equal data budgets; positions the work as the first quantum-enhanced physical-layer classifier for satellite constellations.

Conclusion: First demonstration of a quantum-enhanced PLA for satellites; suggests a promising research direction at the intersection of quantum computing and terrestrial/satellite security, with practical implications for data-efficient, robust authentication in X-band SAR systems.

Abstract: X-band SAR satellites (8-12 GHz) play a critical role in disaster response, environmental monitoring, and military intelligence. Yet, they lack robust physical-layer authentication (PLA), a security layer orthogonal to cryptographic solutions. Existing PLA systems, typically based on radio-frequency fingerprinting, are often limited to sub-6 GHz frequencies and rely on classical deep learning. However, this approach underfits the IQ phase nonlinearities that distinguish satellite hardware. In this paper, we present QUASAR, to the best of our knowledge the first quantum-classical hybrid architecture that fuses a CNN spectrogram encoder with a variational quantum circuit (VQC) to provide PLA to X-band SAR signals. Our solution enjoys two distinctive features: (i) it is markedly more data-efficient than classical machine learning, requiring only 10% of the training data to match the accuracy of classical baselines -- data collection being notoriously the most time-consuming phase of PLA; and, (ii) at an equal data budget, it improves classification accuracy over those baselines. In detail, we test our solution under three adversarial scenarios: replay, crafted-IQ injection, and space-borne spoofing. QUASAR rejects spoofed transmissions in 89.7%, 94.1%, and 81.3% of attempts, respectively, establishing the first quantum-enhanced physical-layer classifier for satellite constellations. The fully detailed framework and the supporting results, other than being interesting on their own, show a novel research avenue for physical-layer authentication.

</details>


### [187] [Learning When to Think: Adaptive Reasoning for Test-Time Compute Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20256)
*Gijs Kassenaar,Zhao Yang,Vincent François-Lavet*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A 1.5B language model learns to adapt its reasoning depth by selecting among NoThink, Short, and Long modes as the first token, via GRPO; achieves substantial token savings with minimal loss in accuracy on math and other benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed token budgets in reasoning LMs cause over- or under-computation. Adaptive computation could improve efficiency and accuracy by matching reasoning length to problem difficulty without a separate routing mechanism.

Method: Train a 1.5B distilled model on MATH using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to choose one of three modes as the first token. Enforce hard per-mode token caps to keep modes distinct and apply a shaped reward to make each mode worthwhile for its response length. Evaluate on MATH500 and transfer to GSM8K without retraining.

Result: Three modes emerge without collapsing to a single choice. Brief mode is more accurate than Long. Averaged over three seeds, accuracy on MATH500 is 0.782 vs 0.796 for the base model. Mean response length drops from 4,796 to 2,811 tokens (41% reduction). The approach transfers to GSM8K with substantial token savings (up to 76%) and higher accuracy than baselines at similar lengths.

Conclusion: Adaptive reasoning length per problem is learnable without a dedicated router, achieving efficient computation while largely preserving accuracy; the router seems to sort problems by difficulty rather than randomly.

Abstract: Reasoning language models trained with reinforcement learning typically operate under a fixed token budget rather than an explicitly adaptive one, which can lead to over-computation on easy problems and insufficient computation on difficult ones. We study whether a model can learn to allocate its own reasoning effort by choosing, as the first token of its response, one of three modes: \textsc{NoThink} (answer as quickly as possible), \textsc{Short} (brief reasoning), or \textsc{Long} (extended reasoning). The choice is learned inside Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with no separate router, through a shaped reward that makes each mode worthwhile at a different response length, together with hard per-mode token caps that keep the modes distinct. On a 1.5B distilled model trained on MATH, the three modes emerge without collapsing to a single choice, and the brief modes end up more accurate than \textsc{Long}, which shows that the router sorts problems by difficulty rather than at random. Averaged over three seeds, the resulting policy stays close to the base model's accuracy on the held-out MATH500 ($0.782$ vs.\ $0.796$) while cutting the mean response length from $4{,}796$ to $2{,}811$ tokens (a $41\%$ reduction). Interestingly, it also transfers to other benchmarks without retraining, with the largest savings where problems are easier, with for instance 76\% token reduction on GSM8K and at higher accuracy than the baselines at similar response length. In short, we build a reasoning model that adaptively chooses how much to reason for each problem.

</details>


### [188] [Catching the Rug: Early Prediction of Fraudulent Memecoins on Solana via Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20271)
*Jianghai Li,Pavel Kuznetsov,Yury Yanovich,Konstantin Nott-Whaley,Igor Vodolazov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Solana memecoin rug pulls can be detected early with ML using only the first minutes of trading data. A large-scale dataset (6.4M tokens over 7 months) enables cross-platform generalization between pumps on different DEXs. Without code-level features, gradient boosting (XGBoost) yields robust early detection, and multi-source data fusion mitigates domain shift between platforms (PumpFun and Raydium).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the underexplored problem of rug pulls in Solana memecoins and extend beyond Ethereum-focused studies. High-throughput DeFi on Solana makes early detection critical.

Method: Assemble a large-scale dataset of 6.4 million tokens over 7 months. Characterize rug-pull dynamics (liquidity manipulation and social factors) on Solana. Train classic ML models (notably XGBoost) using only the first 5 minutes of trading data, without code-level features. Assess cross-platform generalization between two platforms (PumpFun and Raydium) and apply multi-source data fusion to reduce domain shift.

Result: Most memecoins show rug-pull characteristics within one hour of launch. XGBoost achieves robust detection using only the first 5 minutes of data. Multi-source data fusion across PumpFun and Raydium improves detection reliability by mitigating domain shift.

Conclusion: This work advances DeFi fraud understanding on high-throughput chains and provides a practical framework for protecting investors through early and robust rug-pull detection on Solana, with demonstrated cross-platform generalization.

Abstract: The rapid proliferation of memecoins on blockchain platforms has increased the risk of fraudulent activities, particularly rug pulls. While previous studies have focused on Ethereum-based tokens, this paper shifts the spotlight to Solana, the leading blockchain for memecoins by trading volume and token count. Unlike Ethereum, where rug pulls often exploit smart contract backdoors, Solana memecoin rug pulls are predominantly driven by liquidity manipulation and social dynamics. This research pioneers large-scale rug pull early detection in the Solana ecosystem by assembling a dataset of 6.4 million tokens over 7 months. Market analysis reveals that a vast majority of these memecoins exhibit rug pull characteristics within one hour of launch, highlighting the urgency of short-horizon prediction. Despite the absence of code-level features, we demonstrate that classic machine learning models, particularly Gradient Boosting (XGBoost), achieve robust performance in detecting potential rug pulls using only the first 5 minutes of trading data. Furthermore, we evaluate cross-platform generalization between PumpFun and Raydium, revealing that multi-source data fusion significantly mitigates domain shift and improves detection reliability. This study advances the understanding of DeFi fraud on high-throughput chains and provides a practical framework for protecting investors.

</details>


### [189] [Break It Down, Pass It On: Cross-Task Skill Transfer in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20274)
*Yiyang Feng,Biddut Sarker Bijoy,Niranjan Balasubramanian,Jiawei Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Subtask-level, text-based skill induction transfers more reliably across tasks than task-level or code-based skills; a combined specificity and abstractness (skill utility) score predicts transfer success and can diagnose skill usefulness without executing tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why induced skills transfer varies and identify predictors of reliable transfer across tasks.

Method: Systematic comparison of induction granularity (task-level vs subtask-level) and representation (text vs code) across multiple tasks; define metrics specificity and abstractness; derive a composite skill utility score and test its correlation with task success.

Result: Task-level skills underperform relative to no-memory baseline; subtask-level skills outperform on average; text skills transfer better than code; specificity and abstractness together predict transfer success; skill utility score correlates with success; subtask-level and text formats yield higher scores.

Conclusion: A practical diagnostic (skill utility) can forecast transfer success; favor subtask-level and text-based skill induction to improve transfer; the score can be computed without task execution and used to guide induction choices.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents can induce skills from completed tasks and reuse them later to grow more capable with experience. In practice, induced skills may transfer unreliably and can even harm the agent that retrieves them. When agent-induced skills transfer reliably across tasks remains an open question. We conduct a comprehensive and controlled study of how the way skills are induced shapes their transfer across tasks. Specifically, we compare task-level with subtask-level skill induction and text with code skill formats, the two axes along which existing methods differ. Task-level skills mostly reduce the agent's performance below its no-memory baseline while subtask-level skills raise it above on average, and text skills transfer better than code skills. To further understand our findings, we examine two complementary properties of the induced skills: specificity, which measures how closely a skill matches real tasks, and abstractness, which measures how evenly its relevance spreads across tasks. Neither property alone predicts task success, but their combined effect does, which we propose as a skill utility score. The score correlates consistently with task success when skills are transferred, and subtask-level and text skills score higher. Computing skill utility only needs the skills and task descriptions but not any task execution, so our score serves as a practical diagnostic of a skill memory before any new task runs.

</details>


### [190] [Phantom Gains: Auditing Self-Improvement Against a Measured Null](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20290)
*Cheng Xu,Nan Yan,Liming Chen,M-Tahar Kechadi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An audit of self-training vs. a frozen control for Qwen3-8B reveals multiple measurement artifacts that can invert findings; replacing naive metrics with per-problem exact tests under FDR yields a more reliable conclusion: external distillation helps problems the base model rarely reaches, while three forms of self-training do not; results depend on how nulls are constructed, requiring a null for every statistic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether observed task improvements from model self-training reflect genuine capability gains or are artifacts of evaluation pipelines and measurement noise; address multiple testing and measurement artifacts in transition-level auditing of model progress.

Method: Three rounds of rank-32 LoRA self-training on Qwen3-8B compared to a frozen control through an identical pipeline. Identify seven measurement failures that can invert findings without a proper null. Use a per-problem exact test against a pooled baseline under false-discovery-rate control to assess each statistic, and apply the approach to a ladder of arms matched in stream, volume, and evaluation. Analyze external distillation vs self-training, and perform a regression to test asymmetry of gains. Require nulls built from baseline replicates for every statistic.

Result: Seven measurement failures identified, including a ledger-based artifact and inference-batching effects. Naive thresholds (e.g., expansion statistic, natural threshold repair) do not survive replication. The per-problem exact test with FDR detects nothing on held-out replicates and remains stable under multiple-testing rules. External distillation improves problems the base model rarely reaches; three forms of self-training do not, and the asymmetry is explained by the overall gain from distillation (p < 1e-8). On problems the base model never reaches, evidence is inconclusive; self-training can worsen problems solved at baseline. Transition-level auditing thus requires a separate null for every statistic reported, constructed from baseline replicates without adding new experiments.

Conclusion: Robust auditing of model progress requires statistic-specific null models. Without them, measurement artifacts can masquerade as gains. The study demonstrates that external distillation yields more reliable improvements on difficult problems, while self-training alone does not; any multi-arm evaluation should embed per-statistic nulls and preserve replication to ensure credible conclusions.

Abstract: Whether a language model has improved itself is increasingly judged not by mean accuracy but by which individual problems it gains and loses. Tracking these transitions means differencing two noisy estimates, leaving them vulnerable to measurement artifacts. Auditing three rounds of rank-$32$ LoRA self-training on Qwen3-8B against a frozen control pushed through the identical pipeline, we identify seven measurement failures, each of which inverts a reported finding when its control is absent. Several are standard practice. A ledger built on a single greedy decode manufactures capability changes on an untrained model, largely an artifact of inference batching; the expansion statistic separating acquisition from sharpening assigns that same model a rate of $0.280$. The natural threshold repair does not survive replication: estimated across the frozen comparisons such a design already contains, its null stays non-zero. We replace it with a per-problem exact test against a pooled baseline under false-discovery-rate control, which detects nothing on any held-out replicate and is unchanged under the multiple-testing rule, error rate and pool size. Applied to a ladder of arms matched in stream, volume and evaluation, the audit finds that external distillation improves problems the base model rarely reaches while three forms of self-training do not; a regression rejects this asymmetry as a by-product of distillation's larger overall gain ($p < 10^{-8}$). On the far smaller set of problems the base model never reaches, the evidence is inconclusive, while self-training corrupts problems solved at baseline at rates well above the measured floor. Transition-level auditing therefore requires a separately measured null for every statistic it reports: nulls that cost no new experiments, built from baseline replicates a multi-arm study already owns, though not from as few as most possess.

</details>


### [191] [MidTool: Mid-training Data Synthesis for Agentic Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20314)
*Fengqing Jiang,Yite Wang,Boyi Liu,Zhaoyang Wang,Canwen Xu,Zhewei Yao,Radha Poovendran,Yuxiong He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MidTool introduces a mid-training corpus for agentic tool-use to improve LLM tool-use capabilities; applied to Qwen models; shows consistent gains on multiple benchmarks under SFT and RL, supporting mid-training for tool use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the gap that tool-use abilities are often learned during post-training; mid-training can instantiate grounding and tool-usage workflows, enabling better generalization and resilience.

Method: Build MidTool-Mix from web, PDF, and code data; synth supervision from real tool APIs, MCP skills, and document-grounded workflows; train Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base with mid-training, followed by supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning.

Result: MidTool-Mix yields consistent improvements on BFCL, tau2-Bench, MCP Universe under both SFT and RL vs baselines.

Conclusion: Dedicated mid-training for general tool use is beneficial and should complement post-training; demonstrates feasibility of mid-training for agentic tool-use capabilities.

Abstract: Mid-training is increasingly recognized as a critical stage for shaping the capabilities of large language models. Recent work has shown that targeted mid-training can strengthen reasoning-intensive abilities such as math and science, and can also improve agentic capabilities in software-engineering settings. In this work, we study the parallel but less explored agentic capability: general tool use. We present MidTool, an open corpus construction pipeline for agentic tool-use mid-training that combines large-scale web, PDF, and code data with synthesized supervision from real-world tool APIs, MCP skills, and document-grounded workflows. MidTool is designed to teach models how to recognize tool affordances, ground arguments from context, compose tool call workflow, and recover from incomplete information. We mid-train Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base on MidTool-Mix, and then apply follow-up post-training with both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Compared with baselines, MidTool-Mix consistently improves downstream performance under both SFT and RL on BFCL, tau2-Bench, and MCP Universe. These results suggest that general tool use, like other important LLM capabilities, benefits from dedicated mid-training rather than being left entirely to post-training.

</details>


### [192] [Pandora's AI Model Routing Box: Efficient Allocation with Costly Value Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20316)
*Adam Fisch,Shubhendu Trivedi,Fantine Huot,William W. Cohen,Michael Kaisers,Mirella Lapata,Kate Larson,Jacob Eisenstein*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework (Pandora's Router) for routing queries among heterogeneous AI specialists by estimating the value of additional information versus cost, using a Gaussian signal model; extended to decentralized Pandora's Bidder; shows near-optimal routing with far less use of expensive estimators; potential efficiency gains and strategic behavior in decentralized setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve quality and efficiency of heterogeneous AI systems by avoiding expensive, high-information estimates when cheaper, noisy estimates suffice; formalize the trade-off as a Pandora's Box problem to optimize when to refine estimates.

Method: Formalization of the routing problem as Pandora's Box under Gaussian signals, deriving closed-form value-of-information expressions; centralized policy Pandora's Router; decentralized extension Pandora's Bidder where specialists self-assess before accepting offers; empirical evaluation across three domains including standard multi-LLM benchmarks and retrieval-augmented systems; analysis of efficiency and strategic behavior.

Result: Pandora's Router achieves routing quality comparable to exhaustive estimation while querying expensive estimators far less often; in decentralised settings, VOF can improve allocative efficiency with accurate competing estimates, but can hurt when estimates are noisy due to strategic behavior.

Conclusion: Value-of-information-based routing in heterogeneous AI systems can substantially reduce costs with minimal loss in routing quality in centralized settings; decentralized adoption yields mixed welfare outcomes depending on estimate accuracies, highlighting the need to consider strategic behavior and possibly safeguards when releasing offers and prices.

Abstract: Heterogeneous AI systems composed of multiple models, architectures, harnesses, or inference-time settings can improve quality and efficiency by routing queries to the specialist who can answer most effectively at the lowest cost. Routing requires estimating each specialist's expected return, but this value estimation has a cost. Cheap estimators (e.g., embedding-based predictors) are fast but noisy, while accurate estimators (e.g., fine-tuned models with access to retrieval results or partial reasoning traces) are expensive. We formalize this tradeoff as an instance of Pandora's Box, the classical problem of optimal search with costly inspection. Under a Gaussian signal model, the resulting policies have closed-form value-of-information expressions that determine, for each specialist and input, whether refining the value estimate is worth its cost. We call the centralized policy Pandora's Router. We extend this to a decentralized setting, Pandora's Bidder, where specialists independently decide whether to invest in self-assessment before accepting an offered price to claim a query. Experiments across three domains---a standard multi-LLM benchmark, retrieval-augmented specialists, and LLMs with variable inference-time reasoning---show that Pandora's Router matches the routing quality of exhaustive estimation, while querying the expensive estimator far less often. In the decentralized setting, value-of-information reasoning improves allocative efficiency when competing estimates are accurate; when competing estimates are noisy, however, it can increase the strategic specialist's utility at the expense of others.

</details>


### [193] [AI4AI-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents in Algorithmic Design for Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20318)
*Yizhe Chi,Wenyi Li,Deyao Hong,Xiaoqiu Wang,Mingju Gao,Kaisen Yang,Bingxiang He,Youjie Zheng,Calvin Xiao,Qinhuai Na*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI4AI-Bench benchmarks recursive self-improvement by requiring agents to rewrite the training algorithm in frozen repositories, measuring the impact on learning objective optimization across 10 tasks. Results indicate limited ability to meaningfully alter the training process, with most submissions not changing how models learn; some attempts show notable gains, but still far from the optimum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess the feasibility of recursive self-improvement (RSI) via improvements to the training algorithm, isolating this capability from data collection or hyperparameter tuning, by creating a benchmark where agents rewrite training code and are evaluated on fixed tasks.

Method: 10 tasks drawn from frozen research repositories, each task uses a 4-hour window to rewrite the training algorithm (on a B300), then runs the modified code for up to 12 hours with a fixed evaluator hidden from the agent. Each task is mapped to a single scale where 0 is uninformative, 0.1 equals the shipped algorithm, and 1.0 equals the task optimum. 29 configurations across 6 systems evaluated on all tasks. The evaluator is fixed and blind, ensuring comparability across submissions.

Result: Mean score across configurations/tasks is 0.166; best is 0.250 (about 25% of the distance to the optimum). Most submissions do not alter the learning procedure. Among the minority that do, average score improves from 0.126 to 0.226 (with reasoning-focused submissions rising from 8% to 64%). The strongest participants approach modest gains but remain far from full RSI potential. The authors release the suite, evaluators, and all submissions for reproducibility.

Conclusion: RSI-related progress in this setting appears limited under current benchmarks; most teams do not change how the model learns, and even when changes occur, improvements are incremental. The paper provides a reusable benchmark to track future RSI progress as algorithms and systems evolve.

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) asks whether an AI system can improve the process that produces AI systems, so that the next system inherits the improvement. That process is the training algorithm: a better objective or update rule improves the compute\mbox{-}capability exchange rate for every subsequent run, including the one that produces the next agent. Whether RSI is feasible therefore turns on whether an agent can design training algorithms. No benchmark isolates that ability: existing suites are won by collecting data or by tuning hyperparameters, and none tells a change to how a run is executed apart from a change to how the model learns. We present AI4AI\mbox{-}Bench, 10 frozen research repositories spanning 10 training algorithm families. In each task, an agent has 4 hours on one B300 to rewrite the training algorithm; its code is then rerun from scratch for up to 12 hours and scored by a fixed evaluator hidden from the agent, against the repository's original algorithm under the same procedure. Because the 10 metrics are incommensurable, every task is mapped onto one scale on which $0$ is an uninformative model, $0.1$ is the algorithm the repository ships, and $1.0$ is the task optimum. Across 29 configurations of 6 systems on all 10 tasks the mean score is $0.166$, and the best system reaches $0.250$: even the strongest closes under a fifth of the distance between the algorithm that was already there and the optimum. The submissions show where that distance went: most never change how the model learns at all, and the minority that do average $0.226$ against $0.126$ for the rest. More reasoning effort mostly buys the willingness to go there, taking that minority from $8\%$ of submissions to $64\%$ and the mean score from $0.094$ to $0.196$. We release the task suite, the evaluators and every scored submission, so that the measurement can be repeated as these systems change.

</details>


### [194] [An Agentic Approach for Active Data Collection, Travel Behavior Modeling, and Weather-Sensitive Demand Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20320)
*Narges Ahmadi,Yubo Jiao,Jônatas Augusto Manzolli,Jiangbo Yu,Luis Miranda-Moreno*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Three-agent workflow linking conversational surveys, structured data processing, and predictive modeling; LLMs and ML benchmarks achieve ~69–72% five-class accuracy on weather-influenced mode choice, with multimodal/contextual data providing gains; demonstrates auditable coordination across stages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Travel-behavior research often treats data collection, processing, and prediction as separate, siloed steps. This work argues for an auditable, multi-agent workflow that integrates conversational data collection, structured processing, and predictive modeling, including multimodal LLMs, to improve and rationalize the predictive pipeline.

Method: Proposes a three-agent workflow: (1) conversational data collection via chatbot-administered, image-augmented stated-preference survey collecting mode choices under five weather scenarios (n=454 respondent-scenario observations); (2) structured data processing and conventional behavioral modeling (multinomial logit for weather associations; logistic regression and random forest as ML benchmarks); (3) evaluation of nine locally deployed large language models (2–35B parameters) across four zero-shot prompt-and-context conditions, extended with persona, few-shot, and vision-based configurations; comparison against ML baselines; also uses same weather images for vision prompts.

Result: Random Forest: 69.6% five-class accuracy; best text-only zero-shot LLM: 69.9%; best vision-based configuration: 71.5%. Habitual travel information provided the most consistent gains; Expert framing generally outperformed Role-Play; persona information most useful when habitual info is unavailable. Few-shot prompting improved several models with gains stabilizing after a small number of examples. Vision context offered additional predictive value for selected models.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a coordinated, auditable multi-agent workflow integrating conversational surveys, structured data processing, conventional modeling, machine learning, and multimodal LLM prediction. Provides a blueprint for integrating AI-assisted survey data collection with traditional modeling and ML in travel-behavior research, and highlights how prompting configurations, habitual-travel cues, and visual context can influence predictive performance.

Abstract: Travel behavior research increasingly combines digital data collection with predictive modeling, yet these stages are often developed and evaluated separately. This study proposes a three-agent workflow integrating conversational data collection, structured data processing, and behavioral prediction. A chatbot-administered, image-augmented stated-preference survey collected mode choices from student commuters across five predefined weather scenarios, yielding 454 respondent-scenario observations. Weather-related associations were analyzed using a multinomial logit model, while logistic regression and random forest provided machine-learning benchmarks. Nine locally deployed large language models (LLMs), ranging from 2 to 35 billion parameters, were evaluated across four zero-shot prompt-and-context conditions and extended through persona, few-shot, and vision-based configurations. Random forest achieved 69.6% five-class accuracy, while the best text-only zero-shot LLM reached 69.9% without task-specific fitting. Habitual travel information produced the most consistent gains, Expert framing generally outperformed Role-Play, and persona information was most useful when habitual travel information was unavailable. Few-shot prompting improved prediction for several models, with gains stabilizing after a small number of examples. Using the same weather images shown to respondents, the best vision-based configuration reached 71.5% five-class accuracy, indicating that visual context may provide additional predictive information for selected models. Overall, the study shows how conversational surveys, structured data processing, conventional behavioral modeling, machine learning, and multimodal LLM prediction can be coordinated within an auditable multi-agent workflow.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [195] [APPROVE: Visual End-User-in-the-Loop Robot Programming with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19281)
*Bijan Kavousian,Miray Özakkas,Josefine Monnet,Oliver Petrovic,Christian Brecher*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: APPROVE is an LLM-powered, multi-modal end-user robot programming framework that merges natural language with a Blockly-based block interface and an explicit user confirmation step, visualizes generated programs, and stores confirmed functions in a reusable library to improve transparency, intent alignment, and reusability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Programming robots remains hard for non-experts due to required expertise, lack of transparency, misalignment with user intent, and limited reuse. The work targets these gaps by combining LLMs with visual, verifiable user feedback to enable safer, more controllable robot programming.

Method: An LLM generates robot programs from natural language. The programs are visualized as Blockly blocks in a block-based interface. Users confirm, modify, or reject the generated blocks before execution. Confirmed blocks/functions are stored in a library for reuse, gradually building a set of reliable components. The design emphasizes a human-centered flow with explicit end-user feedback to align with user intent and trust.

Result: The abstract presents a design and framework (APPROVE) rather than reporting empirical results. It outlines the architecture, interaction flow, and reuse mechanism, but provides no quantitative or qualitative evaluation data in the abstract.

Conclusion: APPROVE contributes a human-centered approach to LLM-based robot programming by combining NL input, visual confirmation, and a reusable component library to improve transparency, alignment with user intent, and reusability in end-user robotics programming.

Abstract: Programming robots remains challenging for non-experts, as traditional methods require expert knowledge and even block-based interfaces often lack flexibility. Recent work has explored Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically generate robot programs from natural language, but these systems remain limited by a lack of transparency, missing mechanisms to ensure alignment with user intent, and little support for reuse. We present APPROVE (AI-Powered Programming for Robots with Visual End-User Feedback), an LLM-based multi-modal end-user programming framework that integrates natural language input with a block-based interface and an explicit user confirmation step. Generated programs are visualized using a block-based interface in Blockly, allowing users to confirm, modify, or reject them before execution. Confirmed functions are stored in a library for reuse, gradually building a set of reliable program components. Our approach contributes a human-centered design for LLM-based robot programming that emphasizes user trust, intent alignment, and reusability.

</details>


### [196] [The Missing Touch: Spatially Distributed Tactile Feedback Brings Teleoperation Closer to Human Dexterity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19372)
*Rohan Kota,Gregory Reardon,J. Edward Colgate*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Distributed tactile feedback on a remote manipulator improves teleoperation performance and makes teleop trajectories more natural; higher tactile resolution helps by compressing state-space distribution, aiding autonomous policy learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To close the dexterity gap in teleoperation by restoring spatial tactile cues that are missing in conventional teleoperation, and to assess effects on task performance, behavioral naturalness, and learning-relevant representations.

Method: A 2-DoF bilateral force-feedback teleoperation system paired with a 32-DoF tactile fingertip display was used. Tasks required reproducing localized remote contact on the operator's finger. Experiments varied tactile feedback resolution by adjusting how measured displacements were quantized. Metrics included task performance speed, reliance on corrective actions, trajectory deviation from natural human-like motion (reduced by 29–79%), and state-space distribution of teleoperated motions.

Result: Localized, distributed tactile reproduction significantly improved performance and naturalness, reduced the need for corrective actions, and, with higher tactile resolution, compressed the teleoperation state-space distribution—an attribute linked to better autonomous-policy training outcomes.

Conclusion: Spatially distributed tactile feedback is a key factor in closing the dexterity gap between human and teleoperated manipulation and has favorable implications for training autonomous robots.

Abstract: A fundamental challenge in robotic teleoperation is enabling an operator to control a remote robot as effortlessly and intuitively as their own hands. Despite the growing use of teleoperation to collect demonstration data for training autonomous robot policies, teleoperated robot performance still falls significantly short of human dexterity, even for basic tasks. Here, we present evidence that a key factor contributing to this performance gap is the absence of spatially distributed tactile feedback. Using a two-degree-of-freedom (DoF) bilateral force-feedback telemanipulator paired with a 32-DoF tactile fingertip display, we show that operator performance improves significantly when localized deformations on the remote manipulator are faithfully reproduced on the operator's fingertip. In a series of teleoperation tasks, reproducing distributed contact information not only accelerated task performance but also brought teleoperated movements closer to natural human behavior by minimizing corrective actions and task completion steps, thereby reducing the deviation between teleoperated and natural trajectories by 29$\unicode{x2013}$79%. Furthermore, we found that increasing the resolution of the tactile feedback$\unicode{x2014}$by refining how finely the measured displacements were quantized for reproduction$\unicode{x2014}$compressed the state-space distribution of teleoperated motions, which has been associated with improved training outcomes for autonomous robot policies. Together, these results suggest that spatially distributed tactile feedback is essential for closing the gap between human and teleoperated dexterity and training the next generation of autonomous robots.

</details>


### [197] [Learning the Right Abstraction: Neural Reduced Dynamics for Complex Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19375)
*Harry Zhang,Dan Negrut*

Main category: cs.RO
