TL;DR: Neural Reduced Dynamics (NRD) learns a reduced, control-relevant state-space and dynamics model to enable fast, policy-learning inside the model, with validation in a high-fidelity simulator. It achieves ~10^4× speedups and zero-shot transfer across terrains, outperforming single-terrain baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-fidelity simulators provide realistic evaluation but are computationally expensive for large-scale RL; there is a need for data-driven, fast simulators that preserve control-relevant physics through the right abstraction.

Method: Develop NRD framework that separates the propagated state from inputs/recoverable quantities, trains policies entirely inside a frozen learned dynamics model, and validates policies back in a high-fidelity simulator. Demonstrated on three tasks: terrain-aware trajectory tracking across rigid, bumpy, and deformable CRM terrain for HMMWV, and goal-reaching with a stock tracked vehicle and an articulated arm. A single policy, trained without terrain inputs, transfers to real terrain and outperforms terrain-specific policies.

Result: NRD enables roughly four orders of magnitude faster simulated time than the high-fidelity scenes. A single policy achieves zero-shot transfer on bumpy terrain and attains near-perfect success rates: vehicle 100/100 goals; arm 97/100; with no contacts or joint-limit violations. The policy, trained inside NRD, attains lower tracking errors than single-terrain specialists on all terrains.

Conclusion: NRD provides a practical bridge between expensive accurate physics and scalable robot learning, enabling rapid on-policy iterations and transferability to high-fidelity simulators; supports neural dynamics as a viable abstraction for complex robot control.

Abstract: High-fidelity embodied AI simulators provide realistic evaluation of complex robotic systems, but their computational cost limits their direct use for large-scale reinforcement learning campaigns. We advocate the use of less accurate but more expeditious simulations, which might draw on data-driven, e.g., neural dynamics, models. This contribution argues that the practical value of a neural dynamics model for complex robot control lies in learning the \emph{right abstraction}: a reduced state that preserves the control-relevant physics of the high-fidelity system while enabling high-throughput policy learning. We develop a neural reduced dynamics (NRD) framework that separates the state the model propagates from what can be supplied as an input or recovered analytically, trains policies entirely inside the frozen learned model, and validates them back in the high-fidelity simulator. Two case studies instantiate it across three control tasks: terrain-aware HMMWV trajectory tracking on rigid, bumpy and deformable Continuum Representation Model (CRM) terrain; and goal reaching for a stock tracked vehicle and its front-mounted articulated arm. Every policy transfers back to the high-fidelity simulator. A single policy trained inside the terrain-conditioned dynamics model, and given no terrain input of its own, attains lower median and mean tracking error than both single-terrain specialists on all three terrains, including zero-shot bumpy terrain. Quantitatively, the tracked vehicle reaches 100 of 100 goals and the arm 97 of 100, with zero contacts or joint-limit violations. The NRD models advance roughly four orders of magnitude faster in simulated time than the high-fidelity simulator scenes they replace, making iterative on-policy learning practical and supporting neural reduced dynamics as a bridge between accurate but expensive physics simulation and scalable robot learning.

</details>


### [198] [SCAPE: Scenario-Conditioned Simulation-Augmented Policy Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19425)
*Dijie Zhu,Seunghun Oh,Ruopeng Huang,Zhiyu Huang,Jiaqi Ma,Chen Tang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SCAPE is a scenario-conditioned, simulation-augmented policy evaluation framework that predicts scenario-specific real-world performance using limited real-world data and large-scale simulations, correcting sim-to-real bias and providing calibrated uncertainty via conformal prediction; it yields improved scenario-level accuracy, sample efficiency, and deployment guidance, demonstrated on driving and quadruped tasks including a real Unitree Go2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Population-averaged, sim-to-real-biased evaluation obscures scenario-specific risk and safety for deploying learned policies; need scenario-aware estimates to decide when/where to deploy.

Method: Combine limited paired sim-real samples with large-scale simulation rollouts; correct sim-to-real bias in simulation labels; train a predictor of real-world performance conditioned on scenario; use conformal prediction to calibrate uncertainty.

Result: In sim-to-sim studies, reduces scenario-level prediction error by 4.9%/34.7% (driving) and 14.5%/27.7% (quadruped) relative to scene-conditioned neural and aggregate baselines on average. Real-world velocity-tracking on Unitree Go2 shows improved testing sample efficiency, narrower calibrated prediction intervals, better generalization to out-of-distribution scenarios, and enables fine-grained deployment strategies.

Conclusion: SCAPE offers accurate, calibrated, scenario-aware evaluation that guides safe deployment and improves efficiency across robotic domains.

Abstract: Reliable performance evaluation is a central bottleneck for deploying robot-learning policies in real-world conditions. Real-world testing is faithful but costly and difficult to scale, whereas simulation-based testing scales easily but is inevitably biased by the sim-to-real gap. Existing simulation-augmented methods combine limited real-world rollouts with abundant simulation proxies, but focus on performance averaged over initial conditions and deployment settings. Such population-level averages obscure scenario-specific variation and provide limited guidance about when and where a policy can be safely deployed. We propose SCAPE, a scenario-conditioned simulation-augmented policy evaluation framework that predicts scenario-conditioned real-world policy performance using limited paired sim-and-real samples and large-scale simulation rollouts. SCAPE corrects sim-to-real bias in simulation labels before training the prediction model and calibrates prediction uncertainty through conformal prediction. We validate SCAPE on autonomous driving and quadruped velocity tracking. In sim-to-sim studies, SCAPE reduces scenario-level prediction error by 4.9%/34.7% (driving) and 14.5%/27.7% (quadruped) relative to scene-conditioned neural and aggregate statistical baselines on average. We further evaluate a velocity-tracking policy deployed on a physical Unitree Go2. SCAPE also improves testing sample efficiency, produces narrower calibrated prediction intervals, generalizes better to out-of-distribution scenarios, and enables fine-grained deployment strategies.

</details>


### [199] [Hybrid Feedback Sampling for Sample-Efficient Model Predictive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19443)
*Chaoyi Pan,Zeji Yi,John Zhang,Zachary Manchester,Guannan Qu,Guanya Shi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FS-MPC introduces a hybrid sampling strategy that uses an optimized feedback policy to define the sampling distribution in shooting-based MPC, yielding faster convergence than MPPI and better optimality than pure feedback sampling; validated on humanoid loco-manipulation, dexterous manipulation, and real-world locomotion/manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling-based MPC with shooting methods struggles with high-dimensional/open-loop unstable systems, as the required number of samples grows exponentially with horizon, causing poor sample efficiency and numerical issues. There is a need for an optimal sampling proposal that preserves stability and efficiency.

Method: Propose Feedback Sampling MPC (FS-MPC) with a hybrid sampling design that balances local and global search based on system stability and compute budget. The optimal sampling distribution is realized by sampling with an optimized feedback policy. The approach includes theoretical analysis of shooting instability and empirical validation in contact-rich tasks, including real-world robot experiments.

Result: Theoretical analysis shows the hybrid sampling in FS-MPC converges faster than standard MPPI and achieves better optimality than standard feedback sampling. Empirically, FS-MPC handles dynamically unstable tasks where standard sampling-based methods fail and outperforms feedback policies alone. Real-world validation on humanoid locomotion and manipulation tasks corroborates effectiveness.

Conclusion: FS-MPC provides a principled method to realize the optimal sampling distribution via an optimized feedback policy, enabling improved performance for high-dimensional, unstable robotic systems and demonstrating practical viability in humanoid locomotion and manipulation.

Abstract: Thanks to its parallelizability and flexibility, sampling-based Model Predictive Control (MPC) has become widely popular for controlling real-world robotic systems. However, for high-dimensional and open-loop unstable dynamical systems, the required number of samples to improve the control sequence will grow exponentially with the horizon, leading to poor sample efficiency and numerical instability. This paper investigates the instability of shooting methods in sampling-based MPC and shows that the optimal sampling proposal distribution can be realized by sampling with an optimized feedback policy. We refer to this algorithm as Feedback Sampling MPC (FS-MPC). FS-MPC involves a hybrid sampling design which balances local and global search based on the system stability and the available computation budget. Our theoretical analysis shows that our hybrid sampling approach achieves faster convergence than standard MPPI and better optimality than standard feedback sampling. Empirically, in diverse contact-rich control tasks like humanoid loco-manipulation and dexterous manipulation, we show that FS-MPC successfully tackles dynamically unstable tasks where standard sample-based approaches struggle, and strictly outperforms feedback policies alone. Finally, we validate our method on humanoid robot locomotion and manipulation tasks in the real world.

</details>


### [200] [When Automata Meet Streams: Temporal Logic Compilation for Stream-Based Robotics Task and Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19453)
*Sayem Nazmuz Zaman,Cyrus Neary*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SAM-TD enables enforcing arbitrary LTL_f temporal constraints in stream-based TAMP by regressing automata guards into action schemas and synchronizing automata with a shared validity token, allowing dynamically generated stream objects and showing positive results in PDDLStream benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LTL_f temporal requirements are essential for long-horizon tasks, but existing stream-based planners cannot enforce them due to iterative stream refinement and fixed-object assumptions; a method is needed to integrate temporal constraints without fixing object sets or modifying planners.

Method: Translate LTL_f constraints into automata, embed regressed automaton guards into action schemas pre-planning; during search, synchronously update automata and use a shared validity token to prune constraint-violating branches; supports dynamic stream objects; does not require enumerating fixed objects or planner changes.

Result: First demonstration of stream-based TAMP under LTL_f constraints in three robotics PDDLStream environments; competitive with state-of-the-art temporal-constraint compilation methods on discrete PDDL benchmarks.

Conclusion: SAM-TD makes it feasible to enforce temporal specifications in stream-based TAMP with dynamically generated objects, bridging TAMP and temporal logic, and offering practical benefits for long-horizon robotic tasks.

Abstract: Stream-based robotics Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) integrates discrete symbolic planning with dynamically generated continuous geometric parameters, such as poses, grasps, and trajectories. However, stream-based planners typically reason only about goal reachability, whereas long-horizon tasks also demand adherence to temporal specifications, such as safety-critical ordering, invariance, and liveness constraints. No methods currently exist to enforce such temporal constraints for stream-based solvers because streams generate an expanding geometric object set via iterative stream refinement loops during planning, rendering existing temporal-logic compilation techniques incompatible. We therefore present Synchronous Action Monitoring with Token Destruction (SAM-TD), a compilation method that enforces arbitrary Linear Temporal Logic over finite traces ($\textrm{LTL}_f$) specifications in stream-based TAMP. SAM-TD translates arbitrary $\textrm{LTL}_f$ constraints into automata and embeds regressed automaton guards into action schemas, which are pre-specified before planning begins. By doing so, SAM-TD can handle objects generated by streams during planning, thus circumventing the need to enumerate a fixed object set or modify the underlying planner. During search, SAM-TD synchronously updates automaton states and uses a validity token shared across all automata to prune constraint-violating branches. We show that SAM-TD supports dynamically generated stream objects from iterative stream refinements during plan search. Experimental results provide the first ever demonstration of stream-based TAMP under $\textrm{LTL}_f$ constraints in three robotics PDDLStream environments. Furthermore, on standard discrete PDDL benchmarks, SAM-TD is competitive with state-of-the-art temporal-constraint compilation methods.

</details>


### [201] [Fine-Tuning VLAs with Self-Demonstrated Generative Control for Multi-Task Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19490)
*Prachi Garg,Steve Xing,Prahit Yaugand,Saurabh Gupta,Derek Hoiem*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Self-supervised rollout-based finetuning of vision-language-action models preserves priors while adapting to new embodiments, enabling general instruction following and efficient learning of new skills on real and simulated robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large VLA models exhibit strong semantics and instruction following but suffer performance drops when deployed on novel hardware embodiments due to misalignment; fine-tuning with expert data helps task-specific performance but can erode the model's original priors and instruction-following abilities.

Method: Generate online interaction rollouts from the zero-shot VLA in a self-supervised way and use these rollouts as additional training data for finetuning, combining with expert data to obtain multi-task policies on the target robot.

Result: The finetuned model on the target robot inherits prior tasks distilled from the zero-shot model, preserves generalist instruction following, and learns new skills from expert data with improved sample efficiency; demonstrated on a real ALOHA robot and a RoboTwin simulation benchmark.

Conclusion: Self-supervised rollout-based finetuning mitigates the trade-off between adapting to a new embodiment and preserving zeroshot priors, yielding robust multi-task policies across real and simulated platforms.

Abstract: State-of-the-art vision-language-action (VLA) models such as $π_{0.5}$ exhibit strong semantic understanding, instruction following and task behavior. However, when deployed on new robots, even minor mismatches in hardware configuration relative to pretraining can cause severe performance drops. Finetuning the VLA on in-domain expert data from the new embodiment improves performance on the expert task but leads to a loss in its original instruction following and behavioral priors. In this paper, we propose a self-supervised method that generates online interaction rollouts from the zero-shot VLA as additional training data for finetuning. Our experiments show this finetuning scheme yields strong multi-task policies that, on the target robot, (1) inherit prior tasks distilled from the zero-shot model, (2) enable generalist instruction following, while (3) learning new skills from expert data with improved sample efficiency. We demonstrate the success of our approach across test sets probing generalization on a real ALOHA robot and a new simulation benchmark in RoboTwin. Video results are available at https://self-supervised-control.pages.dev/

</details>


### [202] [LF-GICP: Parameter-Free Degeneracy-Aware LiDAR Odometry via a Voxel-Normal Localizability Field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19522)
*Eunsoo Im*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A parameter-free, regularization-free voxel-based GICP framework (LF-GICP) that detects and mitigates degeneracy in LiDAR odometry without environment-specific tuning, achieving strong cross-dataset performance and generalization across sensors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scan-to-map LiDAR odometry drifts unboundedly in geometrically degenerate environments (tunnels, corridors) and existing degeneracy handling requires environment-specific tuning.

Method: Identify that GN Hessian masking occurs due to covariance regularization in voxelized GICP. Propose a regularization-free voxel-normal localizability field with two statistics: f0 (directional anisotropy) and λ0 (per-voxel mass) to distinguish information absence from dilution. Use a temporal-median gate to fuse into Fisher-information weighted correspondences. Calibrate on two short sequences and freeze. Validate across four sensor types and multiple datasets.

Result: LF-GICP achieves KITTI relative translation error of 0.865% under the identical protocol, outperforms baselines on GEODE tunnels and MulRan, leads the HeLiPR mean, and generalizes across four sensors without retuning. Demonstrates that straight, uniform tunnels remain unobservable along their axis for LiDAR-only registration.

Conclusion: A parameter-free, degeneracy-robust LiDAR odometry approach that avoids environment-specific tuning, provides consistent cross-dataset performance, and reveals fundamental observability limits in straight tunnels with LiDAR-only registration.

Abstract: Scan-to-map LiDAR odometry drifts unboundedly along the unobservable axes of geometrically degenerate environments like tunnels and corridors, and existing degeneracy handling requires environment-specific parameter tuning. This paper presents a parameter-free approach. We show that in voxelized GICP the Gauss--Newton (GN) Hessian masks translational degeneracy, because covariance regularization keeps the translation block artificially well-conditioned. We bypass this with a regularization-free voxel-normal localizability field and two of its statistics: a normalized fraction $f_0$ detecting directional anisotropy, and an absolute per-voxel mass $λ_0$ distinguishing information absence (tunnels) from dilution (dense open scenes). A temporal-median gate combines both to trigger Fisher-information correspondence weighting. Calibrated once by fixed rules on two short sequences and then frozen, LF-GICP achieves the lowest KITTI relative translation error ($0.865\%$) under an identical evaluation protocol against re-run baselines, outperforms them on GEODE tunnels and MulRan, leads the HeLiPR mean, and generalizes across four sensor types without re-tuning. We further demonstrate empirically that straight, uniform tunnels remain unobservable along their axis for LiDAR-only registration.

</details>


### [203] [Multimodal Trajectory Planning for Surface Vehicles using Turning Circle-based Control Barrier Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19537)
*Changyu Lee*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A guide-path-free multimodal trajectory planning framework for autonomous surface vehicles using MPC integrated with turning-circle-based CBF (TC-CBF). It accounts for nonholonomic constraints and finite turning, enabling distinct left/right avoidance modes and avoiding reliance on global guide paths; shows superior performance vs Euclidean-distance CBF and single-mode MPC in simulations with multiple moving vessels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address safety and planning limitations in dynamic maritime environments by incorporating nonholonomic constraints and topologically distinct avoidance maneuvers without requiring predefined guide paths, improving success rates and reducing safety violations.

Method: Combine model predictive control with a TC-CBF that uses turning-circle geometry to define feasible avoidance regions. Introduce left and right turning avoidance modes to enable topologically different trajectories within a single optimization. Directly embed avoidance direction into safety constraints to mitigate local minima and deadlocks. Evaluate in simulations with multiple moving vessels, comparing against ED-CBF and single-mode MPC.

Result: The proposed TC-CBF MPC framework achieves higher success rates, fewer safety violations, and smaller residual violations across varying traffic densities compared to single-mode baselines. It enables diverse, topologically distinct trajectories without guide paths, reducing deadlocks and improving computational efficiency.

Conclusion: Embedding turning-circle-based safety constraints into MPC enables topologically diverse, guide-path-free trajectory planning for ASVs in dynamic environments, improving safety and performance with computational efficiency and mitigating local minima issues in multimodal planning.

Abstract: This paper presents a guide path-free multimodal trajectory planning framework for autonomous surface vehicles operating in dynamic environments. The proposed method integrates model predictive control (MPC) with a turning circle-based control barrier function (TC-CBF). Unlike conventional Euclidean distance-based CBFs (ED-CBFs), which evaluate safety solely based on proximity, the TC-CBF accounts for the nonholonomic motion and finite turning capability of a surface vehicle. Its geometric formulation identifies feasible avoidance regions according to the vehicle's turning circles and generates distinct left- and right-turning avoidance modes. These modes allow the optimization solver to explore and select topologically different trajectories without relying on globally planned guide paths, as required by many conventional multimodal planning approaches. By embedding the avoidance direction directly into the safety constraint, the proposed framework alleviates the local-minimum and deadlock problems of single-mode MPC while maintaining computational efficiency. Extensive simulations involving multiple moving vessels demonstrate that the proposed method achieves higher success rates, fewer safety violations, and smaller residual violations than single-mode baselines across all tested traffic densities.

</details>


### [204] [HiTac-WAM: A Hierarchical Tactile World Action Model for Contact-Rich Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19574)
*Chao Xue,Chaofan Zhang,Wenxuan Ma,Guocai Yao,Shaowei Cui,Shuo Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HiTac-WAM introduces a hierarchical tactile world action model that forecasts multi-stage tactile states for candidate action chunks using a directed hierarchy and attention constraints, enabling improved planning and execution in manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tactile-aware methods often predict touch as images or latent streams without modeling the underlying hierarchical physical dependencies of tactile states, limiting planning and robustness.

Method: Forecasts are factorized into three hierarchical stages—contact state, 3D deformation field, and slip risk—organized with stop-gradient connections. A directed attention mask confines tactile queries to video-action context, while preventing cross-attention from video/action queries to tactile tokens. Planning uses tactile forecasts and task-progress estimates; execution keeps the forecast as a reference and triggers replanning when observed tactile states diverge.

Result: Quantitative gains include mean contact F1 of 0.921; a 17.6% reduction in 3D displacement L2 error compared with a deformation-only predictor; 60.4% improvement in slip AUPRC over a slip-only predictor. Real-robot testing across chip grasping, blackboard erasing, and USB insertion shows average success rising from 31.1% to 61.1% with hierarchical forecasts, and 72.2% with the full system.

Conclusion: Hierarchical tactile forecasting with directed dependencies and attention constraints substantially improves tactile state prediction and real-robot manipulation success, demonstrating the value of structured tactile representations for planning and execution.

Abstract: World action models jointly predict future visual observations and actions, whereas existing tactile-aware variants typically represent future touch as an image or latent stream without modeling the physical dependencies that organize tactile states hierarchically. We present HiTac-WAM, a hierarchical tactile world action model that forecasts a sequence of future tactile states for each candidate action chunk before execution. The forecast factorizes into contact state, a 3D deformation field, and slip risk, organized as a directed hierarchy in which each downstream stage is conditioned on stop-gradient signals from preceding stages. A directed attention mask allows tactile queries to attend to the video-action context of each candidate while preventing video and action queries from attending to tactile tokens. For planning, HiTac-WAM ranks candidate action chunks using tactile forecasts and task-progress estimates. For execution, the selected tactile forecast is retained as a reference; persistent discrepancies between predicted and observed tactile states trigger corrective replanning. HiTac-WAM achieves a mean contact F1 of 0.921; under matched training budgets, the directed hierarchy reduces 3D displacement L2 error by 17.6% relative to the deformation-only predictor and improves slip AUPRC by 60.4% relative to the slip-only predictor. Across chip grasping, blackboard erasing, and USB insertion, selection guided by the hierarchical forecasts increases the average real-robot success rate from 31.1% to 61.1%, while the full system attains 72.2%.

</details>


### [205] [OrthoSkillVLA: Continual Skill Learning via Gradient-Informed Skill Subspace Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19589)
*Jiaqi Wang,Zhou Fang,Qiongfeng Shi,Yi Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: OrthoSkillVLA is a parameter-efficient continual-learning framework for pretrained Vision-Language-Action models that applies separate subspace constraints to the VLM and ActionHead and uses a light feature-aware MoE decoder for the output layer to mitigate forgetting without replay.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models possess heterogeneous components: broad semantic VLM representations and localized velocity patterns in the ActionHead, which are each differently susceptible to interference during continual skill acquisition. Existing methods either forget past skills or incur large inference costs. A targeted approach is needed to preserve reusable semantics while protecting velocity mappings.

Method: Impose independent subspace constraints on the VLM and ActionHead to limit updates within unused directions, preserving semantic capacity and velocity fidelity. Introduce a lightweight, feature-aware Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) decoder at the output stage: each skill has a compact expert; a training-free router selects the appropriate expert based on feature-space affinity, avoiding re-training and replay.

Result: In simulated and real-world tests, with ablations, OrthoSkillVLA more effectively preserves prior skills while enabling acquisition of new ones, while keeping overhead low compared to unified constraints or full finetuning.

Conclusion: OrthoSkillVLA offers a principled, parameter-efficient solution for continual skill learning in pretrained VLA models, leveraging component-specific constraints and a modular MoE to maintain stability and plasticity without rehearsal.

Abstract: Pretrained Vision-Language-Action models provide a strong foundation for robot learning, but sequentially adapting them to diverse skills can perturb the representations and velocity mappings used by previous skills, leading to catastrophic forgetting. Architecture-based approaches improve retention by isolating skills but lead to increased inference footprint. Recent subspace-constrained methods restrict parameter updates in an orthogonal subspace to minimize interference but impose a unified constraint on the entire model. We analyze the distinct roles of internal VLA components and identify two VLA-specific challenges. First, the VLM maintains broad semantic representations, making it vulnerable to capacity exhaustion, whereas the ActionHead refines semantics into localized velocity patterns that are highly sensitive to perturbations. Second, the final velocity decoder serves as a readout layer. Freezing it forms an output-stage expressivity bottleneck, while updating it risks overwriting previous velocity mappings. To this end, we propose OrthoSkillVLA, a parameter-efficient framework for continual skill learning in pretrained VLA models without demonstration replay. Given the representation heterogeneity, we impose separate subspace constraints on the VLM and ActionHead, preserving reusable semantic capacity while protecting localized velocity patterns. For the output layer, we introduce a lightweight feature-aware MoE decoder, where each skill is allocated a compact expert and a training-free router selects the expert according to feature-space affinity. Extensive simulated and real-world evaluations, together with ablations, demonstrate that OrthoSkillVLA better preserves prior skills while acquiring new ones.

</details>


### [206] [The Verification Gap in Networked Physical AI: A Post-Semantic Communication Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19593)
*Shunsuke Saruwatari*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A Post-Semantic Communication Framework bridges the verification gap between proposal formation and physical execution in networked Physical AI, distinguishing evidence transfer from coordination and providing finite-state checks and episode-level reporting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the verification gap where proposal-level evidence or authority may be unavailable, hindering justified physical actions in networked Physical AI.

Method: Introduce Post-Semantic Communication Framework; define evidence requirements, evidence records, single-path validation of records, separation of evidence sufficiency from finalization, downstream runtime gate; separate evidence transfer from evidence coordination; finite-state checks; finalizer-dependent asymmetry in sender vs receiver Feedback; episode-level reporting schema.

Result: A theoretical framework detailing how evidence flows and finalization decisions occur, including an asymmetry where sender finalization expands evidence reach via transfer, while receiver finalization suppresses redundant payload via coordination; provides a common reporting schema for future studies.

Conclusion: The framework targets the verification gap in physical AI systems, clarifying roles of evidence transmission and coordination, and enabling standardized measurement in future experiments.

Abstract: A task-effective proposal is not yet a justified physical action. In networked Physical AI, a proposal may be understood while valid, timely, proposal-bound evidence or the authority required to finalize an action remains unavailable. We call this mismatch the verification gap and introduce a Post-Semantic Communication Framework for the systems interface between proposal formation and physical execution. The framework begins with application-declared evidence requirements, represents qualifying observations as evidence records, validates supporting and conflicting records through one path, and separates evidence sufficiency from authorized finalization and a downstream runtime gate. It further distinguishes evidence transfer, which can enlarge the record set reachable by a finalizer, from evidence coordination, which can suppress transmission around records already held at the finalization endpoint. Finite-state framework checks verify that the evaluator implements the declared distinctions consistently. Under the declared model, the controlled communication study exposes a finalizer-dependent asymmetry: sender-finalized Feedback uses evidence transfer to expand evidence reachability throughout the feasible plotted region, whereas receiver-finalized Feedback uses coordination to suppress redundant payload until loss, latency, freshness, and deadline costs shift selection to One-way. Finally, an episode-level reporting schema defines common denominators for future measured Physical-AI studies.

</details>


### [207] [What Matters for Latent Actions in Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19613)
*Xizhou Bu,Qingda Hu,Lei Zhou,Lingfeng Zhang,Yingbo Tang,Zihao Liu,Xinyi Tao,Zhiqiang Ma,Qingqiu Huang,Chufeng Tang,Hongbo Wang,Jing Zhang,Jiayi Ma,Hangjun Ye,Wei Li,Xiaoshuai Hao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Comprehensive empirical study of Latent Action Models (LAMs) for robotic manipulation, unifying methods in an autoencoder framework, evaluating 41 design choices across three axes, and showing that fine-tuning vision-language model backbones with latent actions yields better downstream policy learning, validated on three benchmarks and real robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LAM research is fragmented with inconsistent evaluations, making it hard to identify what factors truly impact downstream manipulation performance; a systematic, cross-method analysis is needed.

Method: Embed representative LAMs into a common autoencoding framework; systematically enumerate 41 design choices across latent action modeling paradigms, learning objectives and regularization, and latent action integration strategies; evaluate four proxy metrics for latent-action quality; perform extensive experiments on three benchmarks; investigate fine-tuning vision-language model backbones with latent actions; validate on real-world robotic tasks.

Result: The study finds that fine-tuning vision-language model backbones with latent actions provides a stronger initialization for downstream policy learning, with consistent gains observed across three benchmarks and real-world robot manipulation tasks; also identifies and assesses multiple design choices and proxy metrics to guide LAM development.

Conclusion: The paper delivers empirical guidance for LAM design, highlighting the benefit of latent-action-informed initialization via VLM fine-tuning and the value of standardized, multi-metric evaluation to predict downstream performance; results support broader use of LAMs in robotic manipulation and pave the way for principled design.

Abstract: Latent Action Models (LAMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for enabling robot learning to leverage large-scale unlabeled videos through latent actions that serve as compact surrogates for physical actions. Despite rapid progress, research on LAM remains highly fragmented, with existing methods evaluating different design choices in isolation under inconsistent experimental settings, making it difficult to identify the factors that truly determine downstream robotic manipulation performance. In this work, we present the first comprehensive empirical study of latent action learning for robotic manipulation. We unify representative LAM methods within a common autoencoding framework and systematically investigate 41 LAM design choices across three dimensions, including latent action modeling paradigms, learning objectives and regularization methods, and latent action integration strategies. We further examine four proxy metrics for evaluating latent action quality and assess their ability to reliably predict downstream robotic manipulation performance. Extensive experiments on three widely used benchmarks provide strong empirical evidence that fine-tuning vision-language model (VLM) backbones with latent actions provides a stronger initialization for downstream policy learning, with further validation on real-world robot manipulation tasks.

</details>


### [208] [Magnetically Self-Sealed MR Haptic Actuator With PWM-Based Excitation and High-Fidelity Torque Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19635)
*Dong Qiang,Tian Yuan,Song Yang,Kequan Xia,Thomas Reddyhoff,Yikun Zhang,Cheng Cheng,Min Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Integrated MRF haptic system with a self-sealed rotary actuator, high-frequency PWM, and model-based torque rendering achieving low hysteresis, accurate tracking, and long-duration stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable reliable, compact, and safe haptic actuation using magnetorheological fluids by addressing sealing, hysteresis, torque fidelity, and long-term operation.

Method: Magnetostatic optimization to concentrate flux in torque and sealing regions; compact self-sealed rotary actuator; high-frequency PWM (up to 10 kHz); nonlinear torque model dependent on torque change direction/speed; real-time controller with feedforward, hysteresis compensation, PI feedback, and sliding-mode correction.

Result: Torque output up to 600 N·mm/A; higher PWM frequency reduces hysteresis and improves repeatability; at 10 kHz, a direction/speed-dependent nonlinear model; controller reduces overshoot, undershoot, and RMSE by 77.4%, 61.9%, and 68.3% relative to PID; tracks sinusoidal and biomechanics-based references; 1.5 h test shows only 2.5°C coil rise with no tracking loss.

Conclusion: The integrated approach delivers high-fidelity, stable torque rendering suitable for safe, intuitive human–robot interaction, with significant improvements over traditional PID control and promising long-duration performance.

Abstract: Accurate and stable torque rendering is essential for safe and perceptive human--machine interaction. Magnetorheological fluid (MRF)-based actuators offer a compact and rapidly controllable solution for haptic feedback, but their practical implementation requires reliable fluid sealing, low-hysteresis excitation, accurate torque control, and stable long-duration operation. This article presents an integrated MRF haptic system featuring a compact magnetically self-sealed rotary actuator, low-hysteresis PWM operation, high-fidelity model-based torque rendering, and stable performance during long-time operation. Magnetostatic simulation guides the arrangement of magnetic and nonmagnetic materials to focus flux in the multidisk torque and permanent-magnet sealing regions, enabling a maximum 600 N$\cdot$mm/A output. Experiments show that higher PWM frequencies reduce hysteresis and improve repeatability. At 10 kHz, the response is represented by a nonlinear model that varies with the direction and speed of torque change. The real-time controller combines feedforward, hysteresis compensation, PI feedback, and sliding-mode correction. Compared with PID, it reduces square-wave overshoot, undershoot, and steady-state RMSE by 77.4\%, 61.9\%, and 68.3\%, respectively. It tracks sinusoidal and biomechanics-model-based references, and a 1.5-h test shows only a 2.5 $^\circ$C rise near the coil with no clear tracking loss. This high-fidelity torque rendering will fundamentally transform human--robot collaboration by making interactions safer, more efficient, and more intuitive.

</details>


### [209] [World-Model-Grounded LLM Planning for AUV and ASV Navigation Near Offshore Wind Farms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19661)
*Markus Buchholz,Ignacio Carlucho,Yvan R. Petillot*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A world-model-augmented LLM planner for marine robotics with MPC-style replanning and a physics-grounded neural world model improves navigation safety and performance, achieving strong sim-to-real transfer and, for ASVs, high navigability via VLM-based perception.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs lack physical intuition about duration and safe maneuvers; integrating a physics-grounded world model enables accurate feasibility assessments and safer, more efficient execution in dynamic offshore environments.

Method: Three components: (1) a physics-grounded neural world model; (2) a three-phase gradient-based trajectory optimizer; (3) an MPC-style closed-loop replanner with a trust-region guard. The LLM selects objectives; the world model determines execution details (e.g., DOF and duration). Evaluated on two marine platforms: a 6-DOF AUV and a 3-DOF ASV, with sim-to-real transfer to GazeboSim under currents, waves, and thruster dynamics. For ASV, a VLM-assisted semantic-mapping pipeline uses satellite imagery, nautical charts, and forecast APIs as an obstacle/context source instead of onboard sensors.

Result: Five benchmark missions per platform: both reach goals with zero predicted collisions; successful GazeboSim transfer under environmental dynamics. Performance improved: GazeboSim goal-distance error reduced by 70–82% for ASV and ~93% for AUV compared to ungrounded baseline. Residual fine-tuning reduces surrogate rollout RMSE by 60% (AUV) and 69% (ASV). ASV with VLM mapping achieves 96% navigability accuracy as a drop-in replacement for hand-specified obstacle geometry.

Conclusion: Integrating physics-grounded world models with LLM-based planning yields safer, more reliable offshore navigation, with strong sim-to-real transfer. For ASVs, VLM-based perception can substantially substitute onboard sensors for obstacle geometry, achieving high navigability.

Abstract: Large language models can turn a natural-language mission into a sequence of robot actions, but they do not have a sense of physics: they cannot judge how long a command should run, or whether it will make the robot drift into an obstacle. We proposed the use of a world model to expand the capabilities of Large Language model-based planners. Our method has three components: a physics-grounded neural world model, a three-phase gradient-based trajectory optimizer, and a Model Predictive Controller (MPC)-style closed-loop replanner with a trust-region guard. The language model decides what to do, and the world model decides how long, whether that means driving eight thrusters through 6 DOF or two differential thrusters through 3 DOF. We evaluate two marine vehicle classes operating near offshore wind infrastructure: a 6-DOF Autonomous Underwater Vehicle (AUV) and a 3-DOF differential-drive Autonomous Surface Vehicle (ASV). In five benchmark missions per platform, both vehicles reach every goal with zero predicted collisions, and both transfer to GazeboSim under ocean current, waves, and thruster dynamics, remaining collision-free and cutting GazeboSim goal-distance error versus the ungrounded baseline by 70-82% (ASV) and roughly 93% (AUV), after a residual fine-tuning pass that separately reduces surrogate rollout Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) by 60% (AUV) and 69% (ASV). For the ASV we further demonstrate a Vision language model (VLM)-assisted semantic-mapping pipeline that extracts obstacles and environmental context from satellite imagery, nautical charts, and forecast Application Programming Interface (API) instead of onboard sensors, reaching 96% navigability accuracy as a drop-in replacement for hand-specified obstacle geometry.

</details>


### [210] [SAGE: Ergodic Control for Autonomous and Adaptive Inspection of Subsea Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19671)
*Markus Buchholz,Ignacio Carlucho,Yvan R. Petillot*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Ergodic-control-driven adaptive inspection reallocates AUV time to mirror live risk, outperforming fixed tours in revisit frequency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Valves in subsea Xmas trees have varying leak risk; need dynamic, risk-aware inspection that updates with new findings without operator re-planning.

Method: Develop SAGE ergodic-control architecture; compute live risk distribution from sensor data; steer vehicle to match the distribution; compare with fixed-loop A* in a 2-XT, 5-valve setup; derive the control law and simulate.

Result: Dominant-risk valve revisited ~5.8 s under SAGE vs 8.1 s in A*; fixed tours may miss leaks longer; adapting distribution on the fly allows reallocation without explicit re-planning; simulation results demonstrate efficacy.

Conclusion: Ergodic control enables responsive, risk-aware inspection that matches time budgets while increasing checks on high-risk components; further work needed for scaling, real-world validation, and uncertainty handling.

Abstract: Subsea Christmas Trees (XTs) are underwater structures that use valves for directing oil flow, needing constant inspection. But not every valve carries the same risk at the same time: a valve with a suspected leak needs to be revisited far more often than one with a clean history, and that risk picture changes during the mission as new leaks are found. To handle this, we present SAGE (Semantic and Adaptive Generative Ergodicity), an ergodic-control architecture that allocates vehicle time in proportion to a live, sensor-derived risk distribution rather than a scripted route. We study a two-XT scenario, with five valves in total, and compare a fixed-loop A* tour against SAGE. Both methods can be tuned to spend similar total time near a high-risk valve, but only ergodic control also checks it more often: in simulation, a dominant-risk valve was revisited every 5.8 s under ergodic control against a fixed 8.1 s for every valve under A*, regardless of risk, so a leak can go unnoticed for barely two-thirds as long. Because the tracked distribution is recomputed rather than planned once, a newly detected leak shifts vehicle behavior on the next control cycle with no explicit re-planning step and no operator in the loop, which a fixed tour cannot do without a discrete re-route. We derive the ergodic control law behind this behavior and report simulation results on the five-valve scenario.

</details>


### [211] [Keeping the Franka Emika Panda alive: a ROS 2 stack with a reliable position interface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19740)
*Antonio Langella,Davide Risi,Vincenzo Petrone,Enrico Ferrentino,Pasquale Chiacchio*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Open-source ROS 2 stack restores Panda support and fixes unreliable external position control by decoupling RT control, rate-matching, and position-domain refs; validated on two Panda arms across planning, manipulation, and teleoperation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Restore a modern, reliable, and open ROS 2 ecosystem for Franka Emika Panda by addressing unstable external position control caused by timing and sampling jitter rather than robot hardware, enabling safer and reproducible human-robot collaboration.

Method: Introduce an asynchronous hardware interface to decouple real-time communication from the ROS 2 control loop, implement rate-matching for slower command sources, and develop a position-domain reference generation strategy for smooth, reliable position commands; validate experimentally across planning, compliance control, position-controlled manipulation, and haptic teleoperation on two Panda platforms.

Result: The architecture reliably tracks velocity references, reduces motion artifacts from the official implementation, and demonstrates robust performance across multiple tasks on two Panda arms; the stack restores a modern ROS 2 workflow for Panda hardware.

Conclusion: The work provides an open, reliable ROS 2 ecosystem for Panda that enables safe, responsive, and reproducible human-robot collaboration, lowering barriers to integrated planning, perception, interaction, and shared autonomy; code and videos are available online.

Abstract: This paper presents an open-source software stack that restores ROS 2 support for the Franka Emika Panda robot while resolving the long-standing unreliability of its external position control interface. We first analyze the root causes of unstable position control and show that the observed vibrations and protective stops arise from the timing of the external control loop and sampling jitter, rather than from limitations of the robot itself. Building on this analysis, we introduce an asynchronous hardware interface that decouples real-time communication from the ROS 2 control loop, a rate-matching mechanism for slower command sources, and a position-domain reference generation strategy that produces reliable, smooth position commands. Experimental validation shows that the proposed architecture reliably tracks velocity references by reducing motion artifacts introduced by the official implementation, and the stack is validated across motion planning, compliance control, position-controlled manipulation, and haptic teleoperation on two independent Panda platforms. By restoring a modern, reliable, and open ROS 2 ecosystem for the Panda, this work lowers the barrier to developing safe, responsive, and reproducible human-robot collaboration applications that integrate planning, perception, interaction, and shared autonomy. Code and videos are available on our website at https://sites.google.com/view/fer-ros2/.

</details>


### [212] [GOAG: Generative and Object-Agnostic Grasp Planner for Dexterous Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19759)
*Julien Merand,Boris Meden,Mathieu Grossard,Liming Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GOAG (Generative and Object-Agnostic Grasp Planner) learns a gripper-specific latent space of contact-surface distributions to sample valid grasps without object-specific training, enabling cross-object generalization and fast grasp generation; achieves strong results (86.93% on MultiDex) and is validated in simulation and real-world with multiple grippers; code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep-learning grasp planners often overfit to object-specific datasets, hindering generalization to novel objects. There is a need for object-agnostic approaches that exploit intrinsic gripper–object contact geometry to enable transferable grasp planning.

Method: A deep generative model learns a compact latent representation of the gripper's contact-surface distribution. At inference, object features are incorporated to retrieve admissible contact areas compatible with the gripper. The approach is evaluated across different grippers, under simulated and real-world scenarios, and does not require object-specific training data.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on MultiDex objects with an average success rate of 86.93%. Demonstrates faster generation of multiple grasps and matches leading methods trained on the dataset, while being object-agnostic.

Conclusion: GOAG provides a scalable, object-agnostic approach to dexterous grasping that mitigates generalization issues inherent in dataset-specific learning, with practical advantages in speed and applicability across grippers; code and videos are available on the project site.

Abstract: Multifingered grasping is a crucial robotic skill, but current deep-learning grasp planners often struggle to generalize to new objects because they are trained on limited, object-specific datasets. We introduce a fundamentally different approach, grounded in the observation that the gripper and the object share identical surface geometry at their mutual contact points. We propose GOAG: Generative and Object-Agnostic Grasp Planner for Dexterous Robotic Manipulation, a novel deep generative model that learns a compact latent representation of a specific gripper's contact surface distribution, enabling the efficient sampling of valid grasp configurations without relying on object-specific training data. We show that by introducing object features only at inference time, our model can effectively retrieve admissible contact areas that are compatible with the gripper's capabilities. We validate our approach through extensive experiments on established grasp protocols in both simulated and real-world scenarios, demonstrating its effectiveness with different grippers from the literature. Our method delivers state-of-the-art results on the objects from the MultiDex dataset, achieving an average success rate of 86.93%. It offers significantly faster processing when generating numerous grasps, while matching the performance of leading approaches specifically trained on this dataset. Unlike these methods, our approach does not rely on object-specific training data, highlighting the advantages of object-agnostic learning. It effectively addresses the generalization challenges faced by traditional data-driven grasp planners. Code and videos are available on our project website https://cea-list.github.io/goagweb/ .

</details>


### [213] [CoToGrasp: Contact-Topology-Conditioned Dexterous Grasp Synthesis via Canonical Workspace Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19776)
*Julien Merand,Boris Meden,Liming Chen,Mathieu Grossard*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CoToGrasp is an object-agnostic, topology-conditioned generative grasping framework that synthesizes diverse, stable grasps, achieving state-of-the-art results on DexGraspNet and validating on hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional dexterous grasp planners optimize stability but ignore downstream functional intent; taxonomy-guided grasps require expensive object-annotated data. A data-efficient, topology-conditioned, object-agnostic approach is needed for generalizable grasp synthesis.

Method: CoToGrasp introduces a generative model conditioned on specific contact topologies. It uses a feature-based canonical workspace to map local object features into a gripper-centric domain, decoupling semantics from geometry. The model learns the gripper's intrinsic contact manifold, enabling zero-shot generalization to unseen objects and training entirely in an object-agnostic manner. Evaluations are conducted on DexGraspNet, with hardware validation.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming taxonomy-guided planners on DexGraspNet. Demonstrates physical viability and kinematic feasibility on a real robot. Code is released on the project website.

Conclusion: By learning the gripper’s intrinsic contact manifold in a canonical workspace and conditioning grasps on contact topologies, CoToGrasp enables diverse, task-relevant grasps without object annotations and generalizes zero-shot to unseen objects, bridging taxonomy-guided planning with data-efficient, scalable synthesis.

Abstract: Current dexterous grasp planners primarily optimize for physical stability, focusing on whether an object can be grasped rather than how it should be grasped to support downstream functional tasks. However, conditioning grasp synthesis on specific human grasp taxonomies typically requires prohibitively expensive, object-annotated datasets. To address these limitations, we propose CoToGrasp, a novel generative framework that synthesizes diverse, stable grasps strictly conditioned on specific contact topologies. To bypass the data collection bottleneck, CoToGrasp is trained entirely in an object-agnostic manner. We introduce a feature-based canonical workspace that projects local object features into a unified gripper-centric domain, effectively decoupling the semantic functional intent from the arbitrary object geometry. By learning the intrinsic contact manifold of the gripper within this workspace, our model achieves zero-shot generalization to unseen objects at inference. Extensive evaluations on the large-scale DexGraspNet dataset demonstrate that CoToGrasp achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming existing taxonomy-guided planners. Finally, we demonstrate the physical viability and kinematic feasibility of our synthesized contact topologies on a physical robot platform. Code is available on our project website https://cea-list.github.io/cotograspweb/ .

</details>


### [214] [MILD: Tractable Terrain Modeling for Learning Improved Bipedal Locomotion on Deformable Surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19955)
*Zeren Luo,Jiahui Zhang,Zhe Xu,Wanyue Li,Xinqi Li,Xuechao Chen,Zhangguo Yu,Annan Tang,Peng Lu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A physics-grounded DE solver (MILD) for spatially varying foot-terrain interactions combined with a terrain-aware RL locomotion controller, enabling robust bipedal walking on deformable yielding terrain and online terrain identification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current simulators fail to capture spatiotemporal heterogeneity of yielding substrates, hindering robust locomotion on deformable surfaces.

Method: Introduce MILD with a discrete-element contact solver that models spatially varying foot-terrain interactions; train a terrain-aware locomotion controller via deep RL with latent modulation and proprioceptive estimation; compare to state-of-the-art and validate on hardware.

Result: Produced more diverse and realistic contact scenarios during training; controllers adapt more naturally to deformable surfaces; hardware experiments show online terrain identification and adaptation across varying surface stiffness.

Conclusion: Integrating physics-grounded terrain simulation with RL-based locomotion yields improved adaptation and robustness for bipedal robots on yielding terrains, with potential for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Enabling robots to walk on yielding terrain is vital for applications ranging from disaster response to planetary exploration. While bipedal robots hold immense potential, their locomotion on deformable surfaces remains limited as current simulators fail to capture the spatiotemporal heterogeneity of such yielding substrates. We present MILD, featuring a physics-grounded discrete-element contact solver that accurately simulates spatially varying foot-terrain interactions. Complementing this model, we train a terrain-aware locomotion controller via deep reinforcement learning with latent modulation and proprioceptive estimation. Quantitative comparisons against state-of-the-art methods show our approach generates more diverse and realistic contact scenarios during training, resulting in controllers that exhibit natural adaptation on real deformable surfaces. Through hardware experiments, we demonstrate the system's capability for online terrain identification and adaptation across a wide range of surface stiffness.

</details>


### [215] [PVRA: A Pointwise Key-point Voting Framework for Robotic Assembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19968)
*Kulunu Samarawickrama,Roel Pieters*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 3D keypoint-based modular framework to learn assembly dependencies from RGB-D for autonomous progressive assembly; evaluated on an assembly pose estimation dataset and compared to object-centric baselines with augmented metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond object-centric perception and learn assembly dependencies that enable predicting actionable outputs for progressive robotic assembly manipulation.

Method: A 3D keypoint-based modular learning framework that takes RGB-D input of an assembly scene to infer assembly dependencies and output actionable manipulation signals.

Result: Empirical evaluation on an assembly pose estimation dataset with comparisons to object-centric baselines and augmented metrics tailored for progressive assemblies.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables learning assembly dependencies to predict actionable outputs for progressive assembly, showing competitive performance and improved evaluation for progressive assembly tasks.

Abstract: Modern computer vision has enabled partial autonomy in robotic assembly manipulation. However, performing autonomous manipulation of a progressive assembly demands a more specific set of skills, in addition to perceiving the objects. Through a comparative analysis of research in the associated domains, we deduce that object-centric perception must advance towards learning assembly dependencies to predict meaningful actionable outputs for autonomous assembly manipulation. Subsequently, we present a 3D keypoint-based modular learning framework to learn assembly dependencies to infer actionable outputs given a RGB-D input of an assembly scene. We train and evaluate our trained network on an assembly pose estimation dataset and compare it against object-centric baselines with an augmented set of metrics for progressive assemblies.

</details>


### [216] [Learning Highly Dynamic Skills Transition for Quadruped Jumping Through Constrained Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19977)
*Zeren Luo,Jiahui Zhang,Yimin Han,Ji Ma,Minghao Lu,Ioannis Havoutis,Peng Lu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Hierarchical RL enables agile gate traversal for quadrupeds by combining imitation-learned low-level skills with a vision-informed high-level planner.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable explosive, dynamic locomotion of legged robots through constrained spaces and to emulate biological agility (e.g., aerial gate traversal) in ground robots.

Method: Train low-level policy via imitation learning to capture diverse skills; a high-level controller uses knowledge of low-level capabilities and visual gate detection to select maneuvers and generate collision-free, dynamic trajectories.

Result: Demonstrates autonomous agile gate traversal on ground-walking robots, with a scalable framework that can extend to other highly dynamic tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed hierarchical imitation-learning RL pipeline bridges low-level skill diversity with high-level planning, advancing lifelike agility in legged robots and generalizing to additional dynamic tasks.

Abstract: Although legged animals are capable of performing explosive motions while traversing confined spaces, replicating this behavior in quadrupedal robots has been a longstanding challenge. Here, we propose a hierarchical reinforcement learning pipeline that empowers the robots to perform aggressive locomotion through constrained obstacles--a narrow gate. The imitation learning technique is used to train the low-level policy, which mimics the behaviors of real animals and forms a set of diverse skills. The high-level controller, having an awareness of the capability of low-level skills and acquiring the gate information via vision-based detection, determines the suitable maneuvers with collision-free trajectories to traverse it dynamically. Notably, we also verify that this framework can be extended to other highly dynamic tasks. This is one of the first works that perform autonomous and agile aerial gate traversal tasks on ground-walking robots, extending the lifelike agility of legged robots to match that of their biological counterparts.

</details>


### [217] [Evidence-Gated Task and Motion Planning with Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20084)
*Tsunehiko Tanaka,Matthew Stephenson,Alistair Macvicar,Edgar Simo-Serra*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EAFG combines VLM-driven exploratory subgoals with TAMP under a feasibility gate to handle partial observability in long-horizon manipulation, by acquiring visual evidence before planning and halting when objects are absent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Under partial observability, VLMs provide priors that may rely on unavailable goal-relevant objects, risking failed or unintended execution in long-horizon tasks.

Method: Evidence Acquisition and Feasibility Gating (EAFG): iteratively generate exploratory subgoals via a Vision-Language Model, acquire supporting visual evidence through exploration, execute with Task and Motion Planning, and apply a feasibility gate to decide to continue planning, obtain more evidence, or halt.

Result: In cooking tasks with ambiguous object use, EAFG improves recipe completion by discovering relevant objects prior to planning and makes appropriate halt decisions when an object is absent, reducing repeated manipulation attempts.

Conclusion: EAFG mitigates reliance on VLM priors under partial observability, increasing reliability of long-horizon manipulation by integrating evidence gathering with feasibility-based planning.

Abstract: Robots executing long-horizon manipulation tasks from natural-language instructions must reason about both semantic task structure and geometric feasibility. However, under partial observability, the availability of goal-relevant objects may be uncertain. In such cases, approaches that combine Vision-Language Models (VLMs) with Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) may generate subgoals that rely on the VLM's prior knowledge without observational support, leading to execution failures or unintended outcomes. We propose Evidence Acquisition and Feasibility Gating (EAFG), a framework that acquires visual evidence through VLM-generated exploratory subgoals and TAMP-based execution. EAFG then applies a feasibility gate to decide whether to proceed with task planning, acquire further evidence, or halt. Our experiments show that, in cooking tasks with ambiguous object use, EAFG improves recipe completion by discovering task-relevant objects before planning. For instructions requiring an absent object, EAFG promotes appropriate halt decisions and reduces repeated attempts to manipulate that object.

</details>


### [218] [Towards Professional Tennis Styles for Humanoid Robots with Adaptive Motion Planning and Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20087)
*Tao Huang,Ruofei Liu,Xuchen Tang,Xinyin Zhang,Junli Ren,Huayi Wang,Feiyu Jia,Yukai Qi,Kangning Yin,Weishuai Zeng,Lipeng Chen,Xi Li,Ting Wu,Kailin Li,Ruoli Dai,Jingbo Wang,Lei Han,Jiangmiao Pang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AdaPT is a hierarchical Adaptive Motion Planning and Tracking framework that learns professional tennis serving and rally styles from broadcast videos and bridges the sim-to-real gap with a learned motion-speed adapter and randomized-speed tracking, validated on Unitree G1 and full-size humanoids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve professional, stylistic motion in humanoid ball sports without sacrificing task performance, and to close the sim-to-real gap exacerbated by autoregressive planning and perception noise.

Method: A two-tier system: a planner that derives stylistic, kinematic motions from broadcast videos, and a tracker that executes these motions with minimal interference. The adaptation mechanism trains the tracker to handle randomized execution speeds and conditions the planner on a learned motion-speed adapter to mitigate compounding errors. Real-world tests include Unitree G1 experiments and full-size Dobot Atom deployments, plus in-the-wild serving without motion capture.

Result: The adaptation improves tracking robustness and narrows the sim-to-real gap, enabling successful real-world serving on both small and full-size humanoids without motion capture, with demonstrated deployment and public code/videos.

Conclusion: AdaPT shows that decoupling planning (style-driven) from robust execution (speed-adaptive tracking) and grounding both in video-based learning yields transferable, high-quality humanoid ball-sport behavior and offers actionable insights for future systems.

Abstract: Humanoid robots have recently demonstrated promising capabilities in real-world ball sports. However, achieving professional motion styles while maintaining strong task performance remains challenging. In this work, we propose AdaPT, an Adaptive Motion Planning and Tracking framework that learns professional tennis serving and rally styles directly from broadcast videos. This hierarchical design is motivated by the key insight that the planner generates stylistic kinematic motions, while the tracker executes them with minimal interference with planning. Despite its effectiveness in simulation, a substantial sim-to-real gap emerges: tracking performance inevitably degrades on real robots, and this degradation is partially overlooked by autoregressive planning and further compounded by noisy perception. To address these issues, our adaptation mechanism improves tracking robustness by learning to track randomized execution speeds, while conditioning the planner on a learned motion-speed adapter to mitigate compounding errors. Real-world experiments on the Unitree G1 demonstrate the effectiveness of our adaptation mechanism in bridging the sim-to-real gap. We further deploy AdaPT policies on the full-size Dobot Atom humanoid robot (1.7m) and demonstrate in-the-wild serving without motion capture. Beyond these results, our real-world experiments reveal both algorithmic and engineering insights for future humanoid ball-sports systems. Videos and code are available on our \href{https://humanoidtennis.github.io/AdaPT/}{project website}.

</details>


### [219] [Planning-Oriented End-to-End Autonomous Driving: Architectures, Evaluation, and Emerging Paradigms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20111)
*Yanchen Guan,Xingcheng Liu,Bin Rao,Chengyue Wang,Guofa Li,Yunjian Li,Lishengsa Yue,Zhiyong Cui,Chengzhong Xu,Zhenning Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A comprehensive survey of end-to-end autonomous driving, tracing the shift toward planning-oriented systems with structured representations and trajectory-level outputs, and analyzing evaluation benchmarks and remaining open challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why modern end-to-end driving emphasizes learned, supervised representations that support safe, feasible, and route-compliant planning, and how benchmarking shapes progress.

Method: Systematic synthesis of the literature across categories (behavior cloning, conditional imitation learning, privileged distillation, BEV and vectorized planning, unified perception-prediction-planning architectures, world-model-based planners, and vision-language-action systems). Organizes methods along input representation, planning output, supervision signal, and evaluation protocol; analyzes the benchmark transition from open-loop trajectory matching to closed-loop simulation, real-log evaluation, long-tail testing, and human-preference metrics.

Result: Key findings: (1) The decisive factor is whether intermediate representations are learned, supervised, and evaluated to support safe planning, not merely if they exist. (2) Progress is hard to interpret without benchmark-consistent evaluation. (3) Displacement-based open-loop metrics provide limited evidence for safety/human alignment. (4) Benchmarking is shifting toward closed-loop simulation, real-log evaluations, long-tail tests, and human-preference metrics. (5) Notable open challenges include uncertainty-aware planning, learner–expert mismatch, runtime safety assurance, language–action grounding, world-model validation, and reproducible benchmarking.

Conclusion: Open challenges include uncertainty-aware planning, learner–expert mismatch, runtime safety assurance, language–action grounding, world-model validation, and reproducible benchmarking.

Abstract: End-to-end autonomous driving has evolved from camera-to-control regression toward planning-oriented systems that use structured representations, trajectory-level outputs, and increasingly realistic evaluation protocols. This survey reviews this transition across behavior cloning, conditional imitation learning, privileged distillation, BEV and vectorized planning, unified perception-prediction-planning architectures, world-model-based planners, and vision-language-action systems. We argue that the key distinction in modern end-to-end driving is not whether intermediate representations are used, but whether they are learned, supervised, and evaluated to support safe, feasible, and route-compliant planning. To organize the literature, we synthesize existing methods along four axes: input representation, planning output, supervision signal, and evaluation protocol. We further examine the benchmark shift from open-loop trajectory matching to closed-loop simulation, non-reactive real-log evaluation, long-tail testing, and human-preference-aware metrics. Our analysis highlights that architectural progress is difficult to interpret without benchmark-consistent evaluation, and that displacement-based open-loop metrics alone provide limited evidence for safe and human-aligned driving. We conclude with open challenges in uncertainty-aware planning, learner-expert mismatch, runtime safety assurance, language-action grounding, world-model validation, and reproducible benchmarking.

</details>


### [220] [Video2DoorTraversal: Push Door Traversal via Simulated Door Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20251)
*Xincheng Tang,Yiji Chen,Youhan Xie,Wanyu Li,Zhengjie Shu,Lai Jiang,Wenkang Hu,Yitong Li,Jinchuang Zhang,Xibin Song,Ruigang Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Video2DoorTraversal introduces a real-to-sim-to-real framework for wheel-legged mobile manipulators that reconstructs a single-door twin from one RGB video, synthesizes executable demonstrations via a sim-in-loop agent, and trains a joint base-arm-gripper policy (ArticuACT) with onboard inference; it achieves high real-world success and strong zero-shot generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon loco-manipulation tasks like door opening require precise perception-action coupling and coordinated base-arm control. A single RGB video to a realistic door model, followed by simulation-augmented policy learning and onboard execution, can improve reliability, speed, and transfer from simulation to real hardware.

Method: DoorTwin creates an instance-aligned articulated door twin from a single RGB video. A simulation-in-the-loop agent converts the recovered articulation into a parameterized skill program and iteratively refines failed rollouts to produce physically executable demonstrations. These demonstrations train ArticuACT, a dual-depth policy that outputs coordinated base, arm, and gripper commands using robot-centric camera conditioning and interaction-aware supervision. All perception and policy inference run onboard, enabling real-time operation.

Result: The approach achieves 96.57% average success over five real doors and 80.95% zero-shot success on structurally similar unseen doors, completing the full open-and-traverse sequence in about 13 seconds on average.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates a robust, end-to-end real-to-sim-to-real pipeline for long-horizon manipulation tasks, combining perceptual twin reconstruction, sim-driven demonstration synthesis, and onboard, interaction-aware control that generalizes to unseen but structurally related doors, with competitive speed.

Abstract: Door opening and traversal is a long-horizon loco-manipulation task that requires precise handle interaction and coordinated base-arm control. We present Video2DoorTraversal, a single-video real-to-sim-to-real framework for wheel-legged mobile manipulators. Given one RGB video of a real door, DoorTwin reconstructs an instance-aligned, articulated, and simulation-ready door twin with realistic geometry and appearance. A simulation-in-the-loop agent converts the recovered articulation into a parameterized skill program and iteratively refines failed rollouts to generate physically executable demonstrations. These demonstrations are used to train ArticuACT, a dual-depth policy that predicts coordinated base, arm, and gripper commands using robot-centric camera conditioning and interaction-aware supervision. With all perception and policy inference running onboard, the system achieves a 96.57% average success rate across five real doors and an 80.95% zero-shot success rate on structurally similar unseen doors, while completing the full approach, opening, and traversal sequence in approximately 13s on average. Project Page: https://video2doortraversal.github.io/.

</details>


### [221] [DART-S: Reachability-Audited Active-Suspension Preconditioning for Off-Road Vehicle Jumps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20275)
*Yu Hu,Fangzhou Zhao,Liang Chen,Chen Min,Wei Li,Mingyuan Sang,Jiajia Ma,Shican Chen,Di Pang,Baolei Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DART-S uses ramp-face suspension preconditioning and a local calibration map to shift the state-budget for takeoff, improving attitude outcomes and preserving drivetrain limits in BeamNG simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate takeoff errors arising from airborne torque reaction constrained by wheel angular momentum by proactively preconditioning pitch, pitch rate, and wheel spin.

Method: Precondition using ramp-face suspension; predict state-budget shifts with a local calibration map; select actions via a support-aware selector and validate with an interval-reachability screen; perform exact-pair audits; evaluate across 600 runs in 72 BeamNG sessions.

Result: DART-S achieves perfect performance at a boundary (24/24) where DART fails (0/24); at a lower speed, 23/24 vs 0/24; drivetrain limit hard-exceeded in all baseline but zero with DART-S due to a 200 rad/s guard; Holm-adjusted p-values indicate significance (p=0.0234, p=0.0156).

Conclusion: DART-S robustly reallocates authority through predictive, calibrated state shifts, yielding reliable boundary performance with formal audit and reproducible code availability.

Abstract: Airborne torque reaction cannot recover takeoff errors beyond the wheel angular-momentum budget. DART-S applies ramp-face suspension preconditioning to change pitch, pitch rate, and wheel spin before liftoff, thereby shifting the queried state and altering the remaining authority budget. To predict how each suspension action reshapes this state-budget pair, DART-S employs a local calibration map. A support-aware selector combines the predicted shift with local outcome evidence and an interval-reachability screen; an exact-pair audit reports residual authority. Across 600 new runs in 72 independent BeamNG sessions, every positive, negative, and boundary query follows its prespecified branch. At the confirmed 40°/13 m/s boundary, DART-S attains 24/24 post-touchdown attitude-criterion successes versus 0/24 for DART (session-level Holm-adjusted p=0.0234). At 11.5 m/s, a 0.35 s timing action attains 23/24 versus 0/24 for the static preset (p=0.0156). The 200 rad/s command guard keeps drivetrain hard-limit exceedance at zero across all 600 runs. The source code will be available at https://github.com/MeridianCAS/DART-S

</details>
